Mysterious Triality and M-Theory
Hisham Sati, Alexander A. Voronov

Abstract
In a previous paper [ SV21 ] , we introduced Mysterious Triality as an extension, via algebraic topology in the form of rational homotopy theory, of Mysterious Duality by Iqbal, Neitzke, and Vafa [ INV02 ] , which provides connections between physics, in the form of dimensional reduction of M-theory, and algebraic geometry, in the form of intersection theory on del Pezzo surfaces. The starting point for that connection to rational homotopy theory is the description of M-theory dynamics using the 4-sphere, via Hypothesis H. This progresses to dimensional reduction of M-theory on tori T k T^{k} with its dynamics described via cyclic loop spaces of the 4-sphere ℒ c k ​ S 4 \mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4} , producing a series of data analogous to that given by the del Pezzo surfaces 𝔹 k \mathbb{B}_{k} , for k = 0 , … , 8 k=0,\dots,8 . With the mathematical constructions established in [ SV21 ] , in this companion physics paper we present novel connections to M-theory that enhance the triality, including those strengthening the duality. This uncovers interesting ties between algebraic geometry, algebraic topology, and M-theory and provides tantalizing links. We further expand on the extension of the duality and triality to the Kac-Moody setting.

Introduction

Mysterious Duality has been discovered by Iqbal, Neitzke, and Vafa
[
INV02
]
as a remarkable, yet mysterious
correspondence between certain symmetry patterns in compactifications of M-theory
on tori
T^{k}
and del Pezzo surfaces
{\mathbb{B}}_{k}
, both governed by the root system corresponding to
the exceptional series
E_{k}
,
k\leq 8
.

Algebraic Geometry

\raisebox{-.9pt} {1}⃝

Physics

The moduli space of M-theory compactified on (reduced on, or simply on)
T^{k}
is
usually taken to be the double quotient
G(\mathbb{Z})\backslash G/K
, where
G
is the U-duality group, which is the (split real form of the) Lie group
E_{k}
,
G(\mathbb{Z})
is its integral form, and
K
is the maximal compact subgroup of
G

[
HT95
]
[
OP99
]
.
In
[
INV02
]
, for “flat tori with no C-field”, this was taken to be of the form

\mathcal{M}_{k}:=A/W,

where
A
is the maximal split torus of
G
and
W
is the Weyl group. This, as we explained in
[
SV21
]
, can be thought of as abelianization:

K\backslash G/G(\mathbb{Z})=(AN)/G(\mathbb{Z})\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.46605pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rm\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}abelianize}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 57.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{k}=A/W\;.

(1)

The duality highlights relations of del Pezzo geometry to
p
-branes in M-theory (M-branes) as well as to string theory upon dimensional
reductions and application of dualities (D-branes).
The main features include
[
INV02
]
:

(i)

Rational curves
\mathcal{C}
on
\mathbb{B}_{k}
with volume
\omega(\mathcal{C})
and degree
d_{\mathcal{C}}=(p+1)
correspond to
\tfrac{1}{2}
BPS
p
-brane states with tension
T_{p}=2\pi\exp\omega(\mathcal{C})
;

(ii)

Automorphisms of
\mathbb{B}_{k}
and
H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})

correspond to the U-duality transformations of M-theory on
T^{k}
.
The U-duality group of M-theory on
T^{k}
,
which for rectangular compactifications
with no
C
-field is given by the Weyl group
W
of
E_{k}
,
is mapped to a subgroup of the automorphism group of

H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})
which preserves the intersection form and canonical class
\mathcal{K}_{k}
;

(iii)

The moduli space
A/W
of compactified M-theory corresponds to the moduli space
H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{R})/W
of generalized Kähler forms
\omega
on
\mathbb{B}_{k}
;

(iv)

Two classes of rational curves
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
related as
\mathcal{C}_{1}+\mathcal{C}_{2}=-\mathcal{K}_{k}
on
\mathbb{B}_{k}

correspond to two
{\rm D}p_{1}
-branes
with
{\rm D}p_{2}
-branes with
p_{1}+p_{2}=7-k
, expressing electric-magnetic duality;

(v)

The
p
-branes of type IIA and type IIB string theory in 10 dimensions correspond to
classes of rational curves on
\mathbb{B}_{1}
and
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, respectively.
Further explicit realizations of these are given in
[
AHRS05
]
.

Studying the cohomology of the del Pezzo surfaces, the authors of

[
HLJP02
]
[
HLJP03
]
managed to extract the algebras found in

[
CJLP98b
]
as Borcherds algebras with a truncated
set of their positive roots corresponding to the generators of the
potentials.

Rational homotopy theory of the 4-sphere
S^{4}
captures the dynamics of
the fields in M-theory, as proposed in
[
Sa13
]
, and developed further under
the name
Hypothesis H
in
[
FSS17
]
[
FSS19b
]
[
FSS19c
]
[
GS21
]
(and applied in
[
Ro20
]
). Many consistency checks have been made, correctly yielding
various subtle effects of charge quantization (see
[
FSS19b
]
[
FSS19c
]
[
SS19a
]
[
SS19b
]
[
SS21
]
).

In the companion mathematical paper
[
SV21
]
, we provided an explicit description of the fields and their dynamics in M-theory compactified on tori, using the power of algebraic topology, through which many detailed aspects can be captured.
We explained there how
bringing in topology,
in the form of (rational) homotopy theory,
leads to a triality
extending the above Mysterious Duality picture
\raisebox{-.9pt} {1}⃝
:

:
Mysterious Triality
. The solid arrow

\raisebox{-.9pt} {2}⃝

stands for a relationship between physics and algebraic topology which is made explicit in
[

, while the dotted arrows

\raisebox{-.9pt} {1}⃝

and

\raisebox{-.9pt} {3}⃝

denote still mysterious correspondences
based on combinatorial coincidences. It is enough to solidify one of the dotted arrows to remove the mystery veil off the face of Triality.

While in
[
SV21
]
we focused on building the duality
\raisebox{-.9pt} {2}⃝
, our contributions here are of three types:

(i)

those that confirm Mysterious Duality
\raisebox{-.9pt} {3}⃝
;

(ii)

those that expand the duality to have further features;

(iii)

and those that extend the duality to the triality.

There are two main models for capturing rational homotopy via algebra, which are dual in some sense (Koszul duality)
[
Ta83
]
[
Maj00
]
[
FHT01
]
:

(I)

The
Sullivan model
:
Sullivan models
[
Su77
]
(see also
[
FHT01
]
[
FOT08
]
[
GM13
]
[
FH17
]
)
in rational homotopy theory (see
section

2.1
)
bring out structures that are visible in
supergravity
(see
[
FSS17
]
[
FSS18a
]
[
FSS18b
]

[
FSS19a
]
)
and which standard homotopy theory might otherwise obscure.
The Sullivan minimal model
M(S^{4})
describes the dynamics of the
form fields, expressed as differential forms on spacetime.
A gentle introduction to the relationship between
M-theory and rational homotopy theory
is given in
[
FSS19a
]
.

(II)

The
Quillen model:

Quillen models
[
Qu69
]
(see also
[
FHT01
]
[

]
[
BFMT20
]
) capture the algebra of gauge transformations, or alternatively that of the branes.
We showed in
[
SV21
]
that the algebra of gauge transformations for these fields is captured
by the Quillen model.
Remarkably, we found that the degree on del Pezzo matches the
degree arising from the Quillen model.

(3)

Coefficients.

Rational homotopy theory is about taking homotopy groups
\pi_{\bullet}

over the rational numbers
\mathbb{Q}
:
\pi^{\mathbb{Q}}_{\bullet}=\pi_{\bullet}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. The theory works equally well over the real numbers

\mathbb{R}
, and indeed, here as in
[
SV21
]
we consider rational homotopy theory over
\mathbb{R}

(as in
[
GM13
]
). This is the version in
which rational (more precisely, real) homotopy theory connects to differential geometry (e.g.
[
FOT08
]
),
since the smooth de Rham complex is not defined over
\mathbb{Q}
but over

\mathbb{R}
. This is favorable when dealing with differential forms
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\rm dR}

and physical fields
– see
[
FSS20
]
for an extensive discussion and applications.
Still, we do at times
consider rational and integral coefficients, for instance
when dealing with algebraic (Lie) groups.
We also get the root lattice, though that sits in a real vector
space, indirectly through combinatorial data. So, in this sense, we
connect to the
\mathbb{Z}
-forms of the U-duality groups
[
SV21
]
.

The cyclified loop space.

We found in
[
SV21
]
a striking matching between the equations
for the Sullivan minimal models of the iterated cyclic loop space (
cyclification
)
{\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}}
of the
four-sphere and the equations of motion (EOMs) of toroidal
compactifications of M-theory to
11-k
dimensions.
This is deeply rooted in the fact that the
process of compactification of supergravity on a circle
S^{1}
is akin to the process
of working out the Sullivan minimal model of the cyclic loop space.
This matching also implies the following general philosophy
[
SV21
]
(extending the
k=1
case in
[
FSS18a
]
[
FSS18b
]
):

Any feature of or statement about the Sullivan minimal model of an iterated cyclic
loop space
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
(or the real homotopy type thereof)
may be translated into a feature of or statement about the
compactification of M-theory on the
k
-torus.

Explicit examples of these features are the root system
E_{k}
, its Weyl group

W(E_{k})
and U-duality, the split real torus action and
trombone and rescaling symmetry, etc. (see
Sections

2.4
and
3.1
).
We view this as providing a strengthening of the Mysterious Duality, as
having an explicit relation was the essence of
the mystery behind the duality proposed by
[
INV02
]
.

The action of the integral form
G(\mathbb{Z})
on
G/K
(see (
1
)),
hence orbits of discrete U–duality groups, are studied in

[
CMS15
]

[
BDDFMR10
]
.
Note that the above are symmetries of the equations of motion and
not of the action,
1
1

1

These symmetries can be implemented at the level of the action, but this
involves a Legendre transformation of the fields.

hence it is appropriate that here and in
[
SV21
]
we consider these symmetries from
studying dynamics given by the EOMs and Bianchi identities arising from the corresponding Sullivan models.

New perspectives and further contributions.

In addition
to extending the duality

\raisebox{-.9pt} {1}⃝

to a triality involving rational homotopy theory via

\raisebox{-.9pt} {2}⃝

and

\raisebox{-.9pt} {3}⃝
in
Figure

1
,
here
we
provide the following perspectives:

(i)

Including the C-field universally via
S^{4}
: This brings in the
C
-field and its (Hodge) dual at the level of nontrivial field strengths,
modeled dynamically through
S^{4}
as a universal
nonlinear space
[
Sa13
]
. Previously these fields were introduced as algebraic coefficients at the level of potentials in
[
HLJP02
]
[
HLJP03
]
.

(ii)

Cohomological nontriviality
:
Closed differential forms on flat spacetime are exact, but the Sullivan and Quillen
models we consider, by construction, reflect
the nontrivial (co)homology of the universal space
S^{4}
and its cyclifications.
We correspondingly take the field strengths to
not necessarily be exact, extending what is traditional in the supergravity literature.

(iii)

Nonlinearity
: Instead of working with (co)homology which is intrinsically linear,
we bring in nonlinear spaces, with a rich structure encoding dualities and dynamics.
The setting in
[
INV02
]
is kinematic and linear, while we will see that ours is dynamic and
both linear (in regards to weights/roots and Weyl group actions) as well
as nonlinear (in terms of generators/field strengths and their equations of motion).

(iv)

Nontrivialilty of torus bundles
: Instead of having trivial torus bundles,
we have nontrivial
ones, each equipped with
an Euler class or a first Chern class that enters crucially into the dynamics leading to the uncovering of the
underlying homotopy structure.

(v)

Global dimensional reduction
:
We already provided in
[
SV21
]
a systematic global reduction on
T^{k}
of the
duality-symmetric theories.
We used cyclic loop spaces for reduction on a circle
as in
[
FSS18a
]
and for reductions on tori (as instances of more general Lie groups) as in
[
BMSS18
]
.
We found iterative reduction on circles
via “cyclification”
{\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}}
to be most relevant.
Here we contrast this with reducing directly on tori
at once via “toroidification”
\mathbb{T}^{k}(S^{4})
(see
section

3.2.3
).

(vi)

Moduli space
:
We introduced in
[
SV21
]
a new moduli space, similar to the moduli space
A/W
of compactified M-theory
(see (
1
)) and the moduli space
H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{R})/W
of generalized Kähler forms
\omega
on the del Pezzo surface
[
INV02
]
, in the context of rational homotopy theory as the moduli space of metric data added to the rational homotopy type.

(vii)

Probing into the nonabelian part of the moduli space
:
We are, in a sense, supplying the topological content of the
nonabelian unipotent part
N
of (
1
) by considering fields, which carry nontrivial weights of the toroidal part
A
.
We plan to expand on this further in
[
SV22
]
.

(viii)

The role of the Weyl group
:
Bringing in both dynamical/nontrivial tori, as the fibers of
iterated circle/torus bundles as above, allows us to
explain and clarify the exact role the Weyl group plays from physical and topological points of view.

(ix)

Data beyond that of Lie groups
: Our approach allows for extending Mysterious Duality
and Triality
to the rank

k\geq 9
case, taking us beyond Lie (to Kac-Moody) groups on the group/root-system side,
and beyond del Pezzo (to non-Fano and general-type) surfaces on the algebraic geometry side in
section

6.1
.
The context of Triality allows for an interpretation of the
imaginary roots of the Kac-Moody algebras, see
Sections

6.2
and
6.3
.

(x)

Real vs. complex
: We provide a careful treatment of real
structures appearing in M-theory and U-duality vs. complex forms that
appear
on the algebraic geometry side. This also allows for a natural transition between

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
S^{4}
initiating link

\raisebox{-.9pt} {3}⃝
for
k=0
,
as corresponding to modding out by complex conjugation.

(xi)

Compatibility with T-duality and S-duality
:
Our approach and extension are compatible with both S-duality in type IIB string
and T-duality between type IIA and type IIB string theories, and same at the
dynamical level from the physical and topological points of view.
See
section

4.1
and
section

4.2
.

(xii)

Modeling classical statements in algebraic geometry
:
Adopting this perspective allows us to provide remarkable topological and physical
manifestations of various famous statements in classical algebraic geometry, including
27 lines on a cubic, etc. in
section

5.2
.
For instance, we showed in
[
SV21
]
that
\pi_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})
is 27-dimensional and splits
canonically into 27 lines
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}
. Here we provide further connections
in
section

5
.

(xiii)

E_{k}
-symmetry structure on rational homotopy groups
: The
E_{k}
symmetry gives a way to organize and handle the computation of rational homotopy groups of cyclic loop spaces
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
, arising in the dimensional reduction on
T^{k}
.
We also expand on this further in
[
SV22
]
.

The duality-symmetric approach.

In the systematic dimensional reduction presented in
[
SV21
]
, we
treat the fields and their
*
-duals as independent,
with all equations of motion and the duality relations between the doubled fields accounted
for, as in the
duality-symmetric (doubled field) formulation of
D=11
supergravity

[
BBS98
]
, of type IIB
D=10
supergravity
[
DLS97
]
[
DLT98
]
,
and of type IIA
D=10
supergravity
[
BNS04
]
, the last obtained
by dimensionally reducing the first.
The global dimensional reduction leading to the latter is performed in

[
MS03
]
[
FOS03
]
.
This duality-symmetric formulation also allows for treating higher-order corrections, as in
[
ST17
]
.
A higher cohomological duality-symmetric formulation was initiated in
[
Sa06
]
and highlighted in
[
Sa10
]
.
Such a duality-symmetric formulation, when pursued in the context of
S^{4}
,
allows for remarkable cancellation of all prominent anomalies in M-theory
[
FSS19b
]
and for compatibility with the K-theoretic formulations in types IIA/IIB

[
FSS18a
]
[
FSS18b
]
[
BMSS18
]
.

Bridging the gap between
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
S^{4}
.

As above,
the topological side of the
Triality
uses the (rational homotopy type of the) 4-sphere
S^{4}
as a starting point. This is a departure from the setting of the complex projective plane
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
on the algebraic-geometric side.
Aside from this being a shift in perspective, can one find a mechanism for going from one to the other?
We propose that this is generally a transition from the complex setting
to the real setting. Beyond the general conceptual level, we offer
two tantalizing routes which support adopting this perspective:

(i)

Via modding out by
complex conjugation
on the algebraic geometry side (cf. item
(x)
above).
We discuss this in
section

2.3
. This uses the Arnold-Kuipers-Massey theorem
(
[
Ar88
]
[
Ku74
]
[
Le84
]
[
La82
]
[
Mas73
]
[
Mar86
]
[
HH11
]
).
We also establish the compatibility of this process with
S-duality in type IIB string theory in
section

4.1
:

\textstyle{\framebox{{Type IIB via}
$\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}$ }\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}quotient\;by}
\scriptstyle{\rm\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}S-duality}
\textstyle{\framebox{{M-theory via} $\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}quotient\;by}
\scriptstyle{\rm\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}complex\;conjugation}
\textstyle{\framebox{{M-theory via} $S^{4}$}}

(ii)

Via
Hopf fibrations
: The transition is from the complex Hopf fibration,
which can be viewed as part of the process of blowing up,
to the quaternionic Hopf fibration
(viewed as a topological, i.e., in some sense, real fibration):
2
2

2

Real and
quaternionic are closely related and can occur together in
topology, much more so than with the complex case; e.g. in the topology of the orthogonal group.
M-theory has prominent real and quaternionic aspects, but not as many complex ones.

\scriptstyle{h_{\mathbb{H}}}

Automorphisms.

The Weyl group acts on cohomology and, equivalently, homology of del Pezzo surfaces.
Viewing the perspective in
[
INV02
]
schematically as:

\textstyle{\text{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65} \bf\footnotesize Weyl group}\ \rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\circlearrowright$}~~H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{R})=\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm linear}
\scriptstyle{\rm duality}
\textstyle{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{*}_{k}\cong H_{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{R})\ \rotatebox[origin]{-270.0}{$\circlearrowright$}~~\text{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65} \bf\footnotesize Weyl group},}

(4)

the picture we have on the topological side is considering, in a certain sense,
a nonlinear version of this;
schematically:

automorphisms

\circlearrowright

Sullivan model
\scriptstyle{\rm Koszul}
\scriptstyle{\rm duality}
\textstyle{\framebox{Quillen model}\ \rotatebox[origin]{-270.0}{$\circlearrowright$}~~\text{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}
\bf\footnotesize automorphisms}.}

(5)

There are two features to highlight here, as demonstrated in
[
SV21
]
:

(a)

We are replacing linear entities, namely cohomology and homology, with nonlinear entities,
namely rational models. The Sullivan model is nonlinear in two respects: it has products of
elements and a nonlinear differential. The Quillen model
combines these two
nonlinearities into one (upon dualizing) captured by the Lie bracket or, more generally, the
L_{\infty}
-brackets.

(b)

The structures are
secondary, in the sense of “gauge transformations of gauge
transformations:”

\text{Weyl group}\quad\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\circlearrowright$}\quad\text{Torus}\quad\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\circlearrowright$}\quad M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})\;.

The first two, i.e., the Weyl group and the torus together with their actions, is what participates in the correspondence
(
4
), while these acting in turn on the third is what is involved in
the correspondence (
5
).
However, we emphasize that both correspondences share one and the same torus.

Thus, the Weyl group, being an isometry group of the abelian Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
of infinitesimal symmetries of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
, is a “second derived” object with respect to
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
:
W(E_{k})
is the group of “symmetries of symmetries” of
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
.

Symmetry via torus actions.

We have seen in
[
SV21
]
that topology emerges in our perspective on the scaling/toroidal
symmetries via self-maps of topological spaces at hand, namely the 4-sphere
S^{4}
and the
circle factors
S^{1}
of the internal torus
T^{k}
, which
involves various actions of the multiplicative group

\mathbb{G}_{m}=\GL(1)
:

(i)

An action of one factor
\mathbb{G}_{m}
already in eleven dimensions
corresponds to the trombone symmetry of the C-field
(see
section

2.4
). In turn,
from the point of view
of cohomotopy (i.e., the generalized cohomology theory arising from mapping to
the sphere) and the 4-sphere as a universal target space

[
Sa13
]
, we find that this corresponds to the scaling symmetry
or self-maps of the 4-sphere.

(ii)

Upon reduction on
T^{k}=S^{1}\times\cdots\times S^{1}
, we get an
action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{k}
, where the
i
th
\mathbb{G}_{m}
corresponds
to the scaling symmetry of the
i
th circle in
T^{k}
, i.e.,
self-maps
S^{1}\rightarrow S^{1}
of the circle fibers. See
section

3.1
and
section

3.2
.

Symmetry via Weyl group actions.

We consider
the following
aspects:

(i)

Relation between the Weyl group
W(G)
and the integral form
G(\mathbb{Z})
of the
U-duality group
G
:
For compactificatons of M-theory on
rectangular tori with no

C
-field,
the U-duality group acting on this class of compactifications is
realized as the Weyl group of
E_{k}

[
EGKR98
]
[
BFM99
]
.
Likewise in supergravity,
W(G)
is often assumed to be a subgroup of the discrete U-duality group
G(\mathbb{Z})
. This is a subtle point, and
a
discussion is needed here – see the end of
section

3.5
;

(ii)

The Weyl group as a symmetry:

We explain how the Weyl group
W
acts on the Picard group
{\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{k})

as a symmetry. Hence it makes sense to mod out by it in the moduli space.
Any two exceptional configurations on
\mathbb{B}_{k}
are
W
-translates of one another,
provided the signs of the intersection forms are preserved

[
Na61
]
[
Har85
]
; see
section

3.4
;

(iii)

Weyl group ‘action’ on field strengths
:
From a physics perspective, the action of
W(G)

for toroidal compactification for
k\leq 8
has been considered in

[
LPS96
]
, where the effect is captured by the axionic/dilatonic prefactors
multiplying the field strengths in their dimensional reduction.
3
3

3

This depends on what one calls a “field strength”. The topological
perspective favors the bare ones, rather than the ones which
are scaled by dilaton and
axion vectors, and possibly additionally shifted by composites of lower-degree
field strengths and potentials, with the understanding that the latter might
be favored in parts of the supergravity literature (see e.g.
[
LP96
]
[
LLP98
]
).

In particular,
the dilaton vectors transform in a similar fashion as
weight vectors under a subgroup of the discrete U-duality group identified
as the Weyl group.
We parametrize the apparent mathematical difficulties encountered in
[
LPS96
]

by clarifying the entities on which the Weyl groups act and how they do so:

W
acts directly on the linear data arising
from field strengths and not on the field strengths themselves. Nevertheless,
there will be a correspondence of the form

Fields
Root vectors in lattice

\scriptstyle{\rm dual}

Divisors on del Pezzo

See
section

3.4
and
section

3.5
, especially the end of
section

3.2.2
;

(iv)

Weyl orbits:

With the relation between
W(G)
and
G(\mathbb{Z})
clarified,
the orbits of
W
are related to the orbits of
G
, with the latter
being determined in
[
CMS15
]
(see also
[
BDDFMR10
]
). The Weyl orbits have also
been studied in
[
FRT04
]
from the point of view of sigma models.
Our formulation via moduli spaces clarifies the role of the Weyl group
and the corresponding geometry (see
section

3.4
);

(v)

Weyl group and automorphisms of del Pezzo surfaces:

The two groups
W(G)
and
\Aut(\mathbb{B}_{k})
are related but they
generally
do not coincide (depending on the rank), and we clarify the role of each
and how they relate. This helps in understanding the relationship between
U-duality and Mysterious Duality/Triality (see
section

3.5
).

The
E_{k}
symmetry patterns.

The dimensional reduction of M-theory on
a
k
-torus gives rise to a theory in
D=11-k
dimensions
with symmetry pattern
[
INV02
]
in the five columns in Table

1
below, matching the familiar pattern for del Pezzo surfaces
[
Ma74
]
,
to which we added in
[
SV21
]

the 6th column for cyclic loop spaces (“cyclifications”
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
of
S^{4}
),
as well as the 7th column corresponding to torus symmetry.
This highlights the interrelations among Lie theory (4th column),
algebraic geometry (5th column), and topology/physics (6th column),
as appropriate by the trichotomy/triality in Figure
1
.

Extension to Kac-Moody.

As indicated in
[
SV21
]
, on which we expand further in
section

6.1
below,
our formulation allows us to extend the theory to the higher ranks,
k=9
,
10
, and
11
,
corresponding to infinite-dimensional cases.
Indeed, since our discussion extends beyond the Lie setting to the Kac-Moody
setting, we
have extensions of the Triality in Figure
1
that go beyond
the del Pezzo/Fano case on the algebraic side.
We also observe that cyclic loop spaces, when we go beyond
k=8
, undergo a transition analogous to that on the del Pezzo/root systems/Lie algebra side: the degree of the cyclification
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
in the sense of (
36
) ceases to be positive, the corresponding root system becomes infinite, and the metric on the
k
-dimensional real vector space holding the root system ceases to be Euclidean; see
[
SV21
, Remark 4.7]
.
The surface
\mathbb{B}_{9}
and cyclification
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{9}S^{4}
give rise to a rank-9 parabolic lattice,
while
\mathbb{B}_{10}
and
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{10}S^{4}
, and
\mathbb{B}_{11}
and
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{11}S^{4}
correspond to rank-10 and rank-11 hyperbolic lattices, respectively.

Further proposals and outlook.

This paper, as well as its mathematical companion
[
SV21
]
,
can be viewed as an initiation of a research program. We have established
new connections
and provided correspondences in these two installments, but clearly there
is a lot more that deserves to be investigated. Among these are the following:

(i)

We have obtained the rational model for type IIB
directly via minimal models in
[
SV21
]
.
It would be interesting to work
out a model at the topological space level, analogous
(and certainly related) to the 4-sphere;

(ii)

Our discussion suggests a much bigger
picture. For instance, we should take the Quillen model as a symmetry Lie algebra
seriously. We expect that one would be able to work out general
results on a gauge action of the Quillen model for any topological space.
Hence further Lie-theoretic aspects wait to be uncovered.
We will indeed discuss this in
[
SV22
]
.

(iii)

Studying only toroidal, i.e., commuting symmetries of the real homotopy types of the
space
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
(and its Sullivan minimal model) for each
k
already allows us to
uncover the hidden
E_{k}
root structure. It would be interesting to identify the full
symmetry group of this real homotopy type. This would be a certain Lie group that contains
the toroidal symmetry group
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{k+1}
, as the maximal split torus. We anticipate
the answer to be some real algebraic group between
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{k+1}
and a toroidal extension
of a real form of the Lie group
E_{k}
. In the latter case, we would have a manifest action
of the U-duality group
E_{k}
on the fields, coming from an action on the universal model
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
.

(iv)

In order to encode geometric information, such as connections/potentials,
smooth structure, and (further)
metric data, we would need geometric/differential/smooth refinements of our
cyclification and toroidification (cf. the discussion in

section

3.2.3
) models,

\widehat{\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{T}^{k}(S^{4})}
,
generally along the lines of

[
FSS15
]
[
FSS17
]
[
GS21
]
for the special cases
k=0
and
1
, i.e.,
M-theory and its reduction to type IIA string theory.

(v)

A parametrized version
of the sphere model has been proposed in
[
BMSS18
]

as a way to include higher degree field strengths,
F_{8}
and
F_{10}

in type IIA and also
F_{9}
in type IIB.
It would be interesting to see the relation
to del Pezzo surfaces (and beyond). Similarly, extending beyond rational homotopy theory
via
S^{4}
rather than
S^{4}_{\mathbb{R}}
, in the context of Hypothesis H (see
section

2.1
).

(vi)

Relating del Pezzo and cyclifications: extending the transition from
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
to
S^{4}

to that of del Pezzo surfaces
\mathbb{B}_{k}
to the cyclification spaces
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
.
The correspondence for
k\geq 1
seems to be subtler than
just modding out by complex conjugation:

\textstyle{\mathbb{B}^{\prime}_{1}=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\bf/S-duality}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.3906,0.0195}\bf blowup}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}_{0}=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\bf/complex\;conjugation}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.3906,0.0195}\bf blowup}
\textstyle{S^{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\bf cyclification}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.3906,0.0195}\bf blowup}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\bf cyclification}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.3906,0.0195}\bf blowup}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}^{2}_{c}S^{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\bf cyclification}
\textstyle{\overset{\vdots}{\mathbb{B}_{k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.3906,0.0195}\bf blowup}
\textstyle{\overset{\vdots}{\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\bf cyclification}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}

(6)

(vii)

Clarifying the role of Kac-Moody groups and algebras from a topological perspective:
what is so special about
k=9
,
k=10
or
k=11
from the perspective of cyclic loop spaces?

2
From Mysterious Duality to Mysterious Triality via Hypothesis H

2.1
Hypothesis H: M-theory dynamics via the
Sullivan model of
S^{4}

As in
[
SV21
]
, we replace the notion of a
rational Sullivan minimal model

of a topological space
[
FHT01
]
[
FOT08
]

[
GM13
]

with that of a
real Sullivan minimal model
, given that real coefficients of physical
fields could be a bit more natural than rational ones (see the
discussion in
[
FSS20
]
). We will therefore
assume that our algebraic models are defined over the reals
\mathbb{R}

(see
[
BS95
]
[
GM13
]
).

We adopt the perspective proposed in
[
Sa13
]
and known as
Hypothesis H
of viewing the 4-sphere
S^{4}
as the
universal space of form fields in M-theory. The significance of this is that
S^{4}
encodes, entirely in its topology, the field
G_{4}
and its dual
G_{7}
as well as their

dynamics
.
This space is viewed as a universal space in the sense that these field
configurations are given at the homotopy level by real homotopy classes of
maps from spacetime
Y^{11}
to
S^{4}
, and whenever geometry is included, one
would need to consider all maps; see
[
FSS15
]
[
FSS17
]
[
GS21
]
(but
here we will concentrate on topology).

In 11-dimensional supergravity, which is the low-energy limit of M-theory, the equations of motion (EOMs) are
[
CJS78
]

dG_{4}=0,\qquad d*G_{4}+\tfrac{1}{2}G_{4}\wedge G_{4}=0\;.

(7)

When combined with the self-duality condition

G_{7}:=*G_{4}\;,

(8)

these may be rewritten as

dG_{4}=0,\qquad dG_{7}+\tfrac{1}{2}G_{4}\wedge G_{4}=0\;,

(9)

where the fields
G_{4}
and
G_{7}
are represented by differential forms of degree 4 and 7, respectively, on the 11-dimensional spacetime
Y^{11}

of M-theory, and
*
denotes the Hodge star operator, which captures the dependence on the metric on
Y^{11}
.
Note that
locally
we may write

G_{4}=dC_{3},\qquad G_{7}=dC_{6}-\tfrac{1}{2}C_{3}\wedge G_{4}\;

for some differential forms
C_{3}
and
C_{6}
, viewed as the corresponding
potentials
.

However, we will use the duality-symmetric (doubled field)
formulation, where
G_{4}
and
G_{7}
are treated
independently
[
BBS98
]
(see also
[
MS03
]
[
Sa06
]
[
ST17
]
[
BMSS18
]

for more global treatments). This will also
suppress any explicit dependence on the metric, suitable for
our topological perspective, which may be regarded as describing the topological background of the full story. To get the full picture at the level of fields, one simply adds metric data to spacetime and imposes the duality relation
*G_{4}=G_{7}
. We have also found a way to add metric data to the universal real homotopy model of
S^{4}
(and its cyclifications) via introducing moduli parameters
[
SV21
]
; see also “Geometric aspects of the moduli space…” at the end of
Section

3.4
.

The topological aspects of the M-theory dynamics at the real homotopy level are captured by the real homotopy
theory description given by the Sullivan minimal model
of
S^{4}
,
[
Sa13
]
,
which we denote
M(S^{4})
:
4
4

4

We will use lowercase letters for universal elements and uppercase letters to denote
spacetime fields.

\displaystyle M(S^{4})=(\mathbb{R}[g_{4},g_{7}],d)\,\qquad dg_{4}=0,\qquad dg_{7}=-\tfrac{1}{2}g_{4}^{2}\,,

(10)

where the degree of each of the generators
g_{4}
and
g_{7}
is given by the
corresponding subscript:

\lvert g_{4}\rvert=4
,
\lvert g_{7}\rvert=7
.
Here choose to include the factor of
-\tfrac{1}{2}
in the
equations of the model, as opposed to being absorbed by the generators
(see
[
FSS17
, Ex. 3.3]
).

The de Rham
cohomology of
S^{4}
is generated by the volume form
d
vol of degree
n
.
This gives a generator
g_{4}
in degree 4, with
dg_{4}=0
, which generates a polynomial algebra.
Since
M(S^{4})
must be free, the only way
to kill
g_{4}^{2}
is to introduce an element
g_{7}
of degree 7 with
dg_{7}=g_{4}^{2}
(or any scalar multiple of
g_{4}^{2}
). The resulting the DGCA,
(
10
),
is minimal. The map from
M(S^{4})
to the de Rham algebra (differential forms),
given by
\Omega^{\bullet}(S^{4})
by
g_{4}\mapstochar\rightarrow

d
vol and
g_{7}\mapstochar\rightarrow 0
,
defines a
quasi-isomorphism
,
i.e
., an algebra homomorphism which
induces an isomorphism on cohomology.

In order to include spacetime, comparing (
9
) with (
10
), we see that there exists a differential graded (dg) algebra homomorphism

\displaystyle M(S^{4})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow(\Omega^{\bullet}(Y),d),

(11)

identifying
g_{4}
with
G_{4}

and

g_{7}
with
G_{7}
,
where
(\Omega^{\bullet}(Y),d)
is the de Rham algebra of the 11-dimensional spacetime
Y^{11}
. The de Rham algebra is, in fact, a real homotopy model of the manifold
Y^{11}
, and this model could be different from the Sullivan minimal model of
Y
.
Rational (or, actually, real
[
FSS20
]
) homotopy theory provides a canonical continuous map

Y\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow S^{4}_{\mathbb{R}},

(12)

where
S^{4}_{\mathbb{R}}
is the
rationalization over
\mathbb{R}

of
M(S^{4})
, a certain universal topological space whose Sullivan minimal model
M(S^{4}_{\mathbb{R}})
is
M(S^{4})
, such that the pullback map from the Sullivan minimal model of
S^{4}_{\mathbb{R}}
to the de Rham model of
Y^{11}
is given by (
11
);
see
[
BS95
]
[
FSS20
]
.

Cohomotopy vs. cohomology.

Even though the above connection is at the rational level,
it was suggested in
[
Sa13
]
, that there is actually a continuous map to
the honest-to-goodness 4-sphere
S^{4}
,

Y\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow S^{4}
,
which induces the identification between the rational model and differential forms.
Indeed, a comparison of this target to its linearization, that is to say, the
Eilenberg-MacLane classifying space
K(\mathbb{Z},4)
,
which encodes the C-field as captured by a degree 4 class, with the
4-sphere is presented in
[
GS21
]
through a Postnikov tower analysis.
The nonabelian nature and the shift by Pontrjagin classes in the
quantization condition of the C-field are studied in
[
FSS19b
]
.
As also indicated below in
section

2.3.3
, the quaternionic
Hopf fibration
h_{\mathbb{H}}
over
S^{4}\cong\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}^{1}

plays a central role in describing the field
strengths on the M-theory
side in

[
Sa13
]
[
FSS19c
]
[
FSS19b
]
,
while quaternionic orientation using
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
(and its
finite-dimensional stabilizations in low dimensions) are
central to the constructions in

[
SS21
]
.
Rationally, since
\pi_{i}(\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}^{\infty})\cong\pi_{i-1}(S^{3})
is torsion for

i>4
, we have a (weak) rational homotopy equivalence between

\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
K(\mathbb{Q},4)
.
The Postnikov tower of
S^{4}
can be related to
\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}

(with stages described in
[
GS21
]
):

\textstyle{S^{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K(\mathbb{Z},4)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\simeq}_{\dim\leq 4}}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{H}\mathbb{P}^{\infty})_{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\simeq}_{\dim\leq 15}}
\textstyle{BE_{8}.}

See
[
SS19a
]
[
SS19b
]
[
SS21
]
for recent developments in that direction. Note that a map
Y\rightarrow K(\mathbb{Z},4)
defines a cohomology class in
H^{4}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\cong[Y,K(\mathbb{Z},4)]
, while
Y\rightarrow S^{4}
defines a cohomotopy class in
[Y,S^{4}]
.

Postnikov tower and minimal model for
S^{4}
.

The part of the Postnikov tower above can be viewed as a decomposition of
S^{4}
, and is intimately related to
a dual decomposition of the corresponding Sullivan minimal model (
10
) (into successive cofibrations).
Generally, and noting that Postnikov towers can be constructed over any abelian group
including
\mathbb{R}
(see e.g.
[
MP12
, § 3.5]
),
we have the following scheme
[
GM13
]
of connections:

\textstyle{{\rm Manifolds}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm smooth\;forms}
\textstyle{{\rm DGAs}/\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}{\rm-spaces}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\framebox{${\rm Minimal\;Models}/\mathbb{R}$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm dualization}
\textstyle{\framebox{${\rm Postnikov\;towers}/\mathbb{R}$}\phantom{AAA}}

The starting point for both is the same. As in the case of Postnikov tower, where one starts with base space
K(\mathbb{R},4)
,
one can build the Sullivan model of
S^{4}
by starting with the minimal model
of
K(\mathbb{R},4)
, which is
\mathbb{R}[g_{4}]
. In order to build
M(S^{4})
, we
adjoin the above data of
g_{7},dg_{7},
and
z_{8}
through two maps as follows:

– a map
i:\mathbb{R}[z_{8}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}[g_{7},dg_{7}]
taking

z_{8}
to
dg_{7}
.

– a map
f:\mathbb{R}[z_{8}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}[g_{4}]
taking

z_{8}
to
g_{4}^{2}
or a scalar multiple of it, such as
-\tfrac{1}{2}g_{4}^{2}
, which is used in this paper for better matching the EOMs.

These maps form the pushout square

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}[z_{8}]_{\phantom{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{\ulcorner\phantom{AAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAAA}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}[g_{4}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}[g_{7},dg_{7}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(S^{4}),}

which means we amalgamate the NE and SW pieces into the model in the SE corner in such a way that they agree in the
NW corner.
Dually (see
[
GM13
]
), we have the first nontrivial Postnikov section
(S^{4}_{\mathbb{R}})_{4}=K(\mathbb{R},4)
, whose minimal
model is
\mathbb{R}[g_{4}]
, and dually to the map
i

we have the path fibration

\pi:PK(\mathbb{R},8)\rightarrow K(\mathbb{R},8)
.
We get the following pullback diagram, where
k_{2}
is the second
k
-invariant of the Postnikov tower for

S^{4}_{\mathbb{R}}
, capturing the quadratic nature of the EOMs (
7
)
(see
[
GS21
]
for the general integral case):

\textstyle{S^{4}_{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{PK(\mathbb{R},8)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}

\textstyle{(S^{4}_{\mathbb{R}})_{4}\simeq K(\mathbb{R},4)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k_{2}}
\textstyle{K(\mathbb{R},8).}

2.2
M-theory gauge structure via the Quillen model of
S^{4}

Quillen’s rational homotopy theory allows us to pass back and forth from a simply connected
topological space
X
to the Lie algebra of its loop space

\framebox{${\rm Spaces}/{\rm rational\;homotopy\;equivalance}$}\;\;\overset{\sim}{\longleftrightarrow}\;\;\framebox{${\rm DGLAs}/{\rm quasi-}{\rm isomorphism}$}

such that if a space
X
corresponds to a DGLA
L
, then

H^{*}(L)=\pi_{*}(X;\mathbb{Q})
as vector spaces.
Every reasonably nice space

5
5

5

That is, simply connected spaces with rational homology of finite type.

admits a minimal free Lie model
(L(v),\partial v=0)
, unique up to isomorphism.

6
6

6

Note that in modern perspectives, one considers
L_{\infty}
rather than free Lie.

The Sullivan minimal model
M(Z)
(see
section

2.1
)
of each of the spaces
Z
we are
considering has quadratic differential. This model
is actually the symmetric algebra on a space of
generators:

M(Z)=(S(Q(Z)[-1]^{*}),d),

where
Q(Z)
is the
Quillen minimal model
of

Z

[
Qu69
]
, which in our quadratic-differential
case is given by the
graded Lie algebra of real homotopy groups

Q(Z):=\pi_{\bullet}(Z)\otimes\mathbb{R}[1]

of
Z
.
An equivalent, more standard description of the corresponding
Lie bracket on
\pi_{\bullet}(Z)[1]\otimes\mathbb{R}\cong\pi_{\bullet}(\Omega Z)\otimes\mathbb{R}
, is known as
Whitehead product
, which does
not appeal to based loop spaces; see, e.g.,

[
FHT01
]
. We will not need it here
(see
[
FSS19c
]
[
FSS20
, §3.2]
for detailed discussion in this context).
As indicated in the Introduction, the two models are dual

Sullivan model
M

\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\bf\rm homotopy\;groups,\;or\;generators}

Quillen model
Q
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\bf\rm de\;Rham,\;or\;graded\;commutative\;algebra}

Now we start with the case

Z=S^{4}
.

k=0
.
The following is, in a sense, dual (see (
2
)) to the description of the fields via the Sullivan
minimal model of
S^{4}
in (
10
).

M-theory gauge algebra via the Quillen (free Lie) model of
S^{4}
.

The graded Lie algebra
\pi_{*}(\Omega S^{4})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
is a free graded
Lie algebra on a generator
v_{3}
of degree 3.
In the tensor algebra
T(v_{3})
on a single degree 3 generator
v_{3}
, this is the free
Lie subalgebra
\mathbb{L}(v_{3})=\mathbb{R}v_{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}[v_{3},v_{3}]
.
The Sullivan minimal model
M(S^{4})
can be extracted in a direct fashion from this model
as what is called the cochain algebra
C^{*}(\mathbb{L}(v_{3}))
.
This algebra is free in that the differential is zero.
Alternatively, one can use a priori independent generators, so that the Quillen model of
S^{4}
is just the graded Lie algebra on two
generators

\displaystyle Q(S^{4})=\mathbb{R}e_{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}e_{6},

(13)

\displaystyle\lvert e_{3}\rvert=3,\quad\lvert e_{6}\rvert=6,

\displaystyle[e_{3},e_{3}]=e_{6},\quad[e_{3},e_{6}]=0,\quad[e_{6},e_{6}]=0,

which is actually the free graded Lie algebra over
\mathbb{R}
generated by
e_{3}
.
Remarkably, as highlighted in
[
SV21
]
, this captures the algebra of
gauge transformation of the C-field and its
dual and, hence, also captures the Dirac quantization of the M-branes
(see
[
CJLP98b
]
[
LLPS99
]
[
KS03
]
[
Sa10
]
).

We will later consider the reduction of this algebra,
corresponding to cyclic loop spaces
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
for
k>0

(see
section

3.1
and
section

3.2
).

2.3
Algebraic topology vs. algebraic geometry: real vs. complex

Adopting the perspective that
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
and del Pezzo surfaces

\mathbb{B}_{k}
are about the complex world
while
S^{4}
and its cyclifications
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
are about the real world, several aspects fall naturally and compatibly into place. These include

(i)

the fact that, generally, M-theory is naturally a real theory;

(ii)

the appearance of real groups (the noncompact forms) as U-duality groups in toroidal reductions of M-theory;

(iii)

the passage from complex algebraic geometry to real homotopy theory as a transition from
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
to
S^{4}
by modding out by complex conjugation, see below. Similarly, we can transition from
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
corresponding to type IIB
to
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
corresponding to M-theory (see
Section

4.1
).

2.3.1
From
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
to
S^{4}
via complex conjugation

We consider a natural way of transitioning from
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
to
S^{4}
as a possible way of directly relating algebraic geometry and algebraic topology in
Mysterious Triality, see
Figure

1
.

Complex conjugation automorphism.

We intuitively describe how to utilize the quotient by complex conjugation on the complex manifold

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}

being a smooth manifold diffeomorphic to
S^{4}
, via
the Arnold-Kuipers-Massey theorem;
see
[
Ar88
]
[
Mas73
]
[
Ku74
]
[
La82
]
[
Le84
]
[
Mar86
]
[
AB03
]
[
HH11
]
for various approaches and proofs.

Effect of complex conjugation on middle (co)homology.

In transitioning from
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
to
S^{4}
, we would like to ask what happens to cohomology classes.
The group
of homotopy classes of self-homotopy
equivalences of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(see e.g.
[
Ba96
]
),
generated by complex conjugation
\sigma:\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
which,
in homogeneous coordinates
[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}]
on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
takes the form

\sigma:[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}]\longmapsto[\overline{z}_{0}:\overline{z}_{1}:\overline{z}_{2}]\;.

This is a diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
which is orientation-preserving, while it is orientation-reversing on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
therefore reverses the signs of the second (co)homology classes.
In fact, the action of
\sigma
on the homology of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
is trivial on
degree 4 classes.
7
7

7

Generally, trivial on
4k
-dimensional classes and multiplication
by
-1
on
(4k+2)
-dimensional classes
[
Mos00
]
.

This is consistent with the discussion we will see in
section

2.3
.

Identifying the quotient via gluing disks.

As in

[
Mos00
]
,
the conjugation action on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}={\rm Sp}^{2}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})
, viewed as the symmetric
square of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
acts by reflecting pairs of points in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
with respect to the real line. Denote by
D_{+}
(
D_{-}
) the subspace of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
formed by pairs of points

(z_{1},z_{2})
such that
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
lie in the same (different) open hemisphere of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Both
D_{\pm}
as well as their closures
\overline{D}_{\pm}
are
\sigma
-invariant.
Let
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\cong\mathbb{D}^{2}\cup_{S^{1}}\mathbb{D}^{2}
be the presentation of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
as two closed hemispheres
(or 2-disks
\mathbb{D}^{2}
)
glued at their intersection circle.
Notice that
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}/\sigma\cong(\overline{D}_{+}/\sigma)\cup(\overline{D}_{-}/\sigma)
.
The orbit spaces
\overline{D}_{\pm}/\sigma
can be identified with the symmetric
square of
\mathbb{D}^{2}
:

\overline{D}_{+}/\sigma=\overline{D}_{-}/\sigma={\rm Sp}^{2}(\mathbb{D}^{2})\simeq\mathbb{D}^{4},

where the intersection
(\overline{D}_{+}/\sigma)\cap(\overline{D}_{-}/\sigma)
is exactly the
boundary of
\overline{D}_{+}/\sigma
and
\overline{D}_{-}/\sigma
. Hence
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}/\sigma

is homeomorphic to a union of two 4-disks glued together along their boundary, i.e., to
a 4-sphere

\mathbb{D}^{4}\cup_{S^{3}}\mathbb{D}^{4}\simeq S^{4}.

2.3.2
Real vs. complex Lie algebras/groups

In the spirit of the previous section, here we clarify the role
of real vs. complex forms of Lie algebras and groups appearing
in our context.

Real forms of Lie groups.

Real forms of complex reductive algebraic groups arise in two equivalent ways.
First, since
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R}
is a Galois extension, for a complex affine algebraic group

G
(see
[
Bo91
, §11-14]
[
Sp98
, §11]
),
one can
define a real form
using an action of the Galois group on

G
. Since the Galois group has only one nontrivial element, a real
form of
G
is given by a single map,
called
conjugation
,

\sigma:G\rightarrow G
.
Such a map comes from an antilinear
automorphism of order two of the (complex) Hopf algebra of regular functions on
G
.
The corresponding
real form

G_{0}
of
G
will be the fixed-point subgroup of
\sigma
:

G_{0}:=G^{\sigma}.

Second, a
real form
of the complex Lie group

G(\mathbb{C})
of complex points of
G
is
the subgroup

G_{0}(\mathbb{R}):=G(\mathbb{R},\sigma):=G(\mathbb{C})^{\sigma}

of fixed points of
a real Lie group automorphism

\sigma
on
G(\mathbb{C})
of order two:

\sigma:G(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow G(\mathbb{C})
,
\sigma^{2}=\id
,
whose differential at the identity of

G(\mathbb{C})
is
an antilinear Lie-algebra automorphism.
The real form
G_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is a real Lie group of real dimension equal to the complex dimension of

G(\mathbb{C})
.
This repeats the discussion of real forms in the algebraic-group language from above, but now in the
category of analytic manifolds rather than of varieties.
For reductive algebraic groups, the two categories give rise to exactly the same real forms.

Real forms of Lie algebras.

A real Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is a
real form
of a complex Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, if

\mathfrak{g}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{0}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}=\mathfrak{g}_{0}\oplus i\mathfrak{g}_{0}\;,

the
complexification
of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, in which case

\mathfrak{g}_{0}=(\mathfrak{g})^{\sigma}\;,

where
\sigma
is the complex conjugation on
\mathfrak{g}
.
Every complex reductive Lie algebra has two special real forms: the compact real form and the split one. In this work, split real forms play a prominent role.
Split real Lie algebras follow their complex counterparts in many
respects: one is (semi)simple if and only if the complex Lie algebra is, the real rank equals the complex rank, etc. (see
[
OV94
]
).

Real maximal tori.

A complex maximal torus
T\subseteq G
of a complex linear algebraic group
G
is defined over

\mathbb{R}
(with respect to a conjugation
\sigma
on
G
) if it is preserved by
\sigma
.
In that case, the subgroup

T_{0}:=T^{\sigma}=G_{0}\cap T\subseteq G_{0}

is
called a
(
real
)
maximal torus
of the real form
G_{0}
.
All complex maximal tori of
G
are conjugate by elements of
G(\mathbb{C})
and isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{N}
for some
N\geq 0
,
where
\mathbb{G}_{m}:=\GL(1)
is the
multiplicative group
, whose group of complex points
\mathbb{G}_{m}(\mathbb{C})
is
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
the multiplicative group of nonzero complex numbers. There can be more than one conjugacy class of real maximal tori
in the real form
G_{0}
, and in that
case they are not all isomorphic over
\mathbb{R}
to each other or to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}
for any
n
.
Real subgroups of
G_{0}
which are isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}
for some
n\geq 0
over
\mathbb{R}
are called
split
(
real
)
tori

of
G_{0}
. A
maximal split
(
real
)
torus
is one which is not contained in a bigger split torus. Maximal split tori
are always conjugate by elements of
G_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, and the groups of their real points are isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{m}(\mathbb{R})^{n}=(\mathbb{R}^{\times})^{n}
. When
G_{0}
is a split real form of a reductive linear algebraic group, a maximal split torus will also
be a maximal torus of
G_{0}
.
Of course, for us
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{e}_{k}
,
0\leq k\leq 8
, as in

Table

1
.

Example: Maximal tori in SL(2).

In the split real form
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
of
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, defined by the conjugation automorphism
\sigma^{s}(g)=\overline{g}
and
appearing in type IIB and in 9 dimensions,
there are two real forms of the maximal torus
\mathbb{G}_{m}\hookrightarrow{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
: the split one is the standard diagonal torus
{\rm diag}(t,t^{-1})\subset{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})

with real points
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, and the compact one is
\operatorname{SO}(2)\subset{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
real points the (compact) circle
S^{1}
. The latter in this case coincides with
the maximal compact subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
; however, we are interested in
the former. See also
section

4.1
.

2.3.3
Algebraic topology vs. algebraic geometry: Hopf fibrations

The complex Hopf fibration
h_{\mathbb{C}}
and quaternionic
Hopf fibration
h_{\mathbb{H}}
play prominent roles here.
We will see the former arising from the blowup process (cf.
[
LS08
]
), hence
as a source for transitioning from the complex side, with

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
appearing as the cone for
h_{\mathbb{C}}
.
The latter already
plays a central role in describing the field
strengths on the M-theory real side in

[
Sa13
]
[
FSS19c
]
[
FSS19b
]
[
SS21
]
.

Rank 2 bundles over the point.

Over the point
*
, the normal bundle
\nu:\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow E\xrightarrow{\;\pi\;}\ast

has a complex structure.
Set
E_{0}=E\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hskip 0.2pt\setminus}$}}}\{\rm zero\,section\}

and consider the bundle

\nu_{0}:\mathbb{C}^{2}\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hskip 0.2pt\setminus}$}}}\{0\}\rightarrow E_{0}\xrightarrow{\;\pi_{0}\;}\ast
.
Then
\mathbb{C}^{*}=\mathbb{C}\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hskip 0.2pt\setminus}$}}}\{0\}
acts on each fiber in
E_{0}
by
complex multiplication, with orbit space
P_{\nu}=E_{0}/\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
the projective bundle

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\overset{i}{\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}P_{\nu}\xrightarrow{\pi^{\prime}}*
.
These maps fit into a commutative diagram

\textstyle{E_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\textstyle{P_{\nu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ast}

where
q
is the quotient map.
Let
S^{3}\rightarrow S_{\nu}\rightarrow*
be the sphere bundle
and

D_{\nu}
be the disk bundle
\mathbb{D}^{4}\rightarrow D_{\nu}\rightarrow\ast

as the tubular neighborhood of the point
\ast
.
Since
\partial(\mathbb{D}^{4})\cong S^{3}
, then
D_{\nu}\simeq S_{\nu}\rightarrow\ast
is a homotopy
equivalence. Overall, we have the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{2}\supset\hskip-14.22636pt}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}^{4}}
\textstyle{\;S^{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{D_{\nu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\;S_{\nu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{P_{\nu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ast}

We take the complex Hopf fibration as the map of fibers
h_{\mathbb{C}}:S^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}

obtained as the restriction of the map
q:S_{\nu}\rightarrow P_{\nu}
to the fibers over the point
\ast
.

The complex Hopf fibration and orientations.

Consider the class
a\in H^{2}(P_{\nu};\mathbb{Z})
which has fiber restriction

a\in H^{2}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{Z})

(14)

given by the negative first Chern class

-c_{1}(\gamma^{1})
, where
\gamma^{1}
is the restriction to

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
of the
tautological line bundle over the classifying space
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
.
The fibers
\mathbb{D}^{4}
of the disk bundle
D_{\nu}
over the point
\ast
has a canonical
orientation given by its complex structure
\mathbb{D}^{4}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}

and determines a relative cohomology class

u_{\mathbb{D}^{4}}\in H^{4}(\mathbb{D}^{4},S^{3};\mathbb{Z})
. Through the connecting homomorphism

\delta:H^{3}(S^{4};\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\;\cong\;}H^{4}(\mathbb{D}^{4},S^{3};\mathbb{Z})
, this
determines an obstruction class

u_{S^{3}}\in H^{3}(S^{3};\mathbb{Z})\;.

(15)

Extension to
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

The Hopf fibration can be extended to a map
\widetilde{h}_{\mathbb{C}}:\mathbb{D}^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. For
z=(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
set
Z=\big(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}\big)^{1/2}
, so that the 4-disk and the
corresponding sphere are defined, respectively, as

\mathbb{D}^{4}=\big\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\;|\;Z\leq 1\big\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad S^{3}=\big\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\;|\;Z=1\big\}.

Now define the map

\widetilde{h}_{\mathbb{C}}:\mathbb{D}^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
by
z\mapstochar\rightarrow[z_{1}:z_{2}:1-Z]
.
Then for the restriction we have
\widetilde{h}_{\mathbb{C}}|_{S^{3}}=h_{\mathbb{C}}
, where
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}

is considered as the hyperplane with last coordinate 0, and the two maps are related via the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{D}^{4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{h}_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}}
\textstyle{S^{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

By a cofibrant replacement, as in
[
LS08
]
, the restriction is a cofibration

S^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We will use this
further below in (
17
).

The Sullivan model and the generators.

The Hopf fibration

h_{\mathbb{C}}
gives rise to the Sullivan models and projection maps
between them

\textstyle{\big(\bigwedge(x_{2},y_{3});dx_{2}=0,dy_{3}=x_{2}^{2}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{h_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}
\textstyle{\big(\bigwedge(y_{3});dy_{3}=0\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(S^{3})}

such that the corresponding classes coincide with the classes (
14
) and
(
15
) above, namely

[g(x_{2})]=a\qquad\text{and}\qquad[f(y_{3})]=-u_{S^{3}}\;.

The transition via cellular structure

We describe the transition from
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
to
S^{4}
via three further related approaches.

(i) The transition via CW-structure.

Consider
(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})
as a CW-pair by viewing

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
as a CW-subcomplex.
The cell structure of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
is
e^{0}\cup e^{2}\cup e^{4}

while that of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
is
e^{0}\cup e^{2}
.
The quotient space
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}/\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, obtained by collapsing
the subspace
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, inherits a natural cell
complex structure from
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}

and is a CW-complex
whose cells are the cells of the
difference
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hskip 0.2pt\setminus}$}}}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
plus one new 0-cell,
the image of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}/\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
With a single 0-cell
and the original 4-cell from
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, i.e.,
e^{0}\cup e^{4}
,
the quotient map

q:\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow S^{4}

(16)

gives a
homeomorphism
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}/\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\simeq S^{4}
.

(ii) The transition via cofibrations.

The above can also
be described starting from the complex Hopf fibration

S^{1}\rightarrow S^{3}\xrightarrow{h_{\mathbb{C}}}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Associated to this is
a cofibration sequence

8
8

8

This is the Barrat-Puppe sequence associated to a map
f:A\rightarrow Z

A\xrightarrow{f}X\xrightarrow{g}C_{f}\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma A\xrightarrow{\Sigma f}\Sigma X\xrightarrow{\Sigma g\;}\Sigma C_{f}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots

where
C_{\phi}
is the mapping cone of the map
\phi
and
\delta
is the
connecting homomorphism.

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 8.59721pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.59721pt\raise 153.81194pt\raise 5.93056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.93056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Sigma h_{\mathbb{C}}\;}$}}}\kern 178.0417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 210.4743pt\raise 5.89168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.89168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Sigma i_{\mathbb{C}}\;}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 233.77785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 233.77785pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
