289.51399pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 289.51399pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

(17)

Any 3-term sequence in (
17
) is a cofibration so, using
the suspension
\Sigma S^{3}\simeq S^{4}
, we extract

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{S^{4},}

(18)

which exhibits
S^{4}
the cofiber of
i_{\mathbb{C}}
.
This indicates that the origin of the transition indeed has to do with the complex
Hopf fibration
h_{\mathbb{C}}
.

(iii) The transition via homogeneous coordinates.

Consider the usual embedding
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}

given by the mapping of homogeneous coordinates

[z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapstochar\rightarrow[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}]
. Let
Z_{0}=\frac{z_{0}}{z_{2}}

and
Z_{1}=\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}
, assuming
z_{0}\neq 0
, and let

Z^{2}=|z_{0}|^{2}+|z_{1}|^{2}
. Then the map
f:\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\rightarrow S^{4}
is given by

[z_{0}:z_{1}:z_{2}]\mapstochar\rightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\left(\frac{2Z_{0}}{1+Z^{2}},\frac{2Z_{1}}{1+Z^{2}},\frac{-1+Z^{2}}{1+Z^{2}}\right)&z_{2}\neq 0,\\
(0,0,1)&z_{0}=0.\end{array}\right.

The case when
z_{0}=0
corresponds exactly to a point being in

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Hence
f
is constant on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
and so gives
rise to a quotient map
\overline{f}:\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}/\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow S^{4}
,
which is a homeomorphism.

Cohomotopy.

From the map
q:\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\rightarrow S^{4}
arising from collapsing

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
to a point, the homotopy classes of maps are given
by degree 4 Cohomotopy

\pi^{4}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}\;,

with generator the class of the map
[q]
.
Here
\pi^{4}(X)=[X,S^{4}]
is the base-point preserving homotopy classes of maps from
X
to
S^{4}
; see
[
We71
]
[
GS21
]
. Since the two spaces
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
S^{4}
are of the same
(real) dimension, this is of course given by the usual degree.

(Co)homology vs. (Co)homotopy

We consider the transition from (co)homology on the del Pezzo side to
(co)homotopy on the
S^{4}
side. Additionally, and to further
highlight the extent to which the approach is distinguished,
we explain why neither homotopy for
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
nor
(co)homology for
S^{4}
would work.

(Co)homology route for
S^{4}
?

Since the only nonzero (co)homology groups
of
S^{4}
are in degree 0 and 4, there are no higher (co)homology groups
for
S^{4}
and, in particular, none in degree 7 needed
to account for the dual field
G_{7}
. Hence this is clearly not a viable
route and, in fact, was among several considerations for proposing cohomotopy in

[
Sa13
]
.

Homotopy route for
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
?

From the long exact sequence on homotopy groups arising from the
fibration
S^{1}\rightarrow S^{5}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
,

\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\pi_{k}(S^{1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\pi_{k}(S^{5})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\pi_{k}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\pi_{k-1}(S^{1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots\;,

we get

\pi_{4}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2})=0
, which does not lead to a degree four
as
\pi_{4}(S^{4})
would.
We use the rational equivalence
\Omega S^{5}\simeq_{\mathbb{Q}}S^{4}
(or better the
suspension
S^{5}\simeq\Sigma S^{4}
, nonrationally) to get

\pi_{5}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2})\cong_{\mathbb{Q}}\pi_{4}(S^{4})\cong_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}\;.

One could say that we perhaps continue with higher homotopy groups of

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. For
k>5
it is still true that
\pi_{k}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2})\cong\pi_{k}(S^{5})
,
but all these groups are going to be pure torsion with no clear interpretation
and with no sign of the dual field or dynamics arising.
Hence this shows that the homotopy route for
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
is not the way to go.
An analogous statement holds, and even more so, for del Pezzo surfaces, which have quite
complicated homotopy groups with large unwieldy growth.

Relative homotopy.

However,
from the complex Hopf fibration
h_{\mathbb{C}}
, we get an isomorphism

\pi_{n}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})\cong\pi_{n-1}(S^{3})\;.

The relative homotopy group on the left is defined so that
corresponding homotopy classes of maps are trivial on the

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
factor which are are eventually contracting.
For
n=4
, we get
\pi_{4}
to appear naturally via
\pi_{4}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})
.
We also highlight that in the process the group
\pi_{2}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})

is killed (note that
\pi_{2}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z})
) since

S^{3}
is simply connected.

This now allows us to transition to
S^{4}
.

Common ground via Hurewicz.

We have seen indications that the map on homology
H_{4}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2})\rightarrow H_{4}(S^{4})
is nontrivial.
Indeed, the map
q
from (
16
) induces an isomorphism on
H_{4}
, so it is homotopically
nontrivial.
Aside from degree 0, homology only makes sense in top degree, which matches the lowest
degree occurring in homotopy, where they match by the Hurewicz isomorphism

\pi_{4}(S^{4})\cong H_{4}(S^{4})\cong\mathbb{Z}\;,

signalling the appearance of the generator
g_{4}
(universally) and the
field
G_{4}
(in spcetime). Hence, overall we have the following picture:

\textstyle{\underbrace{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{\rm\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}(co)homology}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\pi_{4}-,\,H_{4}-{\rm isomorphism}}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm`common\;ground^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\underbrace{S^{4}}_{\rm\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}(co)homotopy}}

Dynamics and the dual via higher homotopy.

In order to see the dual and the dynamics, we need higher homotopy
groups; in particular, (rationally)
\pi_{7}(S^{4})\cong\mathbb{Q}
.
So this should be a new effect in homotopy and a feature that
does not arise in (co)homology. Indeed, this confirms the viability of
the proposal for using

S^{4}
which achieves exactly that.

The above also indicates that it is quite natural to use
S^{4}
to extend,
and to some extent explain, Mysterious Duality to include fields via the Triality
(Figure
1
).

2.4
Trombone symmetry of M-theory in 11 dimensions via
S^{4}

Here we describe the toroidal symmetry of
S^{4}
and provide
a topological interpretation.

Trombone symmetries in M-theory.

We will employ scaling symmetries of the fields in the theory,
which we interpret topologically below
in terms of winding numbers (self-maps) of spheres in appropriate dimensions.
This novel perspective should be interesting in its own right, but it also allows us to
study Mysterious Duality from a topological viewpoint.

There is a rigid scaling symmetry, called
trombone symmetry
, of 11-dimensional supergravity
which preserves the ratio of the coefficients
of the Einstein-Hilbert and gauge-field kinetic terms and also keep the coefficient of the
Chern-Simons term in the same ratio. This is the
\mathbb{R}^{+}
symmetry

[
DNP86
]
[
CDF91
]
[
CLPS98
]

on the metric
g
and the C-field
C_{3}

g\mapstochar\rightarrow\lambda^{2}g\;,\qquad C_{3}\mapstochar\rightarrow\lambda^{3}C_{3}\;.

(19)

•

The Lagrangian scales homogeneously as
L\mapstochar\rightarrow\lambda^{9}L
. As this

\mathbb{R}^{+}
symmetry leaves
invariant the EOMs, it is appropriate that we use them in describing the dynamics.

•

This can alternatively be viewed as an engineering scale invariance of the classical equations,
as a consequence of the fact that there is just one overall dimensionful coupling constant,
which sits in front of the entire eleven-dimensional action.

•

While this symmetry is not a symmetry of
the Cremmer-Julia type, it plays a crucial role in understanding the
occurrence of the Cremmer-Julia
E_{k(+k)}
symmetries in the lower dimensional theories
[
CJLP98a
]
.

•

Generally, it acts on any
p
-form as multiplication by
\lambda^{p}
.
So for us, considering the duality-symmetric formulation, where we
treat the C-field and its dual as independent variables (giving up
metric and Hodge duality relations),

C_{3}\mapstochar\rightarrow\lambda^{3}\,C_{3}\;,\qquad C_{6}\mapstochar\rightarrow\lambda^{6}\,C_{6}\;.

Instead of using this action, which is associated with the anticanonical class
-K_{k}
in our context,
we will, however, use a simpler scaling arising from the action of tori in
section

2.4
:

C_{3}\mapstochar\rightarrow t\,C_{3}\;,\qquad C_{6}\mapstochar\rightarrow t^{2}\,C_{6}\;.

(20)

The corresponding scalings for field strengths
G_{4}
and
G_{7}
are
of the same form.

Trombone symmetries via weights associated to the 4-sphere.

In
[
SV21
]
, we studied real toroidal symmetries of minimal Sullivan
algebras
M
, i.e., diagonalizable actions
T\rightarrow\Aut M
of a

real split torus
, an affine algebraic group
T
isomorphic over

\mathbb{R}
to the group
\mathbb{G}_{m}=\GL(1)
, the multiplicative group (see
section

2.3.2
).
Here
\Aut M
is the group of automorphisms of
M
as a DGCA,
and by an
action

T\rightarrow\Aut M
we mean
a morphism of algebraic groups defined over
\mathbb{R}
.
Toroidal symmetries of
M
can be captured by considering a
maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus

T\subseteq\Aut M
.
M-theory in 11 dimensions will correspond to rank 1,
while later (in
section

3.1
and
section

3.2
)
we will see that higher ranks are obtained via dimensional reduction.

We start with the automorphism group
\Aut M(S^{4})
of the Sullivan minimal model
M(S^{4})

of
S^{4}
; see (
10
). By the degree argument,
an automorphism of
M(S^{4})
must take
g_{4}
to a scalar multiple of itself:

g_{4}\mapstochar\rightarrow tg_{4}

(21)

for some
t\in\mathbb{G}_{m}(\mathbb{R})
, and this determines the action of the
automorphism on
g_{7}
:

g_{7}\mapstochar\rightarrow t^{2}g_{7},

and thereby on the whole DGCA
M(S^{4})
. This gives an identification

\Aut M(S^{4})\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
over
\mathbb{R}
, and, therefore,
\Aut M(S^{4})

automatically coincides with its maximal split torus. Note that this
identification is unique up to automorphism of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, which could
only be
t\mapstochar\rightarrow t^{-1}
if not trivial. Thus, we get a
weight
decomposition
determined by

g_{4}\in M(S^{4})_{\epsilon_{0}},\qquad g_{7}\in M(S^{4})_{2\epsilon_{0}},

(22)

with the weights defined up to common sign, that is to say,

t^{\epsilon_{0}}=t

(23)

or
t^{\epsilon_{0}}=t^{-1}
.
Again, the weights are just determined by the weight of
g_{4}
, as
that is the only
d
-closed generator. We can always normalize this
ambiguity so as to have positive weights and assume (
23
) is valid. This choice also has topological motivation, as we will see below. We will combine weights in

section

2.5
.

Now we will present an interpretation of the toroidal symmetries
as rational (real) homotopy equivalences, see detailed construction
in
[
SV21
]
. These symmetries have physical
interpretation of trombone symmetries above.

Topological interpretation of toroidal symmetries coming from
S^{4}
.

Let us start with the symmetry (
21
). The
n
-folding map

\varphi_{0}(n):S^{4}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow S^{4}

(actually, any continuous map of degree
n
) acts on the homotopy group
\pi_{4}(S^{4})
as multiplication by
n
. Over the rationals and reals,
the map
\varphi_{0}(n)
has a rational homotopy inverse, which acts on
\pi_{4}(S^{4})
as multiplication by
1/n
, provided
n\geq 1
.
Combining this with an
m
-folding self-map of the sphere, we will get a map (morphism, to be precise)
S^{4}\rightarrow S^{4}
in rational homotopy theory,
which acts on
\pi_{4}(S^{4})
as multiplication by
m/n
. This map gives an action of the multiplicative group of the rationals
\mathbb{Q}
on
S^{4}
in
rational homotopy theory, which is equivalent to an action on the Sullivan minimal model
M(S^{4})
. This action extends by continuity to
an action of the multiplicative group
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
of the reals. A bit more generally, we can view this as an action of the real algebraic
group
\mathbb{G}_{m}
on
M(S^{4})
:

\mathbb{G}_{m}\times M(S^{4})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow M(S^{4}),

(24)

expressed explicitly by Equations (
21
)–(
23
).

The Lie algebra.

As in
[
SV21
]
, we can use the toroidal symmetries of
S^{4}
from above
and consider it at the Lie algebra level, by taking derivations.
The idea is to use the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{0}=\Lie(T^{1})
of the maximal real split torus
T^{1}
of
\Aut M(S^{4})
with its canonical factorization
T\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{G}_{m}
. This Lie algebra constitutes the
infinitesimal symmetries corresponding to the toroidal symmetries of

S^{4}
and acts on the
Sullivan minimal model
M(S^{4})
by derivations.
That is, we have a Lie algebra homomorphism:

\mathfrak{h}_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Der M(S^{4}),

which comes from taking the differential of the action

T^{1}\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Aut M(S^{4}).

\textstyle{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\footnotesize\rm Group}\;\;T^{1}\ \rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\circlearrowright$}~~\;\;\framebox{$\Aut M(S^{4})$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm differential\;of\;map}
\scriptstyle{\rm linearization}
\textstyle{\framebox{$\Der M(S^{4})$ }\;\;\;\ \rotatebox[origin]{-270.0}{$\circlearrowright$}~~\mathfrak{h}_{0}=\Lie(T^{1})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\rm Algebra}}

Now in order to proceed by extracting root data from topology, we will recall the
analogous situation of the corresponding del Pezzo surface.

Complex Projective space.

Consider the
del Pezzo surface

\mathbb{B}_{0}
which is just
the complex projective plane
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
.
The Picard group
{\rm Pic}(\mathbb{CP}^{2})
of line bundles is isomorphic to
the divisor class group and the 2nd cohomology group:

\Pic(\mathbb{CP}^{2})\cong H^{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{2};\mathbb{Z})
. This is a rank-
1
lattice with generator
\mathcal{H}
:

H^{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{2};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{H}

where
\mathcal{H}
is the class of the proper transform of the line
(here also a hyperplane)
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
.
The 2nd integral cohomology has a natural inner product
given by the intersection form:

\mathcal{H}\cdot\mathcal{H}=1.

(25)

\mathbb{CP}^{2}
has the
canonical class

\mathcal{K}_{0}:=\Omega^{2}_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}=-3\mathcal{H}

as well as the
anticanonical class
:

-\mathcal{K}_{0}=3\mathcal{H}
.
The
degree of the surface

\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is the self-intersection number
(-\mathcal{K}_{0})\cdot(-\mathcal{K}_{0})=9
.
The
degree of a divisor
\mathcal{D}\in\Pic(\mathbb{CP}^{2})
is measured with respect to the anticanonical map.
The
anticanonical class
-\mathcal{K}_{k}
“acts” on the
Picard group (degree-two cohomology)
\Pic(\mathbb{CP}^{2})\cong H^{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{2};\mathbb{Z})
by degree:

\deg\mathcal{D}:=-\mathcal{K}_{0}\cdot\mathcal{D},\qquad\mathcal{D}\in\Pic(\mathbb{CP}^{2}).

(26)

Here the “action” is understood as the intersection product
\Pic(\mathbb{CP}^{2})\otimes\Pic(\mathbb{CP}^{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. The
degree of a divisor

\mathcal{D}\in\Pic(\mathbb{CP}^{2})
is defined using the anticanonical morphism

f:\mathbb{B}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{d}
, and

d=h^{0}(\mathbb{CP}^{2},-\mathcal{K}_{0})-1=(-\mathcal{K}_{0})\linebreak\cdot(-\mathcal{K}_{0})=9

is known as the
degree of the surface
\mathbb{B}_{0}=\mathbb{CP}^{2}
,
whence
-\mathcal{K}_{0}
is the pullback
f^{*}\mathcal{H}
of the hyperplane class
\mathcal{H}\in\Pic(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{d})
and formula (
26
)
makes sense; see, e.g.,
[
Do12
]

and also
[
SV21
]
.

Degree in Quillen model of
S^{4}
.

In the case of M-theory we have a
notion of degree, natural for the
Quillen minimal model. The significance of this is coming from the fact that it corresponds to the degree of
the C-fields, i.e., the potentials
C_{3}
and
C_{6}
of the basic
fields, such as
G_{4}
and
G_{7}
, see (
8
) and
(
20
), and thereby to the dimension of the corresponding
branes, the M2- and M5-branes, respectively. This other notion of degree
just differs from the degree we have been using on the generators of
the Sullivan minimal model by one, but has a homotopy-theoretic
origin, as we now explain.

We have seen the Quillen model in
section

2.2
. The maximal split torus
T^{1}
and its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
act on the
Quillen minimal model
Q(S^{4})
with the same weights as on
the generators of the Sullivan minimal model
M=M(S^{4})
.
A remarkable fact is that the
degree operator

x\mapstochar\rightarrow\lvert x\rvert\cdot x,\qquad x\in Q(S^{4}),

in the Quillen minimal model singles out a distinguished element of the Lie
algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
.
Analyzing the degrees shows
[
SV21
]

that there is a unique element of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
, namely,

-K_{0}:=3h_{0}

(27)

which acts on the Quillen minimal model
Q(S^{4})
as the
degree operator:

-K_{0}x=\lvert x\rvert\cdot x.

Since the anticanonical class
-\mathcal{K}_{0}
of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}

acts on the Picard group by degree, it makes
all sense to use the element

-K_{0}=3h_{0}

as a distinguished element, the analogue of the anticanonical class.
Indeed, using (
13
), we have

-K_{0}v_{3}=3h_{0}v_{3}=|v_{3}|\cdot v_{3}

2.5
M-theory and del Pezzo cohomology classes

Here we provide an extension of
[
INV02
]
to include field
strengths
G_{4}
and
G_{7}
and show how (combinations of) these correspond to classes on the del Pezzo side.

Electric-magnetic/Hodge duality.

This duality in M-theory was interpreted in
[
INV02
]
as corresponding to classes
adding up to the anticanonical class

\mathcal{C}_{\rm Electric}+\mathcal{C}_{\rm Magnetic}=-\mathcal{K}=3\mathcal{H}
on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
We interpret this from the perspective of field strengths.
We naturally take
\mathcal{C}_{E}:=\mathcal{C}_{\rm Electric}=\mathcal{H}
to correspond to the field

G_{4}
and
\mathcal{C}_{M}:=\mathcal{C}_{\rm Magnetic}=2\mathcal{H}
to its dual
G_{7}
.
This also works universally for the elements

g_{4}
and
g_{7}
in the minimal model
M(S^{4})
.

Degree

M(S^{4})

There are two interpretations, one via the Sullivan model and another via
the Quillen model. We will focus on the former, leaving the latter, which involves
Whitehead products, for a separate treatment elsewhere
(as this requires a longer discussion).

The anticanonical class.

In the Sullivan perspective, we take
-\mathcal{K}
to correspond to the Chern-Simons form

CS_{11}:=C_{3}\wedge G_{4}\wedge G_{4}
when using only
G_{4}
, or the composite

G_{4}\wedge G_{7}
when using both fields,
G_{4}
and its dual
G_{7}
.

Entity

-\mathcal{K}

products
g_{4}^{3}

Division/Contraction

Let us explain the last row in the table above. This feature works in the simplest way on our universal model, the minimal Sullivan algebra
M(S^{4})=(\mathbb{R}[g_{4},g_{7}],d)
.
With the weight space decomposition (
22
), the weight space
M(S^{4})_{3\epsilon_{0}}
of the distinguished weight
3\epsilon_{0}
, which corresponds to the anticanonical class
-\mathcal{K}=3\mathcal{H}
, contains the element
g_{4}g_{7}
:

g_{4}g_{7}\in M(S^{4})_{3\epsilon_{0}}\;.

The weight
2\epsilon_{0}

corresponding to the class
-2\mathcal{H}=-\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{H}
,
contains the element
g_{7}
:

g_{7}\in M(S^{4})_{2\epsilon_{0}}\;.

The generator
g_{4}
is a non-zero-divisor in the algebra
M(S^{4})
, and we can interpret the subtraction
-\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{H}
as division by
g_{4}
:

g_{4}g_{7}\longmapsto g_{4}g_{7}/g_{4}=g_{7}\;.

At the level of spacetime fields
G_{4}
and
G_{7}
, which are actually not non-zero-divisors in the de Rham algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}(Y)
of the spacetime
Y
, this correspondence could be reincarnated
as the contraction/interior product

``-\iota_{\mathcal{H}^{\vee}}\mathcal{K}\,"=\iota_{G_{4}^{\vee}}G_{4}\wedge G_{7}=G_{7}\;.

Here
we admit some frivolity in taking the interior product
\iota
with a multi-vector
G_{4}^{\vee}
, some sort of dual of
G_{4}
.

Note a relation of
G_{7}
to the degree-eight
electric current form

j_{E}:=-2dG_{7}=G_{4}\wedge G_{4}
, which may be obtained by mimicking the Lie derivative of
-\mathcal{K}
in the direction
\mathcal{H}^{\vee}
:

\displaystyle``-\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{H}^{\vee}}\mathcal{K}=-\mathbb{L}_{G_{4}^{\vee}}\mathcal{K}\,"

\displaystyle=(d\iota_{G_{4}^{\vee}}+\iota_{G_{4}^{\vee}}d)C_{3}\wedge G_{4}\wedge G_{4}

\displaystyle=d\iota_{G_{4}^{\vee}}C_{3}\wedge G_{4}\wedge G_{4}

\displaystyle=-2dG_{7}

\displaystyle=j_{E}\;.

Serre duality.

In relation to Serre duality
-\mathcal{C}\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathcal{K}+\mathcal{C}
,
we have (cf.
[
INV02
]
without fields):
:

•

Electric-to-magnetic
: The transformation
\mathcal{H}\mapstochar\rightarrow-\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{H}
is
manifested universally by

g_{4}\longmapsto g_{4}g_{7}/g_{4}=g_{7}\;,

and at the level of field strengths by

G_{4}\longmapsto\iota_{G_{4}^{\vee}}(G_{4}\wedge G_{7})=G_{7}\;.

•

Magnetic-to-electric
: Likewise, the transformation
2\mathcal{H}\mapstochar\rightarrow-\mathcal{K}-2\mathcal{H}
is the adjoint
transformation

G_{7}\longmapsto\iota_{G_{7}^{\vee}}(G_{4}\wedge G_{7})=G_{4}\;.

3
Dimensionally reduced theories and ‘root data’ via iterated cyclic loop spaces

M-theory on
T^{k},k\leq 8
and higher cyclic loop spaces.

The reduction of the M-theory system on tori
T^{k}=(S^{1})^{k}
leads to a low-dimensional
system corresponding to duality-symmetric supergravity actions in
these dimensions matching the EOMs of M-theory
compactified on a
k
-dimensional torus
T^{k}
in an iterative way
[
SV21
]
.
This arises via the iterated cyclic loop spaces

\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
of the 4-sphere
S^{4}
, both for
0\leq k\leq 8
and for
k\geq 9
.
For
k=1
this is studied extensively in
[
FSS18a
]
[
FSS18b
]

in relation to T-duality and the twisted K-theory description of the fields
in type II string theory (cf.
section

4.2
). The generalization
is possible thanks to the general construction in
[
BMSS18
]
.

3.1
Type IIA in 10 dimensions and the reduction on
S^{1}

The reduction to type IIA.

The reduction from M-theory in 11 dimensions to type IIA string theory in 10 dimensions is
captured by looping. Such a process has been utilized topologically
at the level of bundles
leading to loop bundles in 10 dimensions starting from an
E_{8}
gauge bundle
(capturing the purely topological aspects of
G_{4}
) in 11 dimensions
[
MS03
]
.
In our current case, one is to do this at the level of universal spaces, and
taking into account the rotation of the circle. This leads to the concept of
a cyclic loop space or cyclification, advocated in
[
FSS18b
]

\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}:=\mathcal{L}S^{4}/\!\!/S^{1}.

The Sullivan minimal model of the cyclic loop space

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}Z)
in terms of the Sullivan minimal model
M(Z)

of a simply connected (or at least nilpotent) space
Z
is identified in
[
SV21
]
. For
Z=S^{4}
,
this gives a duality-symmetric reduction of fields
in M-theory on tori, extending and organizing partial/local results in the
supergravity and M-theory literature.

Starting with
M(S^{4})
in (
10
), the Sullivan minimal model
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})
of the cyclic loop
space of
S^{4}
is presented in
[
SV21
]
(cf.
[
FSS17
, Ex. 3.3]
[
FSS18b
, Ex. 2.7]
)

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})=\big(\mathbb{R}[f_{2},f_{4},f_{6},h_{3},h_{7}],\ df_{2}=0,\,dh_{3}=0,\,df_{4}=h_{3}f_{2},\,df_{6}=h_{3}f_{4},\,dh_{7}=-\tfrac{1}{2}{f_{4}}^{2}+f_{2}f_{6}\big).

Being defined for
\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}
, these equations are universal, and we obtain the corresponding ones in
spacetime by pulling back,
giving the datum of a closed 3-form
H_{3}
and of 2-, 4- and 6-forms
F_{2}
,
F_{4}
and
F_{6}
on
X
such that

dF_{2}=0;\qquad dF_{4}=H_{3}\wedge F_{2};\qquad dF_{6}=H_{3}\wedge F_{4},

together with a 7-form
H_{7}
, which is a potential for a certain
8-form:

dH_{7}=-\tfrac{1}{2}F_{4}\wedge F_{4}+F_{2}\wedge F_{6};

cf.
[
CW84
]
[
HN85
]
[
GP84
]
for the classical theory and
[
BNS04
]

for the duality-symmetric formulations of type IIA supergravity.

Scaling symmetries in type IIA.

In type IIA, one has two
\mathbb{R}^{+}
symmetries: one,
\alpha
, is inherited by dimensional reduction
from M-theory (see
section

2.4
) and similarly scales the Lagrangian, with weights

3
to both
F_{4}
and the once-reduced field
s_{1}F_{4}
,
the field strength of the NS B-field.
The second,
\beta
, leaves the Lagrangian invariant.
We are interested in the first symmetry, which arises from Kaluza-Klein dimensional reduction, where
all
p
-index potentials scale with a factor
\lambda^{p}
, while all scalar
fields are left invariant.
We will see the corresponding description for type IIB in
section

4.3
.

Type IIA and the cyclification
\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}
of the sphere
S^{4}
.

A general framework for describing the automorphisms of Sullivan
minimal models is given in
[
SV21
]
.
The maximal real split torus of
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})
compatible with the structure of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})
as the
Sullivan minimal model of the cyclic loop space of
S^{4}

can be canonically identified
with
\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
, acting on
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})=\mathbb{R}[g_{4},g_{7},sg_{4},sg_{7},w]
as follows:

t\cdot\phi_{i}=t^{\lambda_{i}}\phi_{i},\qquad t\in(\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{G}_{m})(\mathbb{R}).

Field

\phi_{i}

degree

weight

\lambda_{i}

g_{7}

7

2\epsilon_{0}

sg_{7}

6

2\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1}

g_{4}

4

\epsilon_{0}

sg_{4}

3

\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1}

w

2

\epsilon_{1}

(28)

Here
w
is the class corresponding to the circle (loop), and

S
corresponds to reduction (i.e., contraction).
This collection of fields
(g_{4},g_{7},sg_{4},sg_{7},w)
corresponds to a weight
decomposition

\displaystyle M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})_{\epsilon_{0}}\oplus M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})_{2\epsilon_{0}}\oplus M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})_{\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1}}\oplus M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})_{2\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1}}\oplus M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})_{\epsilon_{1}}=M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}).

Topological interpretation of toroidal symmetries coming from
S^{1}
.

As in the case for
k=0
, here we present an interpretation of the toroidal symmetries
(
28
) as rational (real) homotopy equivalences; see details in
[
SV21
]
.
These symmetries have physical interpretation of torus rescaling symmetries.
The situation with the action with weights
\epsilon_{i}
,
i>0
, in (
28
) is similar
but a little subtler than the case of
S^{4}
.
The
n
-fold winding maps
S^{1}\rightarrow S^{1}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, of the source
S^{1}
for the loop space
\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}
induce maps

\varphi_{1}(n):\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{\longmapsto}\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}

in the rational homotopy category.
These morphisms are invertible, and the compositions of them with their inverses, as in
Section

2.4
,
give a group homomorphism

\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})(\mathbb{Q})
, which defines naturally a morphism of
\mathbb{R}
-algebraic groups

\mathbb{G}_{m}\xrightarrow{\;\;\sim\;\;}\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})\;,

expressed through the contribution of
\epsilon_{1}
in (
28
).

We now consider the Lie algebra level as in the case of 11d.

The Lie algebra.

Here we use the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{1}=\Lie(T^{2})
of the maximal real split torus
T^{2}
of
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})
with its canonical factorization
T\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}
. This Lie algebra constitutes the
infinitesimal symmetries corresponding to the toroidal symmetries above
and acts on the
Sullivan minimal model
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})
by derivations.
That is, we have a Lie algebra homomorphism:

\mathfrak{h}_{1}\rightarrow\Der M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})

which comes from taking the differential of the action

T^{2}\rightarrow\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})

\textstyle{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\footnotesize\rm Group}\;\;T^{2}\ \rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\circlearrowright$}~~\;\;\framebox{$\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm differential\;of\;map}
\scriptstyle{\rm linearization}
\textstyle{\framebox{$\Der M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})$ }\;\;\;\ \rotatebox[origin]{-270.0}{$\circlearrowright$}~~\mathfrak{h}_{1}=\Lie(T^{2})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\rm Algebra}}

Under the action of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
on
M=M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})
,
the weight decomposition
becomes

M=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in P(\mathfrak{h}_{1})}M_{\alpha},

where
P(\mathfrak{h}_{1})\subseteq\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{*}=\Hom_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathbb{R})
is the

weight lattice
, the image of the character group

\mathfrak{X}(T)=\Hom_{\mathbb{R}}(T,\mathbb{G}_{m})\linebreak\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}

under the differential map

\mathfrak{X}(T)\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{*}

taking
\beta
to
d\beta
.

Bases for the Lie algebra of symmetries and its dual.

(i)

The
2
-dimensional real abelian Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{1}=\Lie(T^{2})\subseteq\Der M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})
of the maximal

\mathbb{R}
-split torus
T=T^{2}
of the algebraic group
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})
has a canonical basis
\{h_{0},h_{1}\}
.

(ii)

The weights
\epsilon_{0}
of
g_{4}
and
\epsilon_{1}
of
w_{1}

give a canonical basis
\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}\}
of the
vector space
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{*}
. This is also a basis of
the weight lattice
P(\mathfrak{h}_{1})\subseteq\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{*}
.

The dual lattices and inner product.

(i)

The lattice
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}}:=\mathbb{Z}h_{0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}h_{1}
is the dual of the weight lattice

P(\mathfrak{h}_{1})=\mathbb{Z}\epsilon_{0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\epsilon_{1}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{*}
.

(ii)

The nondegenerate bilinear form
\mathfrak{h}_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{h}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

determined by the isomorphism

\mathfrak{h}_{1}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{*}
, which maps

h_{i}
to
\epsilon_{i}
,
provides the vector space
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
with a canonical Minkowski inner
product
(-,-)
, of signature
(+1,-1)
, i.e., satisfying

(h_{0},h_{0})=1,\qquad(h_{1},h_{1})=-1\;.

(iii)

The inner product induced on the dual space
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{*}
is given by the formulas:

(\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0})=1,\qquad(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{1})=-1\;.

Overall, we have the following picture

\textstyle{\framebox{$\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\framebox{$\Der M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})$}}
\textstyle{\framebox{$\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}\cong T^{2}$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{\rm Lie\;group\;action}
\scriptstyle{\Hom_{\mathbb{R}}(-,\mathbb{G}_{m})}
\textstyle{\framebox{$\mathfrak{h}_{1}=\Lie(T^{2})=\mathbb{R}h_{0}\oplus\mathbb{R}h_{1}$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm Lie\;algebra\;action}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\Hom_{\mathbb{R}}(-,\mathbb{R})}
\scriptstyle{\rm isomorphism}
\textstyle{\supseteq\quad\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}h_{0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}h_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm linear\;dual}
\textstyle{\framebox{$\mathfrak{X}(T)=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\framebox{$\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{*}=\mathbb{R}\epsilon_{0}\oplus\mathbb{R}\epsilon_{1}$}}
\textstyle{\supseteq\quad P(\mathfrak{h}_{1})=\mathbb{Z}\epsilon_{0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\epsilon_{1}}

We can compute the action of
the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
on the other generators of the minimal DGCA

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{1}S^{4})
.

Action of the Lie algebra on the cyclic loop space in type IIA.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
acts on each weight space
M_{\alpha}
with the
weight
\alpha
:

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})_{\alpha}=\big\{\phi_{i}\in M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})\;|\;h\cdot\phi_{i}=\alpha(h)\phi_{i}\quad\text{for all $h\in\mathfrak{h}_{1}$}\big\}.

For the
\phi_{i}
running over the universal fields in (
28
), and for
h=\{h_{0},h_{1}\}
, we have

\displaystyle h\cdot g_{4}=\epsilon_{0}(h)g_{4},\quad h\cdot g_{7}=2\epsilon_{0}(h)g_{7},\quad h\cdot sg_{4}=(\epsilon_{0}(h)-\epsilon_{1}(h))sg_{4},

\displaystyle h\cdot sg_{7}=(2\epsilon_{0}(h)-\epsilon_{1}(h))sg_{7},\quad h\cdot w=\epsilon_{1}(h)w\,.

With the Minkowksi metric and with the dual bases
\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}\}
and
\{h_{0},h_{1}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}(h_{0})

\displaystyle=1,\qquad\epsilon_{0}(h_{1})=0,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}(h_{0})

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\epsilon_{1}(h_{1})=-1\,,

so that

(i)

For the action of
h_{0}
we have

\bullet

g_{4}
and
sg_{4}
have weight 1:

h_{0}\cdot g_{4}=g_{4}
,
h_{0}\cdot sg_{4}=sg_{4}

\bullet

g_{7}
and
sg_{7}
have weight 2:

h_{0}\cdot g_{7}=2g_{7}
,
h_{0}\cdot sg_{7}=2sg_{7}

(ii)

For the action of
h_{1}
we have

\bullet

sg_{4}
and
sg_{7}
have weight 1:

h_{1}\cdot sg_{4}=sg_{4}
,
h_{1}\cdot sg_{7}=sg_{7}

\bullet

w_{1}
has weight
-1
:

h_{1}\cdot w_{1}=-w_{1}
,

with all other actions having weight zero.

Del Pezzo surface
\mathbb{B}_{1}
.

This is obtained as the blowup of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
at a point

x_{1}
.
The Picard group
{\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{1})
in this case is a rank-
2
lattice with
two generators
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E}_{1}
:

H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{1};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}_{1}

where
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is the class of the exceptional divisor over
x_{1}
.
See
[
Ma74
]
[
De80
]
[
Be96
]
[
KSC04
]
.
This has a natural inner product
given by the intersection form:

\mathcal{H}\cdot\mathcal{H}=1,\quad\mathcal{H}\cdot\mathcal{E}_{1}=0,\quad\mathcal{E}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{E}_{1}=-1\,,

given by the Lorentzian form

Q={\rm diag}(1,-1)
.
The surface
\mathbb{B}_{1}
has the
canonical class

\mathcal{K}_{1}=-3\mathcal{H}+\mathcal{E}_{1}

as well as the
anticanonical class
:

-\mathcal{K}_{1}=3\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{1}\;,

which “acts” on the
Picard group
\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{1})\cong H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{1};\mathbb{Z})
by degree:

\deg\mathcal{D}:=-\mathcal{K}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{D},\qquad\mathcal{D}\in\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k}).

Here the “action” is understood as the intersection product
\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{1})\otimes\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{1})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.
The
degree
of
\mathbb{B}_{1}
is the self-intersection number
(-\mathcal{K}_{1})\cdot(-\mathcal{K}_{1})=8
.
The
degree of a divisor
\mathcal{D}\in\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{1})
is measured with respect to the anticanonical map as:

\deg\mathcal{D}:=-\mathcal{K}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{D}\;.

(29)

The brane/reduced M-theory gauge algebra via the Quillen model of the cyclification of
S^{4}
.

We have seen in
section

2.2
the description of the M-theory gauge algebra via Quillen model of
S^{4}

is just the graded Lie algebra on two generators in (
13
).
The gauge algebra of the reduced fields in
11-k
dimensions
will correspond to the Quillen model of
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
.
We will not work out the details, as it is clear that matching the fields and EOMs
of reduced M-theory with the generators and their differentials in the Sullivan minimal model of
the cyclification of
S^{4}

implies similar matching between the gauge algebra and the Quillen model.
Thus, the gauge algebra formulas in
[
CJLP98b
]
[
LLPS99
]
should be reproduced just by
looking at the Quillen model of
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
.

Degree in the Quillen model of
\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}
.

The maximal split torus
T^{2}
and its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
act on the
Quillen minimal model
Q(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})
with the same weights as on
the generators of the Sullivan minimal model
M=M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})
.
Here again the degree operator

x\mapstochar\rightarrow\lvert x\rvert\cdot x,\qquad x\in Q(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}),

in the Quillen minimal model singles out a distinguished element of the Lie
algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
.
Indeed
[
SV21
]
, there is a unique element of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
, namely,

-K_{1}:=3h_{0}-h_{1}
,
which acts on the Quillen minimal model
Q(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})
as the
degree operator:

-K_{1}\cdot x=\lvert x\rvert\cdot x\;.

Here again, we use the element
-K_{1}

as a distinguished element, the analogue of the anticanonical class.
Extending the analogy with del Pezzo surfaces, as in
[
SV21
]
,
we define the
degree of the cyclic loop space
\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}
of the four-sphere
as

\deg\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}=(-K_{1},-K_{1})=8.

(30)

Type
IIA
root system.

In contrast to the type IIB case, we will see later (see (
60
)),
for type IIA we have
(in the notation of
Table

1
):

E_{1}=R_{1}=\big\{x\in\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}}\;|\;(x,3h_{0}-h_{1})=0,\;(x,x)=-2\big\}=\varnothing=A_{0}\,.

(31)

While this might seem trivial, it is the zeroth step in an emerging pattern.
This justifies the type IIA row of
Table

1
.

3.2.1
Reduction to 9 dimensions

Reduction to 9d and the double cyclification
\mathcal{L}^{2}_{c}S^{4}
.

The process can be iterated to give the same result for

\mathcal{L}_{c}^{2}Z
, so that we can now continue with further dimensional reduction.
9
9

9

We will have multiple circle fiber directions and corresponding labels on
the contractions
s_{i}
and the classes of the circles
w_{i}
. As in
[
SV21
]
, we realize that
the notation is not fully in parallel with the convention of using such labels to indicate the degree, but choosing another notation such as
s_{(i)}
might overload the expressions when multiple such occur below. We hope the distinction
will be clear from the context.

Reduction on a 2-torus leads to the double cyclification

\mathcal{L}_{c}^{2}S^{4}

whose Sullivan minimal model

M(\mathcal{L}^{2}_{c}S^{4})

[
SV21
]

gives
equations that are again universal, and we again obtain the corresponding ones in
spacetime by pullback. These are the EOMs and Bianchi identities
of type II string theory
at low energy, i.e., type II supergravity in 9 dimensions in the
duality-symmetric formulation.
The classical EOMs are given in
[
BHO95
]
[
DR96
]
, so the result
(established in
[
SV21
]
) can be
viewed as a duality-symmetric extension.

The group of automorphisms.

The maximal
torus of
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}^{2}_{c}S^{4})
is identified canonically with the

\mathbb{G}_{m}^{3}=(\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{G}_{m})\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
, where the

\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
refers to the maximal torus of
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4})
identified in the above 10d case.
In this case, we have a weight

.

Iterated
S^{1}
vs. direct
T^{2}=S^{1}\times S^{1}
reduction.

We compare the two settings:

(i)
In the iterated case,
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{2}S^{4})
, notice the appearance of the
