s_{2}w_{1}
,
which would be absent in the direct reduction on
T^{2}

corresponding to the “toroidification”
M(\Map(T^{2},S^{4})/\!\!/T^{2})
.
See
eq.

38
and the discussion around it for further details.

(ii)
Note that
dw_{1}=s_{2}w_{1}\cdot w_{2}
above, whereas in the direct
T^{2}
-reduction, we would have
dw_{1}=dw_{2}=0
.
Likewise, note the two terms in the differential
ds_{2}g_{4}
above, while in the direct reduction, it
would be on an equal footing with
ds_{1}g_{4}
, that is,
ds_{2}g_{4}=s_{1}s_{2}g_{4}\cdot w_{1}
.

3.2.2
Reduction to 8 or lower dimensions

Reduction on a 3-torus and the triple cyclification

\mathcal{L}_{c}^{3}S^{4}
.

The Sullivan minimal model

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{3}S^{4})

worked out in
[
SV21
]
leads to equations are again universal, and we obtain the corresponding ones in
spacetime by pullback, and capture the equations of motion and Bianchi identities
of type II string theory
at low energy, i.e., type II supergravity in 8 dimensions in the
duality-symmetric formulation, extending, for instance,
[
AT85
]
.

Weights of fields for triple cyclification
\mathcal{L}^{3}_{c}S^{4}
.

Again the maximal torus of
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}^{3}_{c}S^{4})
splits canonically to
become
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{4}=(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{3})\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
, where the first factor
comes from the double cyclification. The resulting weight decomposition
of
M=M(\mathcal{L}^{3}_{c}S^{4})
repeats the formulas above verbatim
for the weights of those generators which are the generators of

M(\mathcal{L}^{2}_{c}S^{4})
. The weight of
w_{3}
is
\epsilon_{3}
. For the
weights of generators of the type
s_{3}g
, where
g
is a generator on
the above list, the formulas are the same, except
that weight
\epsilon_{3}
gets subtracted, e.g.,

\displaystyle s_{3}g_{4}\in M_{\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{3}},\qquad s_{3}w_{2}\in M_{\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}}.

The weight
\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}
will not be present, as

s_{3}s_{2}w_{1}
gets truncated to zero. See also
section

3.2.3
.

Reduction on
T^{k}
and

k
-fold cyclifications
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
for
k\geq 3
.

The above pattern
pertains in the reduction to 8 and lower dimensions
[
SV21
]
.
The pullbacks to spacetime of the differentials of the set of universal generators
correspond, likewise, to the EOMs and Bianchi identities of
duality-symmetric low energy string theory/supergravity in dimensions
11-k
.
Supplying these leads to a duality-symmetric extension of
the non-duality symmetric versions studied, e.g., in
[
LP96
]
[
LLP98
]
,
and surveyed in
[
CDF91
]
[
Ta98
]
.

Weights of fields for the
k
-fold cyclification
\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4}
,
k\geq 3
.

The general pattern continues for
k\geq 3
. All the weights of the generating space
V
for
S(V)=M(\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4})
will be of the form

\epsilon_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\epsilon_{i_{j}}
,

where
0\leq l\leq 3
and
1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{l}\leq k
,

2\epsilon_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\epsilon_{i_{j}}
,

where
0\leq l\leq 6
and
1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{l}\leq k
,

\epsilon_{i}
,

1\leq i\leq k
,

\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}
,

1\leq i<j\leq k
.

Each of the corresponding weight spaces in
V
will be
one-dimensional.

Here we generally describe the toroidal symmetry of the iterated cyclic loop spaces

\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4}
,
from
section

3
, for which
we provide topological interpretation, leading to root data.

Scaling symmetries in lower dimensions.

Upon reduction on
T^{k}
, the above rescaling
modifies the
\mathbb{R}
-part of
{\rm GL}(11-k,\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}\times{\rm SL}(11-k,\mathbb{R})
, which
becomes as a result an internal symmetry.
It can be traced back to the eleven-dimensional action, as above,
to appropriate multiplicative redefinitions of the 3-form.
The plain
\mathbb{R}
-symmetry rescales the volume of the compactifying space,
and must be combined with the trombone symmetry to define a new internal scaling symmetry,
which in 8 dimensions becomes
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})

[
CJLP98a
]
.
The symmetry is preserved under Kaluza-Klein dimensional reduction, after which
all
p
-index potentials scale with a factor
\lambda^{p}
, while all scalar
fields are left invariant.

The
\mathbb{R}^{q}
symmetry in the reduction on tori.

There is also a rescaling of the
k
-torus
T^{k}

with coordinates

y^{i}\mapstochar\rightarrow\lambda y^{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,k
,
as part of the eleven-dimensional diffeomorphisms.

(i)

This defines a scaling which leaves the scalars in

(11-k)
dimensions
invariant and
is part of the Cremmer-Julia group
embedded as
{\rm GL}(1)\subset{\rm GL}(k)\subset E_{k(k)}

[
DS09
]
.
Gauging this symmetry amounts to having
{\rm GL}(1)
charge
\mathfrak{q}
with

C_{3}\mapstochar\rightarrow e^{-3\mathfrak{q}}C_{3}
,
which is an alternative way of writing (
19
).
See
[
DS09
]
for details.

(ii)

The
\mathbb{R}^{q}
part of the symmetry comes from the local abelian gauge symmetry of

G_{4}
. Specifically, it describes the global shift symmetries of the axionic scalars
that are the potentials for 1-form field strengths coming from the dimensional reduction.

We will consider these matters from the point of view of topology below and algebraic geometry in

section

5
.

Toroidal symmetries of minimal algebras.

Here, generalizing the discussion in
section

2.4
(for
k=0
) and
section

3.1

(for
k=1
),
our
real split torus
, an affine algebraic group
T
isomorphic over

\mathbb{R}
to the group
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{k}
for some
k\geq 1
.
As we showed in
[
SV21
]
, we can consolidate essentially all toroidal symmetries of
M
, which
could be done by considering a
maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus

T\subseteq\Aut M
.
A maximal split torus will play a role similar to that of a maximal torus in the theory of compact Lie
groups or that of a Cartan subalgebra in the theory of complex
semisimple Lie algebras.
Indeed, a real split torus
T
gives rise to a

•

weight decomposition
:

M=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{X}(T)}M_{\alpha}

•

indexed by the
character group

\mathfrak{X}(T)=\Hom_{\mathbb{R}}(T,\mathbb{G}_{m})
of
real algebraic group homomorphisms from
T
to the multiplicative group

\mathbb{G}_{m}
,

•

so that
T
acts on each
weight space

M_{\alpha}
by
the character
\alpha
:

M_{\alpha}=\big\{m\in M\;|\;t\cdot m=\alpha(t)m\quad\text{for
all $t\in T$}\big\}.

Note that a weight decomposition of a minimal Sullivan algebra
M=S(V)

induces a weight decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{X}(T)}V_{\alpha}

of the generating space
V
.

We established the following result in
[
SV21
]
:

The maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus of the real algebraic group
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
for
k\geq 0
is
T^{k+1}
, isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{k+1}
over
\mathbb{R}
.
The structure of
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
as an iterated cyclification
determines a canonical splitting
T^{k+1}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}^{k+1}
.

Topological interpretation of toroidal symmetries coming from iterated
S^{1}
’s.

For the iterated cyclic loop space
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
,
k\geq 1
, the algebraic-group morphisms corresponding
to different iterations commute and thereby define an algebraic-group morphism

(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{k}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Aut M(\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4}),
which provides an action of
(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{k}
on the Sullivan minimal model of the
k
-fold cyclification
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}

of
S^{4}
.
Acting similarly to (
24
) through the target
S^{4}
in the cyclic loop space
\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4}
, we get an action

\mathbb{G}_{m}\times M(\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4})\rightarrow M(\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4})

by the above tables
through the weight
\epsilon_{0}
.

We now unravel the general pattern
E_{k}
symmetry of the iterated cyclic loop spaces

\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4}
.

The “Cartan subalgebra” and weight lattice.

Generalizing the cases
k=0
from
section

2.4
and
k=1
from
section

3.1
,
we consider
k\geq 2
and we use the toroidal symmetries of the cyclic loop
spaces
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
to build certain canonical data: “a lattice

N_{k}
with an inner product
(-,-)
and a distinguished element

K\in N_{k}
”, similar to the triple
(N_{k},(-,-),K)

in the theory of del Pezzo surfaces; see
section

3.5
. This will
produce the
E_{k}
root system in a similar way.

The idea
[
SV21
]
is to use the weight lattice coming from the weight
decompositions
and use the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}=\Lie(T^{k+1})
of the maximal real split torus
T^{k+1}
of
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k+1}S^{4})
with its canonical factorization
T\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{k+1}
.
This Lie algebra constitutes the
infinitesimal symmetries corresponding to the toroidal symmetries
and acts on the
Sullivan minimal model
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k+1}S^{4})
by derivations.
That is, we have a Lie algebra homomorphism:

\mathfrak{h}_{k}\rightarrow\Der M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
,
which comes from taking the differential of the action

T^{k+1}\rightarrow\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
.

\textstyle{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\footnotesize\rm Group}\;\;T^{k+1}\ \rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\circlearrowright$}~~\;\;\framebox{$\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})$}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rm differential\;of\;map}
\scriptstyle{\rm linearization}
\textstyle{\framebox{$\Der M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})$ }\;\;\;\ \rotatebox[origin]{-270.0}{$\circlearrowright$}~~\mathfrak{h}_{k}=\Lie(T^{k+1})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\rm Algebra}}

As we explained in
[
SV21
]
, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
is an avatar of the Cartan
subalgebra of the Lie algebra of type
E_{k}
of “hidden” symmetries
of the cyclic loop spaces of the four-sphere.
The cases
k=0
and
k=1
are the beginnings of a pattern, the general structure of which is
as follows:

Bases for the Lie algebra of symmetries and its dual.

(i)

The
(k+1)
-dimensional real abelian Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}=\Lie(T^{k+1})\subseteq\Der M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
of the maximal

\mathbb{R}
-split torus
T=T^{k+1}
of the algebraic group
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
has a canonical basis
\{h_{0},h_{1},\linebreak\dots,\linebreak h_{k}\}
.

(ii)

The weights
\epsilon_{0}
of
g_{4}
and
\epsilon_{i}
of
w_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
give a canonical basis
\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}\}
of the vector space
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
. This is also a basis of
the weight lattice
P(\mathfrak{h}_{k})\subseteq\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
.

As in the case for
k=0,1
above, we can compute the action of
the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
on the other generators of the minimal DGCA

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
.

The dual lattices and inner product.

Generalizing the previous special cases, we have:

(i)

The lattice
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{\mathbb{Z}}:=\mathbb{Z}h_{0}\oplus\mathbb{Z}h_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbb{Z}h_{k}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{k}
is the dual of the weight lattice

P(\mathfrak{h}_{k})=\mathbb{Z}\epsilon_{0}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbb{Z}\epsilon_{k}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
.

(ii)

The nondegenerate bilinear form
\mathfrak{h}_{k}\otimes\mathfrak{h}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

determined by the identification (isomorphism)

\mathfrak{h}_{k}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
, sending

h_{i}
to
\epsilon_{i}
,
provides the vector space
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
with a canonical Minkowski inner
product
(-,-)
. This inner product satisfies the formulas:

(h_{0},h_{0})=1,\qquad(h_{i},h_{j})=-\delta_{ij}\quad\text{ for \;
$i\geq 0$, $j\geq 1$.}

(iii)

The inner product induced on the dual space
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
is given by the formulas:

(\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0})=1,\qquad(\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{j})=-\delta_{ij}\quad\text{ for \;
$i\geq 0$, $j\geq 1$.}

The anticanonical class on del Pezzo.

Consider the
del Pezzo surface

\mathbb{B}_{k}

obtained as the blowup of

\mathbb{CP}^{2}
at
k
generic points

x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
,
0\leq k\leq 8
.
The Picard group

\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k})
is a rank-
(k+1)
lattice with generators
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{E}_{k}
:

H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{E}_{k},

where now

\mathcal{E}_{i}
is the class of the exceptional divisor over
x_{i}
.
See
[
Ma74
]
[
De80
]
[
Be96
]
[
KSC04
]
.
This has a natural inner product
given by the intersection form:

\mathcal{H}\cdot\mathcal{H}=1,\quad\mathcal{H}\cdot\mathcal{E}_{i}=0,\quad\mathcal{E}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{E}_{j}=-\delta_{ij},\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq k\;,

(32)

thus given by the Lorentzian form

Q={\rm diag}(1,-1,-1,\dots,-1)
.
Hence
H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}

and
\mathbb{B}_{k}
has Betti numbers
b_{2}^{+}=1
,
b_{2}^{-}=k
, with signature

\sigma=1-k
.

The
anticanonical class
:

-\mathcal{K}_{k}=3\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{1}-\dots-\mathcal{E}_{k},

(33)

in this general case “acts” on
\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k})\cong H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})
by degree:

\deg\mathcal{D}:=-\mathcal{K}_{k}\cdot\mathcal{D},\qquad\mathcal{D}\in\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k}).

Here the “action” is understood as the intersection product
\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k})\otimes\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. The
degree of a divisor

\mathcal{D}\in\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k})
is defined using the anticanonical morphism

f:\mathbb{B}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{d}
, and

d=h^{0}(\mathbb{B}_{k},-\mathcal{K}_{k})-1=(-\mathcal{K}_{k})\linebreak\cdot(-\mathcal{K}_{k})=9-k

is known as the
degree of the del Pezzo surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
.

The “anticanonical class” for
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
analogous to del Pezzo.

The maximal split torus
T^{k+1}
and its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
act on the
Quillen minimal model
Q(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
with the same weights as on
the generators of the Sullivan minimal model
M=M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}
.
Also in this general case, the degree operator

x\mapstochar\rightarrow\lvert x\rvert\cdot x,\qquad x\in Q(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}),

(34)

in the Quillen minimal model singles out a distinguished element of the Lie
algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
, namely
[
SV21
]

-K_{k}:=3h_{0}-h_{1}-\dots-h_{k},

(35)

which acts on the Quillen minimal model
Q(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
as the
degree operator:

-K_{k}\cdot x=\lvert x\rvert\cdot x.

Extending the analogy with del Pezzo surfaces,
We define the
degree of the cyclic loop space
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
of the four-sphere
as

\deg\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}=(-K_{k},-K_{k})=9-k.

(36)

Exceptional root data from the Sullivan model of cyclic loop spaces of the 4-sphere.

The following result summarizes one of the main constructions in
[
SV21
]

and establishes the entries in Table
1
:

(i)
For each
k\geq 0
,
the data

\big(\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*},\linebreak\{\epsilon_{o},\epsilon_{1},\linebreak\dots,\epsilon_{k}\},(-,-),K_{k}^{*}\big)

associated to the cyclic loop space

\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4}
and its Sullivan minimal model
M(\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4})
consists of

(a)

a real vector space
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
with a basis
\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}\}
, which generates a lattice
P(\mathfrak{h}_{k})\subset\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
;

(b)

a symmetric bilinear form
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

given by

(\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0})=1,\qquad(\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{j})=-\delta_{ij},\qquad i>0,j\geq 0;

(c)

a distinguished element
K_{k}^{*}=-3\epsilon_{0}+\epsilon_{1}+\dots+\epsilon_{k}
.

(ii)

This data replicates the data

\big(H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{R}),\{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{E}_{k}\},(-,-),\mathcal{K}_{k}\big)

determined by the rational surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
,
considered as the blowup of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
at
k
points; see

section

3.5
in the del Pezzo case, when
k\leq 8
. For
k\leq 8
, the data produces the

root system

R_{k}:=\big\{\gamma\in P(\mathfrak{h}_{k})\;|\;(\gamma,K_{k}^{*})=0,(\gamma,\gamma)=-2\big\}\subset(K_{k}^{*})^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}

(37)

of type
10
10

10

True/genuine
E_{k}
for
k=6,7
, and 8, and using the conventions of
Table

1

for
0\leq k\leq 5
.

E_{k}
and the Weyl group
W(E_{k})
, generated by the
reflections in the hyperplanes orthogonal to the
roots
r\in R_{k}
, now in the context of cyclic loop spaces of
S^{4}
.

3.2.3
The toroidification model

We have adopted the iterated cyclification model
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
so far,
providing here and in
[
SV21
]
a proper setting for matching of the fields and their dynamics.
For comparison and completeness,
we will now briefly consider a variant model, which has some clear virtues in
capturing the description of M-theory configurations/fields, but which
will also naturally come with its own limitations.

Instead of reducing M-theory in 11 dimensions to
a theory in
11-k
dimensions on

k
iterations of the circle
S^{1}
,
this model is obtained by viewing the reduction as directly over the
k
-torus
T^{k}=S^{1}\times\dots\times S^{1}
.
From a physics point of view, the former highlights how one goes to the low-dimensional
theory by going through all intermediate theories,
while the former goes directly to
(a variant of) that theory, skipping the intermediate stages.
The toroidification of the sphere
S^{4}
is given by

\mathbb{T}^{k}(S^{4}):={\rm Map}(T^{k},S^{4})/\!\!/T^{k}

(38)

and follows the general construction for any Lie group as source
given in
[
BMSS18
, Theorem 2.44 and (28)]

(the Ext/Cyc-adjunction). Of course the toroidification model coincides with the cyclification model
when
k=1
, and so the difference arises when dimensionally reducing to nine dimensions or below.

Virtues.

The toroidification model puts the source circles on an equal footing and allows for the symmetric group
\Sigma_{k}
action
on the minimal model
M(\mathbb{T}^{k}(S^{4}))
(and related spacetime fields), rather than just on the weight lattice, cf. (
49
).
From the point of view of del Pezzo surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
,
this corresponds to exchanging the points at which blowups take place.
From our perspective, this corresponds to the exchange of source loops, arising from
the symmetry of the torus, in the toroidification model.
This model will also be prominent in
[
SV22
]
.

Limitations.

In the toroidification model, the generators

s_{j}w_{i}
and the corresponding fields
s_{j}\omega_{i}
are not detected, while all the

w_{i}
’s will be closed:
dw_{i}=0
.
The fields
s_{j}\omega_{i}
are
axions
, i.e., 1-form field strengths (corresponding to 0-form potentials). The missing axions
s_{j}\omega_{i}

arise from the form-field

F_{2}
, the latter corresponding to the universal element
w_{2}
.
However, we will still find axions
arising from the reduction of the
C
-field, i.e., axions of type

s_{3}s_{2}s_{1}G_{4}
, in the toroidification model.
The fact that the
w_{i}
’s are closed reflects the fact that,
in the toroidification perspective,
there is no room for twisting which takes place in
the iterated cyclification.
Thereby, we will continue adopting our cyclification model.

Scalars.

In deriving the above, we needed an assumption that amounts to setting the scalars to zero
[
SV21
]
. Such scalars are
treated from a physics perspective as being coordinates on the
real moduli spaces
E_{k(+k)}/K
, where
K
is the maximal compact subgroup.
Such spaces are contractible hyperbolic spaces with no nontrivial
homotopy, hence perhaps not
that interesting from the rational homotopy theory point of view.
From this angle, the scalars might seem orthogonal to the way
we are probing the structure and
symmetries. However, particularly the axionic scalars have made a prominent
appearance here and will further appear in an interesting fashion in

section

5.1
.

3.3
Reduced electric-magnetic duality via topology and fields

We have seen above, in
section

2.5
,
the interpretation of the anticanonical class from
the perspective of 11-dimensional M-theory for
k=0
. Now we consider the
dimensional reduction of that perspective on electric-magnetic duality.

Reduced M-theory fields and branes.

In our perspective, we consider the fields that couple to the
M-branes. Our interpretation of the classes is given in the fourth column of the following table (for
3\leq k\leq 8
):

Degree

del Pezzo class

BPS state

Form field

Element of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})

1

\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{i}-\mathcal{E}_{j}-\mathcal{E}_{l}

thrice-wrapped M2

s_{l}s_{j}s_{i}G_{4}

s_{l}s_{j}s_{i}g_{4}

2

\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{i}-\mathcal{E}_{j}

twice-wrapped M2

s_{j}s_{i}G_{4}

s_{j}s_{i}g_{4}

3

\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{i}

once-wrapped M2

s_{i}G_{4}

s_{i}g_{4}

7-k

2\mathcal{H}-\sum_{p=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{p}

k
-wrapped M5

s_{k}\cdots s_{1}G_{7}

s_{k}\cdots s_{1}g_{7}

where, as before, for form fields,
s_{i}
corresponds to reduction (i.e., contraction)
along the
i
th circle direction (in the notation of
section

3
).

The reduced anticanonical class via the Sullivan model.

The canonical class (or divisor)
\mathcal{K}_{k}
of the
del Pezzo surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
is given by
the negative first Chern class
c_{1}(\mathbb{B}_{k})
of the tangent bundle:

\mathcal{K}_{k}=-\Big(3\mathcal{H}-\sum_{p=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{p}\Big)\;.

(39)

This has self-intersection number
\mathcal{K}_{k}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{k}=9-k
. Imposing positivity
leads to
k<9
. The same story happens with the “canonical class”
K_{k}
of the
k
-fold cyclification of
S^{4}
, see
Section

3.2.2
.
These generally correspond to the dimensional reduction of the description of M-theory above in
section

2.5
.
For instance, for
\mathbb{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}
, with
corresponding (see
section

3.1
)
\mathcal{K}_{1}=-3\mathcal{H}+\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
K_{1}=-3h_{0}+h_{1}
, respectively, we have

\displaystyle s_{1}(G_{4}\wedge G_{7})

\displaystyle=H_{3}\wedge H_{7}+F_{4}\wedge F_{6}\;,

\displaystyle s_{1}(g_{4}\wedge g_{7})

\displaystyle=s_{1}g_{4}\cdot g_{7}+g_{4}\cdot s_{1}g_{7}\;.

For form-fields, it seems common in the physics literature to leave only the first term
H_{3}\wedge H_{7}
and work modulo
F_{4}
and
F_{6}
,
which actually makes
H_{3}
and
H_{7}
closed.
This gives rise to electric-magnetic pairs, as expected in type IIA.

The terms for higher
k
are analogous and an obvious pattern emerges,
with the other terms being the various dimensional reductions of the
cubic (Chern-Simons) term from eleven dimensions, along the lines of
[
MS03
]
,
and these terms are of the form

s_{k}\cdots s_{1}(G_{4}\wedge G_{7})
.
These give topological terms familiar in the dimensionally-reduced theories.

Note that the analogous statements about electric-magnetic/Serre duality hold
for the reduced forms using
\mathcal{K}_{k}
in place of
\mathcal{K}
and the reduced classes
in the above table in place of the classes
\mathcal{H}
and
2\mathcal{H}
. To a large extent,
the results in
section

3
can be viewed as an illustration of this at the level of EOMs,
and the reconstruction of the terms in the corresponding actions is straightforward.

3.4
The
E_{k}
root system and its Weyl group

We have seen the appearance of the Weyl group
W
in obtaining exceptional root data from

\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
(see the end of
section

3.2.2
).
We now explain further the role of
W
, starting with a description that is close to our perspective
that involves tori and mapping degrees.

The Weyl group via torus action.

For a connected compact Lie group
G
with
T\subset G
its maximal torus,
a maximal connected abelian group, the normalizer
\mathcal{N}(T)
of
T
fits into a short
exact sequence

1\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}(T)\xrightarrow{\;\;\;p\;\;\;}W\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 1\;.

(40)

Since
T
is abelian,
W
acts on
T
by conjugation, i.e., if
\sigma\in W
and

t\in T
then
\sigma
sends
t
to
ntn^{-1}
, where
n\in p^{-1}(\sigma)
.
Note that for such a
G
is determined up to isomorphism by
\mathcal{N}(T)
; see
[
Os92
]
[
No95
]
for the general case,

[
CWW74
]
for semisimple case, and
[
AH17
]
for reductive algebraic groups.
From the short exact sequence (
40
),

\mathcal{N}(T)
is in turn determined up to isomorphism by three ingredients:

(i)

the torus
T
,

(ii)

the finite group
W
, with its conjugation action on
T
, and

(iii)

an extension class
\lambda\in H^{2}(W;T)
, the degree-2 group cohomology of
W
, i.e., of the classifying space
BW
.

For (i) and (ii):
T=(S^{1})^{k}
for
k
the rank of
G
. The natural map

\Aut(T)\rightarrow\Aut(\pi_{1}T)\cong{\rm GL}(k,\mathbb{Z})
is
an isomorphism and resulting conjugation homomorphism
W\rightarrow{\rm GL}(k,\mathbb{Z})

embeds
W
as a finite subgroup of
{\rm GL}(k,\mathbb{Z})

generated by reflections. A
reflection
here is a matrix which is conjugate in

\Aut(\pi_{1}T\otimes\mathbb{Q})\cong{\rm GL}(k,\mathbb{Q})
to the diagonal matrix

{\rm diag}(-1,1,\cdots,1)
.
For (iii) a characterization is given in
[
DW05
]
.
Note that the above realizes the Weyl group itself as a quotient of a torus action, namely

W=\mathcal{N}(T)/T
.
It is natural to identify the maximal torus with the spacetime torus
T^{k}
in the
dimensional reduction. Note also that
E_{k}/T^{k}
probes the nonabelian parts of
E_{k}
, as in
the discussion around (
1
).

Meaning of reflections via covering.

The relation between Weyl group actions and reflections in Euclidean spaces is as follows.
Let
\sigma\in W
be a reflection. This means that if
\sigma:T\rightarrow T
is lifted to the covering space

\tilde{\sigma}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}\;,\qquad k={\rm rank}(G)

then
\tilde{\sigma}
is a reflection in an
(k-1)
-hyperplane
H
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
Let
T^{\prime}=p^{-1}(\sigma)
in (
40
). Since
\sigma^{2}=1=p(T)
, we see that
if
x\in T^{\prime}
then
x^{2}\in T
.

Roots and Weyl via circle mapping degree.

In connecting to the perspective in
section

2.4
,
section

3.1
,
and
section

3.2
,
it is interesting that in classical Lie theory one can interpret certain classical
notions via degrees.
Let
\phi:G\rightarrow G
be the squaring map
\phi(x)=x^{2}
. Then

Q:=\{t\in T~|~x\,t\,x^{-1}=t^{-1}\;\text{for}\;x\in T^{\prime}\}\subset T

is topologically a disjoint finite set of circles. The identity component

Q^{0}
is a circle group. Then one can define
[
CW75
]
the root and coroot
associated with a reflection
w
to be a homomorphism

\theta:T\rightarrow S^{1}
and the homomorphic inclusion
\overline{\theta}:Q^{0}\rightarrow T
, respectively.
Let
\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{r}
be
reflections generating
W
.
For each pair
\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}
, one has a map

S^{1}\overset{\overline{\theta}_{i}}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}T\overset{\theta_{j}}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}S^{1}

and denote the degree of the composite

\theta_{j}\circ\overline{\theta}_{i}
by
d_{ij}
.
Furthermore, for each
\sigma_{i}
associated is the degree
d_{i}
of
[\overline{\theta}_{i}]

in
\pi_{1}(G)
which is either 1 or 2.
Then for each such pair of reflections,
the integers
C_{ij}=d_{i}d_{ij}
are called the
Cartan integers

of
G
and coincide with the usual definition
[
CW75
]

C_{ij}=2\frac{\langle\theta_{i},\theta_{j}\rangle}{\langle\theta_{i},\theta_{i}\rangle}

where
\langle-,-\rangle
is the (
W
-invariant) inner product on the dual Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

Note that, classically, the determinant of the Cartan matrix gives the index of the root lattice
inside the lattice of integral weights. We can relate to del Pezzo as follows:
For
G=E_{k}
,
3\leq k\leq 8
, the determinant of the Cartan matrix is (see
[
Wu82
]
)

\det(C)=9-k
, which is the degree of
\mathbb{B}_{k}
.

Weyl group as symmetry of symmetries.

For each root
x
, an element of the set
R_{k}
(see expression (
37
))
define a reflection

\sigma_{x}:y\;\;\longmapsto\;\;y-2\frac{(y,x)}{(x,x)}x=y+(y,x)x,\qquad y\in\mathfrak{h}_{k},

(41)

of the vector space
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
. As above, the reflections
\{\sigma_{x}\;|\;x\in R_{k}\}
generate the Weyl group
W(E_{k})
of the root system
E_{k}
. It is the group of all linear isometries of
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{\mathbb{Z}}
which preserve the element
K_{k}

[
Ma74
]
.
Thus, the Weyl group, being an isometry group of the abelian Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
of infinitesimal
symmetries of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
, is a “second derived” object with respect to

\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
:
W(E_{k})
is the group of “symmetries of symmetries” of
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
.
This is typical for the role of Weyl groups in Lie theory: a Lie group is usually a group of
symmetries of a certain mathematical object, the Cartan subalgebra is the maximal abelian Lie algebra of infinitesimal symmetries of that object. The Weyl group is a group of symmetries of the Cartan subalgebra.
In this sense, we can write (
40
) as

T^{k}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}(T^{k})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Aut(T^{k})\,.

Geometric aspects of the moduli space of
k
-fold cyclifications of
S^{4}
.

As in
[
SV21
]
, we can interpret an element
\omega
of the linear dual space
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}\cong\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
of the abelian Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
of infinitesimal symmetries of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
as an extra, geometric ingredient complementing the purely topological data carried by the real homotopy type of the
k
-fold cyclification
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
. Indeed, an element
\omega\in\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
is a
weight
, and as such, it tells us which “spectral parameters” we might want to assign to the basic infinitesimal symmetries
h_{0},h_{1},\dots,h_{k}
.

•

For instance, recall from
Section

2.4
that
h_{0}
comes from the action of the real 1-torus
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
by automorphisms of the real homotopy type of
S^{4}
and the resulting action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
on
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
. That action originates ultimately from the folding self-maps of
S^{4}
. The value
\omega(h_{0})\in\mathbb{R}
tells us how much we shall value the effect of the folding self-maps of
S^{4}
. In this sense,
\omega(h_{0})
is akin to the size of
S^{4}
, such as its radius
R_{0}
, or rather the logarithm
\log R_{0}
thereof, since
\omega(h_{0})
is not necessarily positive. This value
\omega(h_{0})
is analogous to the logarithmic Planck scale
\log\ell_{p}
in the 11-dimensional supergravity and the generalized Kähler volume
\omega(H)=\int_{H}\omega
of the line
H=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
in
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
and its image in
\mathbb{B}_{k}
in the del Pezzo story; cf.
[
INV02
, §3.1]
.

•

Similarly, from
section

3.2
, the element
h_{i}
for each
i
,
1\leq i\leq k
, comes from the action of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
by the folding self-maps of the
i
th source circle of the cyclic loop space
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
. Assigning
h_{i}
a real value
\omega(h_{i})
tells us how much we shall value the effect of the folding self-maps of the
i
th source circle of
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
. In this way,
\omega(h_{i})
is analogous to the logarithm
\log R_{i}
of the radius
R_{i}
of the
i
th source circle and the
i
th compactification circle in M-theory on
T^{k}=(S^{1})^{k}
, as well as the generalized Kähler volume
\omega(\mathcal{E}_{i})
of the exceptional curve
\mathcal{E}_{i}
in
\mathbb{B}_{k}
in the del Pezzo story; cf.
[
INV02
, §3.1]
again.

In this sense, the choice of a weight
\omega\in\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
adds a certain ingredient of
metric
flavor, missing in the real homotopy model
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
of
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
. For example, an arbitrary weight
\omega\in\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
will not be a real homotopy invariant of
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
. The values
\omega(h_{i})
,
i=0,1,\dots,k
, that is to say, the logarithmic radii of the target sphere
S^{4}
and the source circles
S^{1}
, may be thought of as the coordinates of the weight
\omega
in the space of all weights.

As in (
1
), we will provide a concrete description of the picture:

{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\text{Nonabelian \; moduli \; space}}\quad\framebox{$K\backslash G/G(\mathbb{Z})$}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.96327pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\text{\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65} abelianize}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 57.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\framebox{$\mathcal{M}_{k}=A/W$}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}\text{Abelian \; moduli \; space }}

The Weyl group
W(E_{k})
, being the group of symmetries of the
E_{k}
data
(\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{\mathbb{Z}},(-,-),K_{k})
, acts on the linear dual
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
.
Thus,
it makes sense to identify the weights
\omega
brought together by this action.
We call the corresponding quotient orbifold the
moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{k}
of
k
-fold cyclifications of
S^{4}
:

\mathcal{M}_{k}=\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathcal{L}^{k}S^{4}):=[\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}/W(E_{k})].

This is a stacky quotient
[\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}/W(E_{k})]
, which is different from the naive, topological quotient

\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}/W(E_{k})
. Another reincarnation of the quotient orbifold
[\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}/W(E_{k})]
is the familiar homotopy
quotient
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}/\!\!/W(E_{k})
, which may be realized via the Borel construction,
but
in this context, the orbifold viewpoint would be more common. The
(k+1)
-dimensional topological quotient

\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}/W(E_{k})
contains the
k
-dimensional quotient
K_{k}^{\perp}/W(E_{k})
, which for
k\leq 8
may be
identified with the closure of a
Weyl chamber

for the Weyl group action in the Euclidean space
K_{k}^{\perp}=\{\omega\in\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}\;|\;\omega(K_{k})=0\}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
, cf.
[
Hal15
, Prop. 8.29]
.

Another motivation for the form
\mathcal{M}_{k}=A/W
of moduli space may come through the Chevalley Restriction Theorem
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}/\!\!/G_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}/\!\!/W
, where
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}/\!\!/G_{\mathbb{C}}
is, by definition, the spectrum

\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}]^{G_{\mathbb{C}}})
of the algebra of polynomial functions on
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
which are invariant under the adjoint

G_{\mathbb{C}}
-action and
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}/\!\!/W:=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}]^{W})
. The spaces
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}/\!\!/G_{\mathbb{C}}
and
K\backslash G/G(\mathbb{Z})

cannot be isomorphic, as
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}/\!\!/G_{\mathbb{C}}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}/\!\!/W=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{C}}=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{h}

while
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}K\backslash G/G(\mathbb{Z})=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}A+\dim_{\mathbb{R}}N=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{h}+\dim_{\mathbb{R}}N
, but should nevertheless be related.

Further interpretation.

We highlight the following:

(i)
Identifying the weights
\omega
under a permutation of the radii of the circles entering

\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
is similar to identifying punctured Riemann surfaces under a permutation of the punctures,
if we wish to consider the moduli space of Riemann surfaces with unlabeled punctures.
The identification of weights under the Weyl group action is also analogous to identifying the Planck scale
and the sequence of radii of the circle components in compactified M-theory under U-duality.
In the del Pezzo story, one also identifies generalized Kähler classes on a del Pezzo surface under
the action of the Weyl group, see
[
INV02
, §3.1]
, and the fundamental Weyl chamber in the Picard
group of a del Pezzo surface plays a prominent role in studying Cremona isometries
[
Do12
, §8.2.8]
.

(ii)

The Weyl group as a symmetry:

There is an action by the group
W
on the group
{\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{k})

of divisor classes of
\mathbb{B}_{k}

that fixes
\mathcal{K}_{k}
and preserves
the intersection product of
{\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{k})
.
For
\mathcal{E}=\{\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E}_{0},\cdots,\mathcal{E}_{k}\}
an exceptional configuration on

\mathbb{B}_{k}
, any other configuration

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is a
W
-translate of
\mathcal{E}
and, if
\mathbb{B}_{k}
is a blowup of

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
at generic points, every
W
-translate of
\mathcal{E}
is an
exceptional configuration
[
Na61
]
.
This can be refined further
[
Har85
]
:
For
\sigma\in W
associated to
\mathcal{E}
, we have that

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\sigma\mathcal{H},\cdots,\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\prime}=\sigma\mathcal{E}_{k}\}

is an exceptional configuration on
\mathbb{B}_{k}
if and only
if
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is numerically effective and

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{\prime}-\mathcal{E}_{j}^{\prime}
is not effective for
i>j>0

[
Har85
]
, that is, if we keep the signs of the
intersection products intact.

(iii)

Ultimately, physical configurations have to be U-duality invariant, so
it makes sense to mod out by that symmetry.
The Weyl group
W=W(E_{k})
is traditionally taken as a subgroup
of the discrete
\mathbb{Z}
-form of the U-duality group
E_{k}(\mathbb{R})
;
see the discussion at the end of
section

3.5
.
And since
W
already contains a substantial part of that symmetry,
modding out by
W
makes sense and is also ‘close’ to the
ultimate moduli space, in the sense of (
1
).

3.5
Automorphisms of del Pezzo surfaces, roots systems, and Weyl groups

The connection between algebraic geometry and Lie theory comes from the fact that the Cartan matrices of the exceptional Lie algebras of type
E_{k}
and their
root systems arise from the above data: a lattice with a distinguished element and inner product
(see
[
Ma74
]
).
In general, even for a fixed
k
, the surfaces
\mathbb{B}_{k}
obtained from varying the blowup points are not isomorphic as complex manifolds. However, they are diffeomorphic, so these surfaces give rise to the same combinatorial data, and we will just speak of “the” del Pezzo surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
for each
k
.
We have extracted analogous data from topology of the fields
in
section

2.4
,
section

3.1
,
and
section

3.2
.

The data of the triple

For
3\leq k\leq 8
, which is assumed throughout this subsection, there exists
in the lattice

N_{k}:={\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{k})=H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})

a free basis
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{E}_{k})
such that
[
Ma74
]
for

\mathcal{K}_{k}:=-3\mathcal{H}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{i},

(42)

the scalar product on
\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}N_{k}\cong\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
defined by
(
32
)
induces on the orthogonal complement
\mathcal{K}_{k}^{\perp}
of
\mathcal{K}_{k}
the structure
of a Euclidean vector space (with a negative-definite form) of dimension
k
.
Hence the triple

\big\{{\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{k}),\,\mathcal{K}_{k},\,\text{intersection form}\big\}\,

up to isomorphism, depends only on
k
. The triple (
N_{k}
,
\mathcal{K}_{k}
,
(-,-)
), consisting of a lattice with an integral inner product and a distinguished element of inner square
9-k
, carries rich enough combinatorics to give rise to a root system.

Special subsets.

The following three special subsets of
N_{k}
are of interest
(for
3\leq k\leq 8
):

(1)

Root system:

The set

R_{k}:=\left\{l\in N_{k}\;|\;(l,\mathcal{K}_{k})=0,(l,l)=-2\right\}

(43)

is a
rank
k
root system in

\mathcal{K}_{k}^{\perp}\cong\mathbb{R}^{k}
of type

A_{1}\times A_{2},\,A_{4},\,D_{5},\,E_{6},\,E_{7},\,E_{8},

respectively.

(2)

Root lattice:
The root lattice of type
E_{k}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-span
\mathbb{E}_{k}
of
R_{k}
; here we also have
\mathbb{E}_{k}=\mathcal{K}_{k}^{\perp}\cap N_{k}
.

(3)

Exceptional vectors:

I_{k}:=\left\{l\in N_{k}\;|\;(l,\mathcal{K}_{k})=(l,l)=-1\right\}\;.

(44)

We will identify these groups later (see
section

5.2
).

(4)

Simple roots:

One can choose the simple roots
of
E_{k},3\leq k\leq 8,
as

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}:=\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{1}-\mathcal{E}_{2}-\mathcal{E}_{3}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\alpha_{i}:=\mathcal{E}_{i}-\mathcal{E}_{i+1},~~i=1,\ldots,k-1.

(45)

Their negative intersection numbers yield the
E_{k}

Cartan matrix
C(E_{k})
. See
section

6.1
for the case
k\geq 9
.

Special groups.

With the subsets above, the following groups coincide:

(a)

The group of automorphisms of the lattice
N_{k}
preserving
\mathcal{K}_{k}

and the scalar product
(-,-)
;

(b)

The group of permutations of the vectors from
I_{k}
preserving
their pairwise scalar products;

(c)

As in (
41
), the Weyl group
W_{k}=W(R_{k})
of the system
R_{k}
generated by the
reflections with respect to the roots.
Any vector
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}
with
\alpha^{2}=-2
determines a reflection

\sigma:\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}
by
y\mapstochar\rightarrow y+(y,\alpha)\alpha
.
The Weyl group
W_{k}\subset\operatorname{O}(\mathbb{Z}^{1,k})

is the group generated by
the reflections
\sigma_{i}

through the
simple roots
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k-1}
.

Note that this approach
differs from the usual treatment of considering

\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Using
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
as above is more convenient combinatorially
and allows for the system of exceptional vectors
I_{k}
.

The Weyl group and the root lattice.

The set of roots
R_{k}=\bigcup_{i}W_{k}\alpha_{i}
of
E_{k}
is the union of
W_{k}
-orbits of the simple
roots
\alpha_{0},\cdots,\alpha_{k-1}
, where the latter form an integral basis
for the root lattice

\mathbb{E}_{k}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}\;.

This root lattice is a lattice of rank
k
, invariant under the action of
W_{k}
, and is

•

negative-definite
for
k\leq 8
,

•

negative semi-definite for
k=9
,

•

and of signature

(1,k-1)
for
k\geq 10
.

Markings.

Let
\pi:\mathbb{B}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a rational surface presented as
the blowup of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
at
k
points
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
in general position.
Let
\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}
denote the free abelian group
\mathbb{Z}^{k+1}
with the standard basis

\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}\}

and the Minkowski
inner product

(\vec{x},\,\vec{y})=\vec{x}\cdot\vec{y}=x_{0}y_{0}-x_{1}y_{1}-\cdots-x_{k}y_{k}\;

(46)

for
\vec{x}=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
and
\vec{y}=(y_{0},y_{1},\dots,y_{k})
.
There is a canonical
marking
isomorphism

\displaystyle\phi:\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})={\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{k})

(47)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{E}_{i},\;\;i=1,\dots,k.

We call the pair
(\mathbb{B}_{k},\phi)
a
marked blowup
. Additionally, the canonical class (
42
) of
\mathbb{B}_{k}
is given by

\mathcal{K}_{k}=\phi({\bf k}_{k})\;

the image of the special vector

{\bf k}_{k}=(-3,1,\dots,1)=-3\epsilon_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\epsilon_{i}
.

Degree of del Pezzo surfaces.

A del Pezzo surface is (equivalently)
a smooth birationally trivial surface
S
on which the
anticanonical sheaf
-\mathcal{K}

is ample. The number
d=(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K})=\mathcal{K}.\,\mathcal{K}
is
called the degree of
S
; cf. (
29
).
In general,
1\leq d\leq 9
. For degree
d\geq 3
, it coincides with the
degree of
the projective embedding

i:S\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{d}

defined by

-\mathcal{K}
, so as
-\mathcal{K}=i^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{CP}^{d}}(1)
.
One has the following characteristics
of a del Pezzo surface
S
of degree
d

(see
[
Ma74
]
[
Do12
]
):

(i)

S
is isomorphic to the blowup
\mathbb{B}_{k}
of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
at
k=9-d
points as above;

(ii)

Every irreducible curve with a negative self-intersection number on

S
is exceptional;

(iii)

If
S
has no exceptional curves, then either
d=9
and
S
is isomorphic
to
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, or
d=8
and
S
is isomorphic to

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Automorphisms (of cohomology) of del Pezzo surfaces.

The marking (
47
) sends the Minkowski inner product
(
46
) to the intersection
pairing on
H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}\times\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi\times\phi}
\scriptstyle{(-,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}}

under which we get the relations (
32
).
The marking determines the birational morphism
\pi:\mathbb{B}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}

up to post-composition with an automorphism of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

Cremona transformation.

In slight generalization of (
45
),
the Cremona involution
\kappa_{ijl}\in\operatorname{O}(\mathbb{E}_{k})
is given by reflection in the vector

\alpha_{ijl}=\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{l}
for distinct indices
i,j,l\geq 1
.
It acts by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}\mapstochar\rightarrow 2\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{l},\quad\epsilon_{i}\mapstochar\rightarrow\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{l},\quad\epsilon_{j}\mapstochar\rightarrow\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{l},

\displaystyle\epsilon_{l}\mapstochar\rightarrow\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j},\qquad\quad\;\epsilon_{m}\mapstochar\rightarrow\epsilon_{m}\quad\text{if}\;m\not\in\{0,i,j,l\}.

The standard quadratic Cremona transformation of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
at the points

[1,0,0]
,
[0,1,0]
,
[0,0,1]

determines an
automorphism of
\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}
, which is the
reflection
\sigma_{0}=\kappa_{123}
through the hyperplane orthogonal to

\alpha_{0}=\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{1}-\mathcal{E}_{2}-\mathcal{E}_{3}
.
Switching points
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
acts on
\mathbb{Z}^{k+1}

by the reflection
\sigma_{i}
through the hyperplane orthogonal to
\alpha_{i}=\mathcal{E}_{i}-\mathcal{E}_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
.
The Cremona action on
{\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{k})=\mathbb{Z}^{k+1}
fixes the anticanonical class

-\mathcal{K}_{k}
.

\Aut(\mathbb{B}_{k})
vs.
W(E_{k})
.

Now suppose there are two birational morphisms

\pi,\pi^{\prime}:\mathbb{B}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, exhibiting
\mathbb{B}_{k}
as the blowup of

\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
at the points
x_{i}
and
x_{i}^{\prime}
, respectively,
i=1,\cdots,k
.
Then there is a birational map
f
making the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{B}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}_{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}}

commute, and the corresponding markings are related by
\phi^{\prime}=\phi\circ\sigma
for a
unique
\sigma\in\operatorname{O}(\mathbb{Z}^{1,k})
. The different blowups are related by the action of
the Weyl group
[
Na60
, p. 283]
[
DO89
, Thm. 2, p. 90]
:
that is,

\sigma\in W_{k}
.
Hence, if
(\mathbb{B}_{k},\phi)
is a marked blowup, and
\sigma\in\operatorname{O}(\mathbb{Z}^{1,k})

such that
(\mathbb{B}_{k},\phi\circ\sigma)
is also a marked blowup, then

\sigma\in W_{k}
. With

W(\mathbb{B}_{k},\phi)=\{\sigma\in W_{k}~|~(\mathbb{B}_{k},\phi\circ\sigma)\;\text{is a marked blowup}\}

(48)

denoting the subgroup of
W_{k}
which is realized by isomorphisms of marked blowup structures on
\mathbb{B}_{k}

[
Mc07
, Thm 5.2]
:

(i)
The right action of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{k}
simply reorders the basepoints
of a blowup, so
\Sigma_{k}\subset W(\mathbb{B}_{k},\phi)
;

(ii)
The Cremona involution
\kappa_{123}
, see above,
is in
W(\mathbb{B}_{k},\phi)
.

Weyl group action and the form fields.

In
[
INV02
]
, the dualities are restricted to compactifications with the
“rectangular, no
C
-field” condition. The full duality group acting on
such compactifications is isomorphic to the Weyl group
of

E_{k}

[
BFM99
]
[
OP99
]
and is generated by:

(i)

The symmetric group
\Sigma_{k}
permuting the
k

radii; this group is generated by the
k-1
exchanges
[
INV02
, (48)]

\sigma_{i}:R_{i}\longleftrightarrow R_{i+1},\;\;i=1,\dots,k-1.

(49)

(ii)

The action of T-duality (M2/M5 exchange, after
T^{3}

compactification) which, when expressed in M-theory language, involves three radii,
requiring one extra generator when
k\geq 3
, namely
[
INV02
, (49]

\hskip-14.22636pt\sigma_{0}:2\pi R_{1}\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{\ell_{p}^{3}}{2\pi R_{2}2\pi R_{3}}\;,\quad 2\pi R_{2}\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{\ell^{3}_{p}}{2\pi R_{1}2\pi R_{3}}\;,\quad 2\pi R_{3}\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{\ell^{3}_{p}}{2\pi R_{1}2\pi R_{2}}\;,\quad\ell_{p}^{3}\mapstochar\rightarrow\frac{\ell^{6}_{p}}{2\pi R_{1}2\pi R_{2}2\pi R_{3}}\;.

(50)

At the level of roots
, we have, respectively,

\alpha_{i}\longleftrightarrow\alpha_{i+1}

and

\alpha_{1}\longmapsto\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}\;,\quad\alpha_{2}\longmapsto\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{3}\;,\quad\alpha_{3}\longmapsto\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\;,\quad\alpha_{0}\longmapsto 2\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}\;.

At the level of fields
, these correspond, respectively, to the following:

(i)

The first, (
49
),
is also related to the toroidification (see
section

3.2.3
),
in which the classes of the circle bundles are on an equal footing and may be exchanged

\omega_{i}\longleftrightarrow\omega_{i+1},\;\;i=1,\dots,k-1;

(ii)

The second, (
50
), is captured by
2-form and 4-form degree-preserving
transformations
exchanging the Euler classes of the circle bundles with the 2-contractions of
G_{4}

and exchanging
G_{4}
with the 3-contraction of
G_{7}
:

\omega_{1}\longmapsto s_{2}s_{3}G_{4}\;,\quad\omega_{2}\longmapsto s_{1}s_{3}G_{4}\;,\quad\omega_{3}\longmapsto s_{1}s_{2}G_{4}\;,\quad G_{4}\longmapsto s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}G_{7}\;.

(51)

Here
s_{i}
corresponds to reduction (i.e., contraction)
along the
i
th circle direction (in the notation of

section

3.1
extended to
section

3.2.3
).

Clarification of the relation between
W(G)
and
G
.

Can we always realize
W
as a subgroup of
G
?

This is not possible in general:
W
may rather be realized as a subquotient
W=\mathcal{N}(T)/T
, where
\mathcal{N}(T)\subset G
is the normalizer of
T
in
G
.
However, in the physics literature,
W
seems to be treated as
a subgroup of the discrete form of
G
; e.g. in
[
INV02
]
:
The non-obvious part of the U-duality group is given by the
Weyl group, hence it is natural to mod out by the action

W(E_{k})
. In the following example, we consider the setting relevant for type IIB and the reduced theory in 9 dimensions.

Example 3.1

(Weyl group of
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
)
.

Note that already for rank 1, one has

W({\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C}))\not\subset{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
.
The Weyl group has order 2, but the only element of order 2 in the normalizer
\mathcal{N}(T)
is
\footnotesize\begin{pmatrix}-1&\;0\\
\;\;0&-1\end{pmatrix}\in T
,
which is trivial in
W=\mathcal{N}(T)/T
;
any representative
\footnotesize\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\
-1/a&0\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{N}(T)
of a nontrivial element of
W
has order 4.

In fact, for a simply connected group, this is essentially the only obstruction:
The Weyl group can always be lifted (uniquely up to conjugation) to a group
\widetilde{W}

which is an extension of
W
by an elementary abelian 2-group of order
2k
,
where
k
is the rank of
G

[
Ti66
]
.

However, the right question to ask about the relation between
W(G)
and
G(\mathbb{Z})
might not be
through the former being a subgroup of the latter, but rather through

conjugation action
,
which explains the appearance of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{k}=A/W
in
[
INV02
]
(see (
1
) in the Introduction). The U-duality group of 11d supergravity reduced on a
k
-torus is a connected semisimple real form
G
of type
E_{k}
.
With the Iwasawa decomposition
G=KAN
, the more standard moduli space
K\backslash G/G(\mathbb{Z})
of 11d supergravity reduced on a
k
-torus may be identified with
(AN)/G(\mathbb{Z})
. If we ignore the topologically trivial unipotent factor
N
, as explained in the Introduction, we turn the right action of
G(\mathbb{Z})
into a conjugation action of the normalizer
\mathcal{N}(A)
(or its integral form) of
A
on
A
. Since
A
is abelian, its conjugation action on itself is trivial, so the normalizer action on
A
reduces to an action of the Weyl group
W=\mathcal{N}(A)/A
.

4
Type IIB: description and compatibility checks

We have seen explicit connections to M-theory in

section

2
and to its dimensional reductions in
section

3
,
including type IIA in
section

3.1
. Now we consider the type IIB case
and provide interrelations to the above.

4.1
Type IIB and S-duality

We start by applying the discussion in
section

2.3.2
to type IIB string theory.

{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
and type IIB in nine dimensions.

{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
has two real forms:
\theta^{s}
and
\theta^{c}

(see
[
AdC09
, Ex. 12.20]
).

(i)

Real forms via anti-holomorphic involutions:

Up to conjugacy, there are two antiholomorphic involutions of

{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
:
\sigma^{s}(g)=\overline{g}
or
\sigma^{c}(g)={}^{t}\overline{g}^{-1}
.
These are the two real forms: the split
G(\mathbb{R})={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
the compact
G(\mathbb{R})={\rm SU}(2)
.

(ii)

Real forms via holomorphic involutions:

Equivalently,
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
has two equivalence classes of holomorphic
involutions.

(a)

Let
\theta^{s}(g)=tgt^{-1}
where
t={\rm diag}(i,-i)
. Then
the maximal compact subgroup is

K(\mathbb{C})=G^{\theta^{s}}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, so
K(\mathbb{R})=S^{1}
, the maximal
compact subgroup of the corresponding real form
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})={\rm SU}(1,1)
.

(b)

Let
\theta^{c}(g)=g
, i.e.,
\theta^{c}=1
, so

K(\mathbb{C})=G^{\theta^{c}}={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, hence
K(\mathbb{R})={\rm SU}(2)
,
and the corresponding real form is
{\rm SU}(2)
.

(iii)

Normalizer:

Let
H=\{{\rm diag}(z,1/z)|z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\}
,
W=+\{1,s\}
,

t={\rm diag}(i,-i)
, and
n
be any element of
N_{G}(H)
mapping to
s\in W
.
Then
N_{G}(H)=H\cup Hn
.

(iv)

Cartan subgroups:
As in
section

2.3.2
,

{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
has two conjugacy classes of Cartan subgroups. The compact
Cartan subgroup
H(\mathbb{R})=T\simeq S^{1}
corresponds to
1\in W
, and the split
Cartan subgroup
H(\mathbb{R})=A\simeq\mathbb{R}^{\times}
corresponds to
s\in W
.

We will describe S-duality in type IIB in a fashion that is compatible with the
description using the 4-sphere for M-theory in
section

2.1
and with the perspective of modding out by complex conjugation in

section

2.3
, building on the constructions in
[
Ku74
]
[
La82
]
[
HH11
]
.

The involution
(x,y)\mapstochar\rightarrow(y,x)
exchanging the two factors on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\cong S^{2}\times S^{2}
has as its fixed point set
the diagonal
\Delta=\Delta(\mathbb{C}P^{1})
consisting of all pairs

\{x,x\}
and is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
It acts as the antipodal map on the anti-diagonal (see below), and the quotient is
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
We interpret this as corresponding to S-duality.

Type IIB with S-duality

M-theory with no S-duality

\mathbb{Z}_{2}\;\;\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\circlearrowright$}~~\;(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})

(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}

This is described by the symmetric square:
{\rm Sp}^{2}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}):=(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. It is the orbit space under the action of the
cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
generated by the involution
\iota
that interchanges the two factors.
Its elements are the unordered pairs
\{x,y\}
for
x,y\in\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The fixed point of

\iota
determines the diagonal subspace
\Delta=\Delta(\mathbb{C}P^{1})
.

The diagonal and self-dual points.

From a physics perspective,
the diagonal
\Delta
represents the elements fixed under the duality, i.e., the self-duality points.
We interpret these as corresponding to the S-duality invariant combinations of field strengths, such as the
combination

G_{3}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\rm Im}\tau}}(F_{3}-\tau H_{3})

or the modified field strength

\widetilde{F}_{5}=F_{5}+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon_{ij}B^{i}\wedge H^{j}\;,\;i\in\{1,2\}
,
where
H^{i}=(H_{3},F_{3})
is an
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
doublet of fields
and
\tau
is the complex parameter formed of the axion and the dilaton (e.g., as in
[
GSVY90
]
).

The passage from type IIB to M-theory via
S^{4}
.

We have seen the effect of S-duality
given by interchanging the
factors
(S^{2}\times S^{2})/(x,y)\sim(y,x)
.
We now combine this with the other involution
given by complex conjugation
\sigma
, equivalent to
[(x,y)]\mapstochar\rightarrow[(-x,-y)]
.
Consider the sequence

S^{2}\times S^{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow(S^{2}\times S^{2})/J={\rm Sp}^{2}(S^{2})=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow(S^{2}\times S^{2})/L=S^{4}

where we have the pair of collections of involutions

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\{{\rm Id},(x,y)\mapstochar\rightarrow(y,x)\}\;,

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\{{\rm Id},(x,y)\mapstochar\rightarrow(y,x),(x,y)\mapstochar\rightarrow(-x,-y),(x,y)\mapstochar\rightarrow(-y,-x)\}\;,

with the action of
L/J=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
being equivalent to complex conjugation
\sigma
.
We see that only products
xy
survive, forming invariant combinations. Overall:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 19.19443pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.19443pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 241.70834pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 271.70834pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 301.70834pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S^{4}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Alternatively, in terms of complex coordinates:
Consider the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}

generated by the involution
(z,w)\mapstochar\rightarrow(w,z)
. It has the diagonal

\Delta=\{(z,z)|z\in\mathbb{C}\}

as the fixed
point set, and it acts as the antipodal map on the anti-diagonal

\overline{\Delta}=\{(z,-\frac{1}{\overline{z}})|z\in\mathbb{C}\}
.
Next, consider the combined
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action
on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
given by

f(z,w)=(w,z)\qquad\text{and}\qquad g(z,w)=\Big(-\frac{1}{\overline{z}},-\frac{1}{\overline{w}}\Big),

where
g
is the antipode
\times
antipode on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Modding out by this action gives the 4-sphere

(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong S^{4}.

Therefore, we see that the S-duality symmetric description of type IIB
is not only compatible with the
4-sphere description of M-theory, but admits an explicit relation and a natural transition to it (cf.
section

2.3
).

4.2
Type IIB and T-duality

We have worked out the Sullivan minimal model for type IIB in
[
SV21
]
.
In terms of
S^{4}
, the expected equivalence in nine dimensions
would amount to the equivalence of Sullivan models

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)\cong M(\mathcal{L}^{2}_{c}S^{4})

and a real homotopy equivalence at the level of spaces

\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB\sim\mathcal{L}^{2}_{c}S^{4}.

The relation between the various models is as appropriate from the physics picture

\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}_{c}^{2}}
\textstyle{IIB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}_{c}}
\textstyle{IIA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}_{c}}
\textstyle{9d}

and can be considered as a topological and physical analogue of the relations on the del Pezzo side

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{b^{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}_{2}}

where
b
denotes the process of blowing up (rather than the blowup map, which would go in the opposite direction).

The Sullivan minimal model
M(IIB)
of the real homotopy type
IIB
.

Consider the free graded commutative algebra
\mathbb{R}[\omega_{1},h_{3},\omega_{3},\omega_{5},h_{7},\omega_{7}]
, and define a differential by the following equations:

\displaystyle d\omega_{1}

\displaystyle=0,\;\;\;\;\qquad\qquad\quad dh_{3}=0,

\displaystyle d\omega_{3}

\displaystyle=h_{3}\omega_{1},\qquad\qquad\;\;d\omega_{5}=h_{3}\omega_{3},

(52)

\displaystyle dh_{7}

\displaystyle=\omega_{3}\omega_{5}+\omega_{7}\omega_{1},\quad d\omega_{7}=h_{3}\omega_{5}.

Pulling back these universal differential forms to spacetime,
we get the EOMs of
D=10
type IIB supergravity
in the duality-symmetric formulation,
and without imposing self-duality;
cf.
[
SW83
]
[
HW84
]
[
Sch83
]
for the classical formulation
and
[
DLS97
]
[
DLT98
]

for the duality-symmetric formulation.
Note that we do not include fields of degree greater than seven,
as in the dual picture of type IIA (see
section

3.1
),
this would require parametrized homotopy theory
[
BMSS18
]
.
Additionally, there are several subtleties in type IIB which makes a purely topological
perspective delicate due to the mixing between geometry and topology,
in the sense that some of the fields arise from the metric, as we explain
further below.

Unstable T-duality: comparing
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)
and
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)
.

In
[
SV21
]
, we computed
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)
, using the recipe of
section

3.1
.
With

\displaystyle c_{2}:=sh_{3},\qquad\omega_{2}:=s\omega_{3},\qquad\tilde{c}_{2}:=w,\quad\omega_{4}:=s\omega_{5},\qquad\omega_{6}:=s\omega_{7},

we have the following for type IIB reduced to 9 dimensions:

\displaystyle M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)=\left(\mathbb{R}[\omega_{1},c_{2},\tilde{c}_{2},\omega_{2},h_{3},\omega_{3},\omega_{4},\omega_{5},sh_{7},\omega_{6},h_{7},\omega_{7}],d\right)

\displaystyle d\omega_{1}=0,\qquad dc_{2}=0,\qquad d\tilde{c}_{2}=0,

\displaystyle d\omega_{2}=-c_{2}\cdot\omega_{1},\quad dh_{3}=c_{2}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2},\quad d\omega_{3}=h_{3}\cdot\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2},

\displaystyle d\omega_{4}=-c_{2}\cdot\omega_{3}+h_{3}\cdot\omega_{2},\qquad d\omega_{5}=h_{3}\cdot\omega_{3}+\omega_{4}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2},

(53)

\displaystyle dsh_{7}=-\omega_{2}\cdot\omega_{5}+\omega_{3}\cdot\omega_{4}-\omega_{6}\cdot\omega_{1},\quad d\omega_{6}=-c_{2}\cdot\omega_{5}+h_{3}\cdot\omega_{4},

\displaystyle dh_{7}=\omega_{3}\cdot\omega_{5}+\omega_{7}\cdot\omega_{1}+sh_{7}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2},\quad d\omega_{7}=h_{3}\cdot\omega_{5}+\omega_{6}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2}\,.

Similarly, for type IIA reduced to 9 dimensions we have the model

\displaystyle M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)=\left(\mathbb{R}[\omega_{1},c_{2},\tilde{c}_{2},\omega_{2},h_{3},\omega_{3},\omega_{4},\omega_{5},sh_{7},\omega_{6},h_{7}],d\right),

\displaystyle d\omega_{1}=0,\qquad dc_{2}=0,\qquad d\tilde{c}_{2}=0,

\displaystyle d\omega_{2}=\omega_{1}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2},\quad dh_{3}=c_{2}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2},\quad d\omega_{3}=-c_{2}\cdot\omega_{2}+h_{3}\cdot\omega_{1},

\displaystyle d\omega_{4}=h_{3}\cdot\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2},\qquad d\omega_{5}=-c_{2}\cdot\omega_{4}+h_{3}\cdot\omega_{3},

(54)

\displaystyle dsh_{7}=\omega_{3}\cdot\omega_{4}-\omega_{5}\cdot\omega_{2}-\omega_{6}\cdot\omega_{1},\quad d\omega_{6}=h_{3}\cdot\omega_{4}+\omega_{5}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2},

\displaystyle dh_{7}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{4}^{2}+\omega_{6}\cdot\omega_{2}+sh_{7}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2}\,.

Matching in 9 dimensions.

We compare
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)
with
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)
.
Up to replacement

c_{2}\leftrightarrow-\tilde{c}_{2},

all the equations for the differential of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)
in (
54
) exactly match those of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)
in (
53
), except for the following mismatch for the generators of degree 7:

Model

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)

Generators

h_{7}

h_{7}
,
\omega_{7}

Differentials

dh_{7}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{4}^{2}+\omega_{6}\cdot\omega_{2}+sh_{7}\cdot\tilde{c}_{2}

dh_{7}=\omega_{3}\cdot\omega_{5}+\omega_{7}\cdot\omega_{1}-sh_{7}\cdot c_{2}
,

d\omega_{7}=h_{3}\cdot\omega_{5}-\omega_{6}\cdot c_{2}

With relations imposed algebraically, this means
that

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)/(h_{7},dh_{7})\cong M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)/(h_{7},\omega_{7},dh_{7},d\omega_{7})\,,

(55)

that is to say, the two DGCAs,
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)
and
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)
, are isomorphic modulo the differential ideals generated by the generators of degree 7. Perhaps, topologically more interesting is an isomorphism between the dg-subalgebras
M_{A}\subset M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)
and
M_{B}\subset M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)
generated by all the generators except those of degree 7:

\textstyle{{}_{\phantom{A}{}_{\phantom{A}}}{M_{A}}\;\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\;\;\;M_{B}{}_{\phantom{A}{}_{\phantom{A}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)}
\textstyle{M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)\,.}

(56)

These dg-subalgebras are minimal Sullivan, and their isomorphism morally corresponds to the rational equivalence over
\mathbb{R}
of quotient spaces of
\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA
and
\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB
.

Subtleties in comparing type IIB with type IIA.

The situation above requires a discussion but it is not desperate,
due to the several subtleties that
arise in type IIB, which once addressed properly will remove the mismatch.
We will address these in detail below, but a summary is the following:

(i)
Classical supergravity fields and their dynamics are
accounted for, hence we certainly have a clean picture at that level.

(ii)
The dual fields are subtle to include from the topological point of view. In M-theory the only such field is supplied by
S^{4}
, while in type IIA they are
supplied by the cyclification

\mathcal{L}_{c}S^{4}
. The dual fields beyond rank 6 in type IIA
require parametrized homotopy theory

[
BMSS18
]
; something analogous would be needed in type IIB.

(iii)
In the quotients (
55
), the right-hand-sides
of the expressions are zero,
as relations, i.e.,
the terms are zero as (linear combinations of) generators.

(iv)
The real reason for the mismatch is that, somewhat unexpectedly and contrary to the treatment of type IIA, one ultimately needs the metric
g
.

Physically (and modulo the above subtleties), the above reincarnation of T-duality reconfirms the validity of the basic fields and EOMs
in
d=10
type IIB supergravity, as dictated by the real homotopy type
IIB
; see (
52
). It also reconfirms the basic fields and equations in the 9d reduction of type IIB supergravity for fields of degree 1 through 6, given by the real homotopy type
\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB
, see (
53
). At the same time, it calls for reassessment of the nature of higher-degree fields in
d=9
type II supergravity.
In order to resolve this subtlety, we will dig into the origin and dynamics of the fields in nine dimensions.

The
d=9
fields and their type II origin.

Maximal
d=9
supergravity
[
GN86
]
arises either from the
compactification of
d=11
supergravity on a 2-torus or of type IIB supergravity
on a circle, hence coincides with the dimensionally reduced version of both
the IIA and the IIB supergravity theories in 10 dimensions
[
BHO95
]
[
KK97
]
,
by virtue of T-duality
[
DLP89
]
[
DHS89
]
.
The form-field content in
d=9
, i.e., the collection of
potentials
(A_{1},A_{1}^{m},B_{2}^{m},\linebreak C_{3})
,
is comprised of three 1-forms
A_{1}
and
A_{1}^{m}
, two 2-forms
B_{2}^{m}
,
and one 3-form
C_{3}
, where
m=1,2
:

d=9

M-theory interpretation

IIB interpretation

A_{1}

s_{2}s_{1}C_{3}

s_{2}^{\prime}g_{{}_{\rm IIB}}

A_{1}^{m}

s_{1}g_{{}_{M}}
,
s_{2}g_{{}_{M}}

s_{1}^{\prime}B_{2}^{m}

C_{3}

C_{3}

s_{1}^{\prime}C_{4}

B_{2}^{m}

s_{1}C_{3}
,
s_{2}C_{3}

B_{2}^{m}

Here
g_{M}
and
g_{IIB}
are the metrics in 11 dimensions and in type IIB, respectively.
So there is a singlet, spanned by
A_{1}
, and a doublet, spanned by
A_{1}^{m}
,
m=1,2
, with respect to
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
The former arises from the reduction of the
metric
in type IIB
and hence couples to the massive Kaluza-Klein states, giving them
charge. The reduction along the
i
th direction from the type IIB side is denoted by
s^{\prime}_{i}
in contrast with that on the type IIA side, denoted thus far by
s_{i}
.

Self-duality.

The compactification of
F_{5}

in type IIB supergravity is delicate.
It is self-dual and gives rise to a duality condition for the massive fields in
d=9
.
Hence
F_{5}
and
F_{4}=s_{1}^{\prime}F_{5}
will be dual, so that using either
leads to equivalent formulations of the theory.

Reduction of cubic term.

Type IIA and type IIB are equivalent
in
d=9
by T-duality.
The
B
-field
B_{2}=s_{1}C_{3}
in type IIA reduces on the further circle
to the 1-form
A_{1}=s_{2}B_{2}
, which measures the winding charge
with respect to the circle. Under T-duality, this becomes the Kaluza-Klein
1-form, which arises as the reduction of the
10d metric in type IIB, i.e., as
s_{2}^{\prime}g_{\rm IIB}
. Then one gets a term

A_{1}\wedge F_{4}\wedge F_{4}
in
d=9
(see
[
DM97
]
).
Hence this is geometric.
Indeed, this is supported by the Lagrangian
[
FOT11
, (2.9)]

as well as the EOMs
[
FOT11
, (2.14)]
in the 9-dimensional reduction of type IIB supergravity:

11
11

11

The fields and corresponding EOMs involve shifts, as is common in supergravity – see also footnote
3
. We have freed the fields and separated into two types of fields, one primed and another unprimed.

dF_{7}=-\tfrac{1}{2}F_{4}\wedge F_{4}+F_{2}\wedge F_{6}+F_{2}^{\prime}\wedge F_{6}^{\prime}.

Hence the equation, which was missing in the real homotopy model of
\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB
, is not
purely topological, but in fact arises from the metric. Therefore,
a description solely via rational homotopy theory would not
encapsulate this (however, cf. the discussion in
section

3.4
in regards to the moduli space of cyclifications).

Stable T-duality.

If we agree to abandon the field
h_{7}
and at the same time run the fields
\omega_{n}
to unbounded degree
n
, we may wish to consider the following “stable” models of types IIA and IIB, which turn out to be in T-duality (
57
), in a way more perfect than (
55
) and (
56
):

\displaystyle M(IIA_{\operatorname{st}})

\displaystyle:=\big(\mathbb{R}[\{\omega_{2p},h_{3}\}_{p=1,2,\dots}]\;,\,d\omega_{2}=0,~dh_{3}=0,~d\omega_{2p+2}=h_{3}\cdot\omega_{2p}\text{ for $p\geq 1$}\big)\,,

\displaystyle M(IIB_{\operatorname{st}})

\displaystyle:=\big(\mathbb{R}[\{\omega_{2p+1},h_{3}\}_{p=0,1,\dots}]\;,\,d\omega_{1}=0,~dh_{3}=0,~d\omega_{2p+1}=h_{3}\cdot\omega_{2p-1}\text{ for $p\geq 1$}\big)\,.

As shown in
[
FSS18a
]
, their cyclifications are naturally isomorphic

M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA_{\operatorname{st}})\cong M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB_{\operatorname{st}}),

(57)

which may be viewed as a stable version (spectra-fication) of T-duality.

4.3
Toroidal symmetries of type IIB

Del Pezzo for type IIB.

In addition to the sequence
\mathbb{B}_{k}
,
k=0,\cdots,8
, connecting to the dimensional reduction of M-theory
on
T^{k}
, there is also an “outlier” del Pezzo surface
\mathbb{B}_{1}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}

of degree 8
with Picard group

\Pic(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1})\cong H^{2}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\mathfrak{l}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\mathfrak{l}_{2}\;,

where
\mathfrak{l}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{l}_{2}
are the classes of the two
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
factors. The intersection pairing works as follows (cf. (
32
)):

\mathfrak{l}_{1}\cdot\mathfrak{l}_{1}=\mathfrak{l}_{2}\cdot\mathfrak{l}_{2}=0,\quad\mathfrak{l}_{1}\cdot\mathfrak{l}_{2}=1\;.

(58)

The anticanonical class is given by
-\mathcal{K}_{\mathbb{B}_{1}^{\prime}}=2\mathfrak{l}_{1}+2\mathfrak{l}_{2}
.
This surface is related to the del Pezzo surfaces
\mathbb{B}_{k}
by a single blowup: if we blow up a point in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we will obtain a surface isomorphic to
\mathbb{B}_{2}
.

Scaling symmetries in type IIB.

The same rules highlighted in
section

3.2.2
– and already implemented for type IIA in
section

3.1
–
apply to the trombone symmetry of the type IIB supergravity theory.
In type IIB, there are also two scaling symmetries. The first corresponds to
one of three one-parameter subgroups of
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, namely
{\rm SO}(1,1)^{+}

with hyperbolic conjugacy classes. The second is an
\mathbb{R}^{+}
symmetry
(which is different from the
\mathbb{R}^{+}
subgroup of
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
corresponding to
parabolic conjugacy classes).
With respect to the first, the fields
(H_{3},F_{3},F_{5})
transform with weights

(2,2,4)
, while with respect to the second they transform as
(1,-1,0)
;
see
[
BdWGLR02
]
. It is manifest that these two symmetries leave the EOMs invariant.
The two groups
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
\mathbb{R}^{+}
combine to form the type IIB classical
global symmetry
{\rm GL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Discrete versions are further studied in
[
CMS15
]
.

Matching of symmetries in 9d:

From a
d=11
perspective, the diffeomorphism invariance of the
2-torus
T^{2}
gives rise to a global
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{2}

symmetry, where the first factor
corresponds to the modular parameters of the torus, while the second captures the
rescalings of the torus, descending from
\alpha
and
\beta
.
Both
\mathbb{R}^{+}
factors leave invariant the EOMs.
From a type IIB perspective,
the
{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
symmetry already exists
as S-duality (strong-weak coupling self-duality, as in
section

4.1
),
while there are two symmetries
descending from type IIB: an
\mathbb{R}^{+}
symmetry
\delta

corresponds to the rescalings of the circle
and an
{\rm SO}(1,1)^{+}\subset{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
symmetry
\gamma
.
The four symmetries are linearly dependent. Under
\alpha

and
\delta
, the potentials
\big(A_{1},A_{1}^{(1)},A_{1}^{(2)},B_{2}^{(1)},B_{2}^{(2)},C_{3}\big)

transform as
(3,0,0,3,3,3)
and
(0,2,2,2,2,4)
, respectively
[
BdWGLR02
, Table 4]
.
For the dual magnetic potentials, one has that

\big(A_{6},A_{6}^{(1)},A_{6}^{(2)},B_{5}^{(1)},B_{5}^{(2)},C_{4}\big)

transform as
(6,9,9,6,6,6)
and
(8,6,6,6,6,4)
, respectively

[
FOT11
, Table 3]
. While we will not be using all these symmetries explicitly, we nevertheless
hope they are useful in clarifying the context and providing comparisons.

Toroidal symmetries in type IIB.

Since the type IIB model (see
section

4.2
) falls out of the previous sequence of cyclifications of
S^{4}
,
we need to treat it separately. Indeed, in
[
SV21
]
we worked with the “unstable” model

M(IIB)=(S(V),d)
of type IIB;
see (
52
).
To identify a maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus
T^{B}
of
\Aut M(IIB)
, we need to start with

\mathbb{R}\omega_{1}\oplus\mathbb{R}h_{3}
in the notation of the system (
52
).
Thus, a maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus is 2-dimensional and could be identified as the group of diagonal matrices in the
basis
\{\omega_{1},h_{3}\}
. Given that a homogeneous basis of

\mathbb{R}\omega_{1}\oplus\mathbb{R}h_{3}

is unique up to scalar
multiplication, a maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus
T^{B}
here is unique.

Denote by
\beta_{0}
the weight of
h_{3}
and by
\beta_{1}
the weight of
\omega_{1}
, so that
we have the action

t\cdot h_{3}=t^{\beta_{0}}h_{3},\qquad t\cdot\omega_{1}=t^{\beta_{1}}\omega_{1},\qquad\qquad t\in T^{B}.

Then, with (
52
), we have the following
[
SV21
]
:

\displaystyle t\cdot\omega_{\,3}=t^{\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}}\omega_{\,3},\qquad t\cdot\omega_{\,5}=t^{2\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}}\omega_{\,5},

(59)

\displaystyle t\cdot h_{7}=t^{3\beta_{0}+2\beta_{1}}h_{7},\qquad t\cdot\omega_{\,7}=t^{3\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}}\omega_{\,7}.

The exponents on the right-hand sides of these equations are exactly the weights appearing in the weight
decomposition of the generators of
M(IIB)
.

The maximal real split torus can be canonically identified
with
\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
, acting on
M(IIB)
as follows:

t\cdot\phi_{i}=t^{\lambda_{i}}\phi_{i},\qquad t\in(\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{G}_{m})(\mathbb{R}).

Using the standard notation as in the case of the M-theory sequence, we have the
following table

Field

\phi_{i}

degree

weight

\lambda_{i}

h_{7}

7

3\epsilon_{0}-2\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{1}

\omega_{7}

7

3\epsilon_{0}-2\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}

\omega_{5}

5

2\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}

\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1}

\omega_{3}
