\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}

We notice from the table that the doublet exchanges
h_{7}\leftrightarrow\omega_{7}

and
h_{3}\leftrightarrow\omega_{3}
as well as the self-duality
\omega_{5}\leftrightarrow\omega_{5}

are witnessed by the exchange of weights
\epsilon_{1}\leftrightarrow\epsilon_{2}
, corresponding
to the exchange
s_{1}\leftrightarrow s_{2}
of the torus directions. Once pulled back from the universal space
to spacetime fields, this exhibits a manifestation of the duality relations

H_{7}\leftrightarrow F_{7}\;,\qquad H_{3}\leftrightarrow F_{3}\;,\qquad F_{5}\leftrightarrow F_{5}

for the doublets
(H_{7},F_{7})
,
(H_{3},F_{3})
, and
(F_{5},F_{5})
, appropriate for type IIB
(and its duality relation to M-theory). See
section

4.1
and
section

4.2
for S-duality and T-duality, respectively.

4.4
The root data of type IIB

Given that the real homotopy type
IIB
was worked out so as to match with type
IIA
via T-duality
(
55
) and (
56
),
we would like to choose a compatible trivialization
T^{B}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
of the maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split
torus of
\Aut M(IIB)
and a compatible basis of weights of the action of
T^{B}
on
M(IIB)
.

Now, let us use these maps to create distinguished bases of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{B}
of the maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split
torus
T^{B}
and the dual space vector space
\mathfrak{h}_{B}^{*}
of weights, as we did in the case of the sequence of torus reductions
of M-theory (see
section

3.1
and
section

3.2
).
In order to emphasize the symmetries, we will use the weights of the universal elements
h_{3}
and
\omega_{3}
as fundamental,
corresponding to spacetime fields
H_{3}
and
F_{3}
associated with the fundamental
string and its S-dual, the D1-brane. With that, we write

t\cdot h_{3}=t^{\gamma_{\,1}}h_{3},\qquad t\cdot\omega_{3}=t^{\gamma_{\,2}}\omega_{3},

where

\gamma_{\,1}:=\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1},\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{\,2}:=\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{2},

given that

h_{3}\mapstochar\rightarrow s_{1}g_{4},\quad\text{and}\quad\omega_{3}\mapstochar\rightarrow s_{2}g_{4}

in the correspondence between
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIB)
and
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)=M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{2}S^{4})
. Here
\epsilon_{0}
,
\epsilon_{1}
,
and
\epsilon_{2}

are the generating weights of
\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{*}
, the Lie algebra of the maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus
T^{A}_{c}
of

\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}IIA)=\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{2}S^{4})
.

We will use the inner product induced on
\mathfrak{h}_{B}^{*}
from
\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{*}
:

(\gamma_{\,1},\gamma_{\,1}):=(\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1})=0,\quad(\gamma_{\,2},\gamma_{\,2}):=(\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{2})=0,\quad(\gamma_{\,1},\gamma_{\,2}):=(\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{2})=1\;.

The dual basis of
\mathfrak{h}_{B}
will be given as
\{\mathfrak{l}_{1}:=h_{0}-h_{1},\mathfrak{l}_{2}:=h_{0}-h_{2}\}
.
Then one can check from Equations (
59
) that there exists a unique element
-K\in\mathfrak{h}_{B}
which acts as the degree operator (
34
)
on the Quillen model
Q(IIB)
, namely, the element

-K=2\mathfrak{l}_{1}+2\mathfrak{l}_{2}.

We can check that this gives the correct degree by forming the inner product

\displaystyle(-K,-K)

\displaystyle=(2\mathfrak{l}_{1}+2\mathfrak{l}_{2},2\mathfrak{l}_{1}+2\mathfrak{l}_{2})

\displaystyle=8\,,

upon using the inner product relations (
58
). Indeed, this matches what is expected
in comparison with the case of type IIA; see (
30
).

Similar to the root system of type IIA in (
60
), we can produce the
root system associated with the corresponding del Pezzo surface, except that now the
root system is nontrivial.

Exceptional root data from the rational model for type IIB.

The data associated to the Sullivan minimal model
M(IIB)

of type IIB replicates the data determined by the del Pezzo surface

\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}

and produces the root system

E_{B}=R_{B}:=\big\{x\in\mathfrak{h}_{B}^{\mathbb{Z}}\;|\;(x,2\mathfrak{l}_{1}+2\mathfrak{l}_{2})=0,\;(x,x)=-2\big\}=\big\{\mathfrak{l}_{1}-\mathfrak{l}_{2},\;\mathfrak{l}_{2}-\mathfrak{l}_{1}\big\}

(60)

which is nonempty and may be identified as a root system of type
A_{1}
.

\mathfrak{l}_{1}
\mathfrak{l}_{2}
\mathfrak{l}_{2}-\mathfrak{l}_{1}
\mathfrak{l}_{1}-\mathfrak{l}_{2}

This justifies the type IIB row of
Table

1
.

5
Algebraic geometric features within the Triality

We provide topological and physical interpretations of classical statements
in algebraic geometry that can be found, for instance, in
[
Be96
]
[
Do12
]
.
We highlight that most statements hold at the universal level (i.e., for the generators
g_{4}
,
g_{7}
,
etc., of the Sullivan minimal models of the cyclifications of
S^{4}
) and all hold in spacetime (i.e., for
the pulled back physical fields
G_{4}
,
G_{7}
).
The notation might use one or the other, with the understanding that the statements
hold for both (unless otherwise specified; in particular, higher products
directly
arise only in spacetime and not universally).

5.1
Exceptional curves and exceptional vectors

Exceptional curves and Weyl groups.

Recall
that the class
l=\mathcal{E}_{i}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})
of
the preimage of a blown-up point
satisfies the condition

(l,\mathcal{K}_{k})=(l,l)=-1\;,

(61)

and all such classes are called
exceptional curves
, cf. (
44
).
The number of exceptional curves on
\mathbb{B}_{k}
, for a given
k
, is presented in the following table:

k

8

7

6

5

240

(62)

On the other hand, the orders of the Weyl groups are given as

k

Note that the ratio of the orders of the Weyl groups of

E_{i}
and
E_{i-1}

is given by the number of exceptional curves corresponding to rank
i
.

Weyl groups and lattices.

As explained in
[
OP99
, §4.6]
, the vectors

\vec{\varphi}=(\ln\ell_{p}^{3},\ln R_{1},\cdots,\ln R_{k})
can be represented as linear functionals
on a vector space

V_{k+1}
with basis
\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{k}\}
and used to assign to any weight vector

\vec{\lambda}=y^{0}\epsilon_{0}+y^{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+y^{k}\epsilon_{k}
its ‘tension’

\mathcal{T}=e^{\langle\vec{\varphi},\vec{\lambda}\rangle}=\ell_{p}^{3y^{0}}R_{1}^{y^{1}}\cdots R_{k}^{y^{k}}.

As we see in
(
49
) and (
50
), a minimal set of Weyl generators incorporates the exchange of the M-theory torus directions

S_{i}:R_{i}\leftrightarrow R_{i+1}
, where
i=1...,k-1
,
as well as the T-duality
T_{123}
on directions
1,2,3
of the M-theory torus.
These linear operators acting on
V_{k+1}
are given explicitly in
[
OP99
, (4.20)]
.
When
k\leq 8
, the action of the Weyl group on
V_{k+1}
is reducible. Indeed, the
invariance of Newton’s constant
\left(\prod_{i}R_{i}\right)/\ell_{p}^{9}
implies that the roots are all
orthogonal to the vector

\vec{\delta}=\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{k}-3\epsilon_{0}

(63)

with length
\vec{\delta}\cdot\vec{\delta}=9-k
, so that the reflections actually restrict to the
hyperplane
V_{k}
, given by
y^{1}+\cdots+y^{k}+3y^{0}=0
, normal to
\vec{\delta}
.
Note that
\vec{\delta}
is
a vector invariant under the Weyl group,
see
[
OP99
, §4.6]
.

Roots and exceptional vectors

The
E_{k}
lattice.

Let
\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}
be our rank-
(k+1)
lattice with the standard Minkowski inner product and the special vector

{\bf k}_{k}=-3\epsilon_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\epsilon_{i}\;.

The
\mathbb{E}_{k}
lattice is the sublattice of
\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}
given by
the orthogonal complement

\mathbb{E}_{k}={\bf k}_{k}^{\perp}

and satisfying
{\bf k}_{k}^{2}=9-k
. Hence
\mathbb{E}_{k}
is a negative-definite lattice for
k\leq 8
.
For
k\geq 3
, the vectors in (
45
)
form a basis of
\mathbb{E}_{k}
.
The stabilizer subgroup
\operatorname{O}(\mathbb{Z}^{1,k})_{{\bf k}_{k}}
of
{\bf k}_{k}

acts simply transitively on the set of canonical root bases in

\mathbb{E}_{k}
and coincides with the Weyl group
W(\mathbb{E}_{k})
.

Lattice
{\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{k})

Lattice
\mathbb{E}_{k}

\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}

\mathbb{Z}^{1,k-1}

Correspondence for roots.

Roots correspond to 1-form field strengths or, equivalently,
0-form potentials. Recall from (
43
) that a vector
\alpha\in\mathbb{E}_{k}
is called
a root if
\alpha^{2}=-2
. For
k\leq 8
, the combinations
(slightly generalizing (
45
))

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j},\qquad\qquad\quad 1\leq i<j\leq k,

(64)

\displaystyle\alpha_{ijl}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{l},\quad 1\leq i<j<l\leq k,

(65)

correspond, respectively, to the spacetime fields

s_{j}\omega_{i},\;\cdots,\;s_{l}s_{j}s_{i}G_{4}\;

and the following (universal) generators of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
:

s_{j}w_{i},\;\cdots,\;s_{l}s_{j}s_{i}g_{4}\;.

The roots in
\mathbb{E}_{k}
are given by
\pm\alpha

and their negatives, in the following table, where we also give a correspondence with

Here, as before, for form-fields,
s_{i}
means contraction (or integration over the fiber) in the direction of
the
i
th loop/circle and
\widehat{s}_{i}
means omitting that. For
form-fields as well as elements of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
, we also assume
that
s_{j}s_{i}:=-s_{i}s_{j}
if
j<i
, and that each
s_{j}
acts as a graded derivation of the wedge product. This allows us to understand compositions of the
s_{j}
’s via
normal ordering
. For instance, formally speaking,
s_{8}C_{3}\wedge G_{7}
would literally have to be zero, because it is supposed to take place in the spacetime reduced to 3 dimensions. However, after normal ordering, we get

s_{8}\cdots s_{1}(s_{8}C_{3}\wedge G_{7}):=\sum\pm s_{8}s_{i_{7}}\cdots s_{i_{p+1}}C_{3}\wedge s_{8}s_{i_{p}}\dots s_{i_{1}}G_{7},

where the summation runs over all
(7-p,p)
shuffles
(i_{7},\dots,i_{p+1}\;|\;i_{p},\dots,i_{1})
of
(7,\dots,1)
, over all
0\leq p\leq 7
, with all but
p=5
and
6
contributing nontrivially,
would make a more meaningful sense.
The case of
\mathbb{E}_{8}
involves a field of degree 10, say
G_{10}
,
which can be interpreted as
an antiderivative of the composite
of
G_{4}

and
G_{7}
, appearing as a spacetime field,
but we have chosen to write a summand
C_{3}\wedge G_{7}
without loss of generality.
This also suggests a relation to
a hypothetical
M8-brane; and the repetition of the
s_{i}
operation applied to
G_{10}
should have a meaning different from the iterated contraction; we discuss this further in
section

6.1
.

Recall from (
61
) that a vector
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{1,k}

({\bf k}_{k},v)=-1

and
(v,v)\linebreak=\linebreak-1
. The
exceptional vectors correspond to the exceptional
curves on the del Pezzo
surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
.

the interpretation in terms of the fields. It is remarkable that

)).

s_{j}w_{i}
,

, etc., of the universal models
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
.

Explanation of the approach in the tables.

Note that the combinations of fields in the above tables are not unique,
and other combinations of similar nature are possible. For instance,
the forms

s_{i}\omega_{j}\wedge G_{4}
and
\omega_{i}\wedge s_{j}G_{4}
have the same weights and correspond to the
same ‘multiplet’ in that they both generally
appear in the same relevant step in the dimensional reduction process. We have chosen to pick one such
representative for ease of recording the
statements, but it should be understood that there is a clear general
pattern here.

5.2
27 Lines

We work with spacetime field strengths. However, everything in this section also works universally, that is to say, for the generators of the Sullivan minimal models of the cyclifications
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}
and
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{7}S^{4}
.
We will map to constructions
in
[

, Ch.9]
.

Systematics of dimensional reduction on
T^{6}

We provide an exact mapping to the dimensional reduction
of M-theory on
T^{6}
, with a
systemization of the latter and doing so within a rigorous mathematical perspective.

Gravitational vs. nongravitational: The gravity line.

Once we reach rank
k=6

corresponding to the Lie subalgebra

of

:

and it also acquires
a vertical node;

This corresponds to
when axions arise from the C-field.

Weyl group action on sixers.

The number of sixers is equal to the number of roots. All the sixers form one orbit
under the Weyl group.
The stabilizer of the sixer
\{\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{6}\}

(and hence of a root) is the symmetric group
\Sigma_{6}
of order
6!
.
There are 72 roots, and note that
|W(E_{6})|=72\cdot 6!
.
The stabilizer of the double-six
D
is the subgroup

\langle\Sigma_{6},\sigma_{\alpha_{0}}\rangle
of order
2\cdot 6!

(see
[
Do12
, Prop. 9.1.4]
).
This gives corresponding statements for the fields and
puts on firm ground (with the proper interpretation)
discussions in the physics literature beyond simple
combinatorics and numerology to
proper setting and interpretation:
The number of 1-form field strengths
F_{1}
’s in the dimensional reduction is
20+15+1=36
,
together with their negatives form the 72-component multiplet of

W(E_{6})
.

Interpretation of fields associated to roots of opposite sign.

Note that the lattice
\mathbb{E}_{6}
is even, so the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector space
V=\mathbb{E}_{6}/2\mathbb{E}_{6}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{6}

can be equipped with a quadratic form
[
Do12
]

q(x+2\mathbb{E}_{6})=\tfrac{1}{2}(x,x)\mod 2.

(66)

Each pair of opposite roots
\pm\alpha
defines a vector in
V
with
q(v)=1
.
The quadratic form
q
has Arf invariant 1, hence vanishes, on 28 vectors.
The remaining 36 vectors correspond to 36 pairs of opposite roots or,
equivalently, double-sixes.
The action of
W(E_{6})
on
V
gives an isomorphism of groups
W(E_{6})\cong\operatorname{O}^{-}(6;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, the orthogonal group of the odd quadratic form on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{6}
.
See
[
Do12
, p. 192]
.

27 Lines in
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}
and on a smooth cubic, and 27 fields

Our interpretation of the statements in this part holds for the universal model
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}

(i.e., topologically)
as well as in spacetime (i.e., physically). We will first remind how the 27 lines arise on a smooth cubic, a del Pezzo surface of type
\mathbb{B}_{6}
, along with presenting the list of 5d-spacetime 27 fields, extending the Mysterious Duality of
[
INV02
]
. Then we will detect the 27 lines on
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}
, confirming the prediction of Mysterious Triality.

Configurations of cubics.

Suppose that the points
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
(
k\leq 6
) are in general position in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
Then the linear system
\lvert-\mathcal{K}_{k}\rvert=\lvert 3\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{1}\ \dots-\mathcal{E}_{k}\rvert
of cubics through these points defines the anticanonical embedding
j:\mathbb{B}_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{9-k}
.
The image
j(\mathbb{B}_{k})
contains a finite number of lines, which are the images
under
j
of the following curves in the del Pezzo surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
:

(i)
the exceptional curves
\mathcal{E}_{i}
;

(ii)
the proper transforms of the lines
\overline{x_{i}\,x_{j}}
,
i\neq j
;

(iii)
the proper transform of the conics through 5 of the
x_{i}
.

Their number is given in the following table
(see
[

, Prop. IV.12]
):

Six points and the cubic.

Let
k=6
and consider the blowup
\mathbb{B}_{6}

at six points
x_{1},\cdots,x_{6}
.
Both
\mathbb{B}_{6}
and its image, the cubic surface

S=\im(\mathbb{B}_{6})\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{3}
,
are isomorphic.
There are 27 rational curves on
\mathbb{B}_{6}
of self-intersection
-1
; the image
of each is a line in
S
. They correspond to 2-form field strengths built out of the
M-theory fields.

(i)

The exceptional divisors
\mathcal{E}_{1},\cdots\mathcal{E}_{6}
, corresponding to the
classes of the circles/line bundles given by the (Chern)
2-forms
\omega_{1},\cdots,\omega_{6}
. There are 6 of these.

(ii)

A line passing through the points
x_{i}
,
x_{j}
has class
\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{i}-\mathcal{E}_{j}
.
The self-intersection is
(\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{i}-\mathcal{E}_{j})^{2}=1-2=-1
. There are

\binom{6}{2}=15
such lines through two of the blown-up points.

These correspond to the 2-form fields
s_{j}s_{i}G_{4}
.

(iii)

A conic passing through the five points
x_{1},\cdots,\widehat{x}_{i},\cdots,x_{6}
is a rational
curve of class
2\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{1}-\cdots-\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\cdots-\mathcal{E}_{6}
. The self-intersection is

(2\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{1}-\cdots-\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\cdots-\mathcal{E}_{6})^{2}=4-5=-1
.

These correspond to the 2-form fields
s_{6}\cdots\widehat{s}_{i}\cdots s_{1}G_{7}
.

There are
6=\binom{6}{5}
of those as the omitted index
i
runs from 1 to 6
(conics through five of the blown-up points).

Overall, we have the precise matching:

Physical and topological interpretation of further statements.

We consider the following (the non-italicized text physically/topologically interprets the italicized text from algebraic geometry):

(i)

There are exactly 10 lines in
X
meeting any given one, and exactly 5 lines in
X
meeting any
two disjoint given ones.
(see
[
Ga14
, Rem. 11.6(b), 11.9]
).
We interpret “meet” to mean “has at most one component/tensor index in common with”,
in the sense of form components.

(ii)

Every exceptional curve
intersects the 5 lines and the 5 conics that pass through this blown-up point.

Fix
i
; then
s_{i}
gives rise to five fields
s_{j}s_{i}G_{4}
or
s_{i}s_{j}G_{4}
, as
j
runs from 1 to 5. This
also gives rise to five fields
s_{1}\cdots\widehat{s}_{j}\cdots s_{i}\cdots s_{6}G_{7}
.

On the topological side, we also have similar the five generators
s_{j}s_{i}g_{4}
or
s_{i}s_{j}g_{4}
of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})
for a fixed
i
, as well as the five generators
s_{1}\cdots\widehat{s}_{j}\cdots s_{i}\cdots s_{6}g_{7}
. This pattern of universal fields repeating spacetime fields persists in (iii) and (iv) below.

(iii)

Every line through two of the blown-up points meets

(a)

the 2 exceptional curves of the blown-up points;

This means, say, taking 1 and 2 as indices for the blown-up points, the line through them corresponds to
the twice-reduced field
s_{2}s_{1}G_{4}
, while the exceptional curves
correspond to
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
.

(b)

the
\binom{4}{2}=6
lines through two of the four remaining points;

These correspond to the six fields:
s_{4}s_{3}G_{4}
,

s_{5}s_{3}G_{4}
,

s_{6}s_{3}G_{4}
,

s_{5}s_{4}G_{4}
,

s_{6}s_{4}G_{4}
,
and
s_{6}s_{5}G_{4}
.

(c)

the 2 conics through the four remaining points and one of the blown-up points.

These correspond to the two fields:

s_{6}s_{5}s_{4}s_{3}s_{1}G_{7}
and

s_{6}s_{5}s_{4}s_{3}s_{2}G_{7}
.

,

,
\mathcal{E}_{4}
,
\mathcal{E}_{5}
,
\mathcal{E}_{6}
at these points, as well as the 5 lines through one of these five points and the remaining point.

Take the first blowup point to be
x_{1}
. Then

s_{6}s_{5}s_{4}s_{3}s_{1}G_{7}
has one index in common with each of

\omega_{1}
,
\omega_{3}
,
\omega_{4}
,
\omega_{5}
,
\omega_{6}

and with each of

s_{2}s_{1}G_{4}
,

s_{3}s_{2}G_{4}
,

s_{4}s_{2}G_{4}
,

s_{5}s_{2}G_{4}
,

s_{6}s_{2}G_{4}
.
Similarly for the other cases.

27 Lines in the cyclic loop space
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}
.

Given that the data
\left(\mathfrak{h}_{6}^{*},\{\epsilon_{0},\dots,\epsilon_{6}\},(-,-),K_{6}^{*}\right)
arising in
Section

3.2.2
is exactly the same as that for the del Pezzo surface
\mathbb{B}_{6}
, we can identify the 27 “lines” in
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}
. These lines are generated by the
\mathbb{R}
-homotopy classes of 27 maps
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}
(to be more precise, linear combinations of such, i.e., 27 elements of
\pi_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})=\pi_{2}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})\otimes\mathbb{R}
) supplied by the following result
[
SV21
]
:

The 27 exceptional vectors
\alpha\in(\mathfrak{h}_{6}^{\mathbb{Z}})^{*}
,
(\alpha,\alpha)=(\alpha,K_{6})=-1
, give rise to 27 canonically defined lines in the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
\pi_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})
. Moreover, these lines freely generate
\pi_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})
and thus
\dim\pi_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})=27
.

Since 27 is also remarkable as the dimension of the standard representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
, this theorem is suggestive of an action (of a real form) of
it
on
\pi_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})
and possibly on all
\pi_{\bullet}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})
. Does the corresponding Lie group act by rational homotopy self-equivalences over
\mathbb{R}
on
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}
? It would be interesting to find out. Of course, the question is equivalent to asking if there is a Lie-algebra homomorphism from a form of
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
to
\operatorname{Lie}(\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}))
.

5.3
28 Bitangents

A similar count of “lines in
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}
” works for
0\leq k<6
, with the numbers of lines given by the “Number of lines on
\mathbb{B}_{k}
” in a table
earlier in the previous subsection. This count starts to break for
k>6
, because some of the exceptional weights will start having trivial weight spaces.
This is reflected in the irregularity of the form of spacetime fields in the last two tables of
Section

5.1
.

28 Bitangents on a quartic etc.

Seven points and fields in four dimensions.

Let
\pi:\mathbb{B}_{7}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the blowup of seven points
x_{1},\cdots,x_{7}
in general position.
There are 56 lines paired into 28 pairs corresponding to
28 bitangents of the ramification quartic curve
C
.
The 28 pairs of lines are the proper inverse transforms of the isolated pairs
of curves, split into:

(i)

21 pairs
: (a line through
x_{i},x_{j}
; the conic through the complementary five points);

(ii)

7 pairs
:
\left(\text{a cubic with a double point at $x_{i}$ passing
through the other points;
the exceptional curve $\pi^{-1}(x_{i})$}\right)
.

Our interpretation of the above is as follows:
The first 21 pairs of lines correspond to the 21 pairs of spacetime 2-form field strengths

s_{j}s_{i}G_{4}
and
s_{7}\cdots\widehat{s}_{j}\cdots\widehat{s}_{i}\linebreak\cdots s_{1}G_{7}
, respectively.
Similarly, we have 21 pairs of
universal fields, the elements
s_{j}s_{i}g_{4}
and
s_{7}\cdots\widehat{s}_{j}\cdots\widehat{s}_{i}\cdots s_{1}g_{7}
of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{7}S^{4})
.
Recalling that cubic means having the class
3H
with weight
3\epsilon_{0}
, and
a double point means having
s_{i}s_{i}
, for that direction
i
,
the second group of 7 pairs of lines corresponds to the pairs of 2-form field strengths

s_{7}\dots\widehat{s}_{i}\dots s_{1}(s_{i}C_{3}\wedge s_{i}G_{7})
and
\omega_{i}
.
However, only the second components of these pairs are
represented by universal fields,
w_{i}
’s.

Since the degree of the field strengths is half of the spacetime dimension

(2=4/2)
, there is a duality symmetry that interchanges the EOMs and the
Bianchi identities and which give rise to an enlarged symmetry group
under which the 2-forms and their duals are treated on an equal footing
(as classically in
[
LPS96
]
).

Formulation in terms of dilaton vectors and weights.

The dilaton vectors are constants that characterize the couplings of the dilatonic
scalars
\vec{\phi}
to the various gauge fields; see
[
LP96
]
[
LPS96
]
.
The following are obtained by the dimensional reduction of the kinetic term.
While we have mostly not adopted that perspective here, the end results are reassuring.

(i)

E_{6}
and reduction on
T^{6}
:
The 27 dilaton vectors are the weights of the 27-dimensional fundamental
representation of
E_{6}
. The Weyl group
W(E_{6})
is isomorphic to the group of 27 lines on a cubic surface,
i.e., to the subgroup of the permutation group
\Sigma_{27}
which preserves the
incidence relation between the lines (see
[
Do08
]
).
Our interpretation of the lines
is as follows:

(a)

6 exceptional divisors correspond to the dilaton vectors
\vec{b}_{i}

associated to the vierbein;

(b)

15 lines connecting two points corresponding to the dilaton vectors

\vec{a}_{ij}
;

(c)

6 conics through 5 of the 6 points correspond to
\vec{a}_{i}^{*}=-\vec{a}_{i}
, the dilaton
vectors of the dualized 3-forms.

(ii)

E_{7}
and reduction on
T^{7}
: Similarly, the 28 2-form field strengths
correspond to the dilaton vectors
\vec{a}_{ij}
(of which there are 21) and

\vec{b}_{i}
(of which there are 7).
Together with their negatives, these correspond to the smallest representation of
E_{7}
,
which, under the decomposition
{\rm SL}(8,\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow E_{7}
, is

{\bf 56}\simeq{\bf 28}\oplus{\bf 28}^{*}\simeq\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{8}\oplus\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{8})^{*}
.

There are 63 1-form field strengths corresponding to 35 dilaton vectors

\vec{a}_{ijk}
, 21 dilaton vectors
\vec{b}_{ij}
, and
7 dilaton vector
\vec{a}_{i}^{*}=-\vec{a}_{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,7
. Together with their
negatives, these give rise to 126 1-forms, corresponding to the nonzero weights of
the adjoint representation of
E_{7}
.

We view this as a testament to the power of dualization of physical fields in spacetime.
It would be interesting to see this aspect universally at the level of cyclifications, as for
E_{6}
and the 27 lines above, but
we leave this treatment for elsewhere.

5.4
Higher and exotic states

Here we first provide interpretation via fields and hence via universal models
(but we choose to write explicitly in terms of the former) of certain special, including
the exotic, states
appearing in Mysterious Duality. We will use
the interpretation in
section

2.5
of sums in the Picard group of
\mathbb{B}_{k}
as
corresponding to (wedge) products of fields to provide an additive
factorization of terms in the classes.

Fields corresponding to (exotic) states in
d=8
spacetime.

The elements

\alpha=a\mathcal{H}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}\mathcal{E}_{i}

(67)

of the cohomology lattice
H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{k};\mathbb{Z})

with relatively high values of
a
and/or the
b_{i}
do not easily lend themselves
to interpretation. Here we interpret these (exotic) states as corresponding
to decomposable fields, demonstrating the utility of factorization.
We illustrate this for several entries from the Table on p. 24 of
[
INV02
]
:

(1)

The state
3\mathcal{H}-2\mathcal{E}_{1}-\mathcal{E}_{2}-\mathcal{E}_{3}
(or
3H-2E_{M}-E_{i}-E_{j}
in the notation of
[
INV02
]
)
is represented by the wedge product of fields

s_{2}s_{1}G_{7}\wedge s_{3}s_{1}G_{4}
,
which, in a more familiar notation, would be
F_{5}\wedge F_{2}
.

(2)

The state
3\mathcal{H}-2\mathcal{E}_{1}-\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
i=2,3
, is represented by the wedge product of fields

s_{i}s_{1}G_{7}\wedge s_{1}G_{4}
,
which, in a more familiar notation, would be
F_{5}\wedge H_{3}
.

(3)

The state
4\mathcal{H}-2\mathcal{E}_{1}-2\mathcal{E}_{2}-2\mathcal{E}_{3}
is represented by the wedge product of fields

s_{3}s_{2}s_{1}G_{7}\wedge s_{3}s_{2}s_{1}G_{7}
,
which in more familiar notation would be
F_{4}\wedge F_{4}
in 8 dimensions.
This state was not identified in
[
INV02
]
in terms of branes.
In our perspective, using factorization, interpretation via fields, and then
field/brane correspondence, we see that
it corresponds to a configuration of two M5-branes wrapped on
T^{3}
.

(4)

The state
3\mathcal{H}-2\mathcal{E}_{1}
is represented by the wedge product of fields

s_{1}G_{7}\wedge s_{1}C_{3}
or
s_{1}C_{6}\wedge s_{1}G_{4}
,
which in more familiar spacetime notation would be
F_{6}\wedge B_{2}
or
C_{5}\wedge H_{3}
, respectively.
On-shell, these are locally equivalent to
F_{8}
and indeed corresponds to the D6-brane.

Higher classes.

We consider a higher generalization of the anticanonical class
-\mathcal{K}_{k}
from
(
39
) to more general coefficients
(specializing (
67
)):

a\mathcal{H}-b\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{i}\;.

Such a class is known to be ample for
a\gg b
(see
[
Kü96
]
[
Xu95
]
).
Nagata’s conjecture asserts that
essentially
a/b=\sqrt{k}

[
Na59
]
.
It would be interesting to find a physical interpretation of these statements. For instance,
recall that we have interpreted
a\mathcal{H}
to correspond to the
a
-fold
wedge product of
G_{4}
with itself (i.e.,
a
times) and
b\mathcal{E}_{i}
to correspond to
contraction with respect to the
i
th circle direction
b
times.
This places a limit on
a
and
b
coming from the dimension of spacetime.
For higher values of
a
and/or
b
, this will take us outside the conventional
dimensions of M-theory. This is somewhat analogous to the situation
in the
E_{11}
approach, where the interpretation of higher degree fields
or representations is generally desirable, yet challenging.

Reduction and Chern classes with multiplicities.

If
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
are the points in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
blown up to the exceptional divisors
\mathcal{E}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{E}_{k}
, then, for
a
large enough compared to the
b_{i}
’s, the class
(
67
) may be represented by the proper transform of a curve of degree
a
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
passing through each point
x_{i}
with multiplicity
b_{i}
.
Accordingly, we interpret classes in (
67
) with
an exceptional
class
\mathcal{E}_{i}
entering with multiplicity as
corresponding to taking multiple
Kaluza-Klein reductions along the same circles.
From the point of view of M-theory
this means that we have identification of fibers in the reduction.
This explains the corresponding exotic states in the table on page 24 in

[
INV02
]
, as we did in the discussion right after (
67
).

Note that in
section

5.3
above we have been able to allow for multiple occurrences of the contraction by
the circle by utilizing the compositeness of the fields corresponding to the canonical classes.

Note also that as before, for
k\leq 8
, the only exceptional curves
without multiplicities in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
’s
are
\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{i}-\mathcal{E}_{j}
, and
2\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{i_{1}}-\cdots-\mathcal{E}_{i_{5}}
.
Also, interestingly, these arise as the 27 lines in
section

5.2
for
k=6
, and this is the highest
k
for which
there are no multiplicities in
\mathcal{E}_{i}
’s needed to describe exceptional
curves (see, for instance, the table on p. 50 in
[
DiR96
]
).

6
Reduction to 2 and lower dimensions: extension
to the Kac-Moody setting

6.1
Blowing up
\geq 9
points, cyclifying
\geq 9
times, and Kac-Moody algebras

As in the case for
E_{8}
in the reduction to 3 dimensions
[
MS83
]
,
the Kac-Moody algebras of type

E_{9}
and
E_{10}
have been suggested as symmetries of
supergravity when dimensionally reduced to 2 and 1 dimensions, respectively

[
Ju81a
]
[
Ju82
]
[
Ju85
]
.
Indeed,
reduction to 1 dimension witnesses the emergence of
E_{10}
(see
[
N92
]
[
Miz98
]
),
and further reduction to 0 dimension points to possible emergence of
E_{11}
(see
[
Ch04
]
[
FK12
]
for some speculations).

The Kac-Moody algebra
E_{9}
is affine,

E_{10}
is hyperbolic (all of whose proper regular subalgebras are either finite or
affine), while
E_{11}
is Lorentzian.
The theory of real forms of complex Kac-Moody algebras
(see
[
KW92
]

[
BBMR95
]
[
BM06
]
)
generalizes the theory of
real semi-simple Lie algebras
(see
[
He01
]
[
Kn02
]
[
HPS08
]
).
Extending the case
E_{8(+8)}
, one obtains
the split real Kac-Moody algebras of type
E_{9(+9)}
,
E_{10(+10)}

and
E_{11(+11)}

(see
[
HPS08
]
); cf. the Introduction and
section

2.3
for the corresponding
forms in the Lie case.

Beyond del Pezzo surfaces.

Recall from (
39
) that the canonical divisor of the blowup
\pi:\mathbb{B}_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
at
k
ordinary points is

\mathcal{K}_{k}=-3\pi^{*}\mathcal{H}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
where
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a hyperplane and
\mathcal{E}_{i}
are the exceptional curves.
The degree of
-\mathcal{K}_{k}
, given by the self-intersection

(-\mathcal{K}_{k})^{2}=\mathcal{K}_{k}^{2}=9-k\,,

shows that
-\mathcal{K}_{k}
can be ample only if
0\leq k\leq 8
. Hence
for
k\geq 9
, we do not have a del Pezzo, but rather a more general rational surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
.
Let
P=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}\}
be a finite set of

k
points
in
the projective plane
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
whose blowing up results in a rational surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
. As for lower values of
k
, the geometry of the surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
depends on the relative position of the points
P
: for example,
\Aut\mathbb{B}_{k}
is trivial if
P
is generic
[
Gi80
]
[
Hi88
]
[
Ko88
]
. For what we do in this paper, the structure of
P
does not matter, but we would not be surprised if the intricacies of the algebraic geometry might be reflected in the subtler structure of physical fields or rational homotopy theory.

Why
k\leq 8
vs.
k\geq 9
in the exceptional root data.

The construction of the data
\big(\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*},\{\epsilon_{0},\dots,\epsilon_{k}\},(-,-),K_{k}^{*}\big)
analogous to
(
37
)
extends beyond
k=8
verbatim. However, the identification of the root system for
k>8
needs to be treated with care.
For
0\leq k\leq 8
, the degree
\deg(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})=(K_{k},K_{k})=(K_{k}^{*},K_{k}^{*})=9-k
of the cyclic loop space is positive,
just like the degree of the del Pezzo surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
. From simple linear algebra of Minkowski inner products, we can see that

(K_{k},K_{k})>0
implies that the inner product induced on the subspace

K_{k}^{\perp}=\{x\in\mathfrak{h}_{k}\;|\;(x,K_{k})=0\}
of

\mathfrak{h}_{k}
by the Minkowski inner product
(-,-)
is negative-definite. (If we switch the sign and use
-(-,-)
, it would be a more
familiar positive-definite inner product). This also implies that the root system
R_{k}
is finite; see
[
Ma74
]
. For
k\geq 9
,
the inner product loses its negative-definiteness, and the root
system
R_{k}
becomes infinite and can be identified as the set of
real roots
of a
more general Kac-Moody algebra; see the discussion of the
k=9
case further below. In fact, for
k=9
, the subspace
K_{k}^{\perp}

is negative semi-definite of nullity 1. For
k>9
, the orthogonal complement
K_{k}^{\perp}
gets a Minkowski inner product.

Beyond 8-fold cyclifications of
S^{4}
.

When we continue cyclifying the four-sphere
S^{4}
, that is to say, consider
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
, beyond
k=8
, its degree in the sense of (
36
) loses its positivity, the metric induced on
K_{k}^{\perp}
loses its positive-definiteness, and the corresponding root system becomes infinite.

Beyond Lie groups.

One can
define the
simple roots
to be, as in (
45
) with the markings
(
47
),

\alpha_{0}=\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\alpha_{i}=\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{i+1}\;.

(68)

Note that
(K_{k}^{*},\alpha_{i})=0
and
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})=-2
,

0\leq i\leq k-1
.
For
k\geq 9
, these elements
generate the root lattice
\mathbb{E}_{k}
of the Kac-Moody root
system, cf.
[
Lo80
]
[
Lo81
]
[
Ka80
]
[
Moo79
]
.

The Weyl group.

The intersection matrix
({\bf\alpha}_{i},{\bf\alpha}_{j})
is equal to

\Gamma_{k}-2I_{k}
, where
\Gamma_{k}
is the incidence matrix
of the Dynkin graph
E_{k}
.
In particular, each class
{\bf\alpha}_{i}
has self-intersection
-2
and determines an involutive
isometry of
\mathbb{Z}^{1,k-1}
by

\sigma_{i}:y\mapstochar\rightarrow y+(y\cdot{\bf\alpha}_{i}){\bf\alpha}_{i}
.
Together, the reflections
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k-1}

generate the Weyl group
W_{k}
with Dynkin diagram
E_{k}
,
and the inequality
k\geq 9
ensures that this Weyl group is infinite. In the algebraic geometric context, it is known that the same group
W_{k}
is infinite if and only if

\rank\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k})=k+1\geq 10

[
Har85
, Lemma 0.5]
.

Dynkin diagram
E_{k}

\sigma_{1}
\sigma_{2}
\sigma_{3}
\sigma_{4}
\sigma_{k-2}
\sigma_{k-1}
\sigma_{0}

(69)

Note that the Dynkin diagram can also be labeled by the roots rather
than by the Weyl reflections.
As in (
43
), an element of
\mathbb{E}_{k}
of inner square
-2
is called a
root
.
The lattice is spanned by the (simple) roots
\alpha_{0},\dots\alpha_{k-1}
.
A
real root
is a root which belongs to the
W_{k}
-orbit of one (or any)
of these simple roots.

Ten dimensions.

Let us
consider for
k\geq 0
the
\mathbb{R}
-split rank
of
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
, the dimension of the maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus, described in
Section

3.2.2
:

\rank_{\mathbb{R}}\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})=k+1\;.

This is also the rank of the Picard group of the complex (del Pezzo for
k\leq 8
) surface
\mathbb{B}_{k}
:

\rank\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k})=k+1\;.

The degree of the cyclification
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}
, see (
36
), is equal to the Minkowski inner square
of the “anticanonical class”

(-K_{k},-K_{k})=9-k\;.

Thus, the following relation holds:

\rank_{\mathbb{R}}\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})+\deg\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}=10\;.

This is replicated for surfaces
\mathbb{B}_{k}
:

\rank\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k})+\deg\mathbb{B}_{k}=10\;.

We find these as a curious possible reference to the critical dimension of (super)string theory.

(-1)
-curves in
\mathbb{B}_{k}
for
k\geq 9
.

The blowup
\mathbb{B}_{k}
of
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
at

k=9
points in (Cremona) general position contains infinitely many
(-1)
-curves

[
Na61
, Lemma 2.5 & Thm. 4a]
. This is generalized to include
k>9
in
[
To08
, Lemma 2.2]
. Here, as before,
(-1)
-curves
are understood as smooth rational curves of degree 1. They will automatically have self-intersection index
-1
. They are particular case of
(-1)
-divisors

D
, which by definition satisfy the relations
D\cdot\mathcal{K}_{k}=D\cdot D=-1
, i.e., they represent the elements of
I_{9}\subset\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{k})
. It is easy to see that the set
I_{9}
is infinite, but finding infinitely many
(-1)
-curves on the surface is a subtler issue, addressed in the papers quoted above.

(-1)
-lines in
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
for
k\geq 9
.

Similarly, for
k\geq 9
there are infinitely many
(-1)
-lines in
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
, defined as the exceptional weights
\alpha\in P(\mathfrak{h}_{k})=\mathbb{Z}\epsilon_{0}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbb{Z}\epsilon_{k}
such that
(\alpha,\alpha)=(\alpha,K_{k})=-1
, as for
k=6
as in

section

5.2
. Indeed, the Weyl group
W_{k}
acts on the weight space
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
of the maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus
T^{k+1}
of
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
by isometries preserving
K_{k}
and the weight lattice
P(\mathfrak{h}_{k})
. Therefore, the Weyl group acts on the exceptional lines. The examples show there are enough exceptional lines to generate the whole vector space
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
. This implies that an element of
W_{k}
is determined by its action on the exceptional lines.
Then the fact that the group
W_{k}
is infinite for
k\geq 9
implies that there are infinitely many exceptional lines.

Recall from
section

5.2
that in the
k=6
case, the 27 exceptional weight spaces of the action of
\mathfrak{h}_{k}
on the Quillen model
Q(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})=\pi_{\bullet}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})[1]
, i.e., on
\pi_{2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4})
were all one-dimensional and presented 27 lines in
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{6}S^{4}
.

On the contrary, for
k\geq 9
, the infinitely many exceptional weights have all but finitely many weight spaces equal to zero, both on
Q(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
and
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
. This is because the underlying real vector space
\pi_{\bullet}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
of the Quillen model and the degree-
2
subspace of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
of the Sullivan model are finite-dimensional for all
k\geq 0
, as follows from the identification of
M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4})
in
Section

3.1
.

We now consider in detail the higher ranks, one case at a time.

6.2
Blowing up 9 points and cyclifying 9 times

Compactification of M-theory on a nine-torus
T^{9}
to two spacetime dimensions
gives rise to a qualitative change in the U-duality group (see
[
OP99
, §4.12]
):
the invariant vector
\vec{\delta}
in (
63
)
corresponding to the dimensionless Newton constant becomes light-like
(null) in the Lorentzian metric, so that the induced metric on the orthogonal complement to
\vec{\delta}
becomes negative semi-definite and the U-duality group is no longer compact.
Accordingly, the generators
S_{i}
and
T_{123}
no longer span a finite group.
Instead, restricting to rectangular tori and vanishing C-field potential,
they
give rise to the Weyl group of the affine Lie algebra
E_{9}=\widehat{E}_{8}=E_{8}^{(1)}

[
EGKR98
]
.

The algebra
\mathfrak{e}_{9}
of type
E_{9}
.

For
k=9
, the Dynkin diagram (
69
) corresponds to
the affine Lie
group of type
E_{9}=\widehat{E}_{8}=E_{8}^{(1)}
.
This is consistent with the occurrence of infinitely many conserved currents
in two spacetime dimensions (see
[
Ni87
]
[
NW89
]
).
The group
E_{9}
is the infinite-dimensional affine group that appears as the global
symmetry of maximal two-dimensional supergravity
[
Ju81b
]
[
Ju82
]
[
Ju85
]
[
Ju86
]
[
Ni87
]
[
NW89
]
.
The affine Lie algebra
\mathfrak{e}_{9}
is
a centrally extended loop algebra

\widehat{\mathfrak{e}}_{8}
over
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
.

The Picard and root lattice.

The surface
12
12

12

As indicated earlier, for
k\geq 9
, the spaces
\mathbb{B}_{k}
are
no longer del Pezzo, even though at times we continue to use the same notation to highlight
the pattern, and we hope this does not cause confusion. The symbol is more meant to indicate the number of blowups rather than the nature of the surface.

\mathbb{B}_{9}
obtained by blowing up 9 points in general position

x_{1},\cdots,x_{9}
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
is an elliptic fibration.
The exceptional curves
\mathcal{E}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{E}_{9}

are sections of this fibration.
The Picard group
{\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{9})\cong H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{9};\mathbb{Z})

is a rank 10 lattice with generators
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{E}_{9}
, where

\mathcal{H}
, as before, is the class of the proper transform of the line
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}

in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
As in the
k\leq 8

case, the intersection relations (
32
) are satisfied,
so that

H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{9};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{1,9}
,
and from (
39
) the anticanonical class is

-\mathcal{K}_{9}=3\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{1}-\cdots-\mathcal{E}_{9}
, which is fixed by the
Weyl group
action on
{\rm Pic}(\mathbb{B}_{9})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{1,9}
.
The sublattice
\mathcal{K}_{9}^{\perp}
of the Picard lattice
is a root lattice for
E_{9}=\widehat{E}_{8}

when
k=9
(see
[
Lo80
]
[
Lo81
]
[
Ka80
]
[
Moo79
]
[
LXZ12
]
).

The
9
-fold cyclification
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{9}S^{4}
and root lattice.

The same
E_{9}
root system data arises as in the previous paragraph verbatim;
see details in
Section

3.2.2
. The “phase transition” from
k\leq 8
to
k=9
is summarized in the following table.

Case

\deg\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}

Inner product on
K_{k}^{\perp}

K_{k}
and
K_{k}^{\perp}

Weyl group
W_{k}

Exceptnl vectors for
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}

k\leq 8

>0

K_{k}\not\in K_{k}^{\perp}

k=9

negative semi-definite

K_{k}\in K_{k}^{\perp}

infinite

infinitely many

Here are some details on the dramatic difference which occurs for the Weyl group
W_{9}
as compared to
W_{8}
.
The Weyl group
W_{9}
is isomorphic to the affine Weyl group of type
\mathbb{E}_{8}
and fits in the extension

0\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{8}\xrightarrow{\;\iota\;}W_{9}\rightarrow W_{8}\rightarrow 1\;,

(70)

so that we have

W_{8}=W_{9}/\mathbb{E}_{8}
.
The lattice
\mathbb{E}_{8}
can be identified with

\mathbb{E}_{9}/\mathbb{Z}K_{9}^{*}=(K_{9}^{*})^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}K_{9}^{*}

and the map
\iota
with
a homomorphism

\mathbb{E}_{9}/\mathbb{Z}K_{9}^{*}\rightarrow W_{9}
,
This allows for an effective reduction from an infinite to a finite-dimensional setting.

The affine root system.

We use the description of an explicit Kac-Moody root lattice of affine

E_{9}=\widehat{E}_{8}
from
[
LXZ12
]
, such that all
(-2)
-classes form a real affine root system,
while the anticanonical class
-\mathcal{K}_{9}
(or
-K_{9}^{*}
, if we are talking about
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{9}S^{4}
, rather than
\mathbb{B}_{9}
) is the null root which generates the
imaginary roots.

(i)

In contrast to the
k\leq 8
case in
section

5.1
,
the set of exceptional divisors

I_{9}=\left\{l\in H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{9};\mathbb{Z})\;|\;l\cdot\mathcal{K}_{9}=l\cdot l=-1\right\}

is now infinite
[
De80
]
[
LXZ12
, Proposition 8]
, and so is the set of
(-1)
-curves
[
LXZ12
, Corollary 12]
.
Similarly, the set

I_{9}=\left\{\alpha\in P(\mathfrak{h}_{9})\;|\;(\alpha,K^{*}_{9})=(\alpha,\alpha)=-1\right\}

of exceptional weights for
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{9}S^{4}
is also infinite, just like for any
k\geq 9
; see the discussion just before this subsection.

(ii)

The root system
R_{9}
given by (
43
) is no longer the standard Lie-theoretic root system of type
E_{9}
, but only part of it, called the
real roots
:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\rm Re}=R_{9}

\displaystyle:=\big\{l\in H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{9};\mathbb{Z})\;|\;l\cdot\mathcal{K}_{9}=0,\;l^{2}=-2\big\}

in the alg. geometric context

\displaystyle=\big\{\alpha\in P(\mathfrak{h}_{9})\;|\;(\alpha,K_{9}^{*})=0,\;(\alpha,\alpha)=-2\big\}

\displaystyle\text{\small in the topological context}.

Explicitly, the system of simple roots of
R_{9}
is given by extending the description of

R_{8}
in (
45
) to (see, e.g.,
[
Ch15
, §3.2]
)

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}=\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{E}_{1}-\mathcal{E}_{2}-\mathcal{E}_{3},\;\;\alpha_{1}=\mathcal{E}_{1}-\mathcal{E}_{2},\;\;\alpha_{2}=\mathcal{E}_{2}-\mathcal{E}_{3},\;\;\cdots,\;\;\alpha_{8}=\mathcal{E}_{8}-\mathcal{E}_{9}

(71)

or

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}=\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3},\quad\;\alpha_{1}=\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2},\;\;\;\alpha_{2}=\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3},\;\;\cdots,\;\;\;\alpha_{8}=\epsilon_{8}-\epsilon_{9}\;,

a system that extends the finite-dimensional case (rank
\leq 8
)
in (
45
).

(iii)

The imaginary roots are given by nonzero integral multiples of the anticanonical class

\Delta^{\rm Im}=\big\{m(-\mathcal{K}_{9})\,|\,m\in\mathbb{Z}\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hskip 0.2pt\setminus}$}}}\{0\}\big\}=\big\{m(-K_{9}^{*})\,|\,m\in\mathbb{Z}\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hskip 0.2pt\setminus}$}}}\{0\}\big\}\;,

(72)

such that

\Delta=\Delta^{\rm Re}\cup\Delta^{\rm Im}=R_{9}\cup\big\{m(-K_{9}^{*})\,|\,m\in\mathbb{Z}\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle{\setminus}$\hss}}{\hskip 0.2pt\setminus}$}}}\{0\}\big\}
. See
[
HL02
]
[
Ka94
]
[
LXZ12
]
.

Infinite number of dualizations and imaginary roots.

We highlight the following observations:

(i)

Real roots:

To each positive real
root of the real split affine algebra
E_{9}
corresponds a BPS solution of 11d
supergravity
[
EHKNT07
]
.

(ii)

Imaginary roots:
The infinitely many fields parametrizing the symmetric space

E_{9}/K(E_{9})
, where
K(E_{9})
is the maximal compact subgroup,
are defined on-shell by virtue of an infinite set of duality relations typically formulated in terms
of a linear system and its expansion through a spectral parameter

[
BCIKS21
]
. Given our interpretation of the anti-canonical class in

section

2.5
,
we observe that this is reminiscent of the infinite multiples
of
-K_{9}^{*}
in the set of imaginary roots (
72
).

Hence from our perspective, what we have is an infinite number of dualizations
associated with the occurrence of
-mK_{9}^{*}
as spanning the imaginary roots.

6.3
Cyclifying 10 times and blowing up 10 points

We now consider the reduction of M-theory on a 10-torus
T^{10}
down
to 1 dimension. In
[
BGH04
]
, brief speculations were given about
the connection to
E_{10}

and possibly extending Mysterious Duality to this case of
k=10

via
\tfrac{1}{2}K3
surface
S
, whose cohomology
lattice
H^{2}(S;\mathbb{Z})
is the root lattice
\mathbb{E}_{10}
.

Here we provide a precise and systematic approach.
Given our perspective on Mysterious Duality via (universal) fields,
we see that this readily extends, with admissible fields with at most of form degree 1 arising from the reduction of

G_{4}
or
G_{7}
or their combinations corresponding to
-\mathcal{K}_{10}
or
-K_{10}^{*}

(see the table below). Furthermore, Triality also holds in this case, with
cyclic loops spaces
\mathcal{L}^{k}_{c}S^{4}
extending to
k=10
.

The roots.

We recall the following:

(i)

Real roots:

The real roots
[
Ka80
]
of the root system are the
W_{k}
-orbits of the simple roots (
68
). Real roots have an inner square of
-2
. If
k>4
,
then the simple roots are
W_{k}
-translates of each
other so that the real roots comprise a single orbit of
W_{k}
.
The familiar instantons such as Kaluza-Klein particles, M2-branes, M5-branes, and
Kaluza-Klein monopoles all correspond to real
roots of
E_{10}

[
BGH04
]
. In fact,
as we encountered earlier in the Lie algebra case,
the Weyl group of
E_{10}
formally acts as U-duality on instantons

[
OPR98
]
[
OP99
]
.

(ii)

Imaginary roots:

The physical interpretation of the fields corresponding to the
imaginary roots
, which
are the roots (the weights of the corresponding Kac-Moody algebra) of nonnegative inner square, is explored in
[
BGH04
]
and proposed
to be matched with some brane configurations.
Also in
[
DN05
]
, the higher derivative quantum corrections to the action
of M-theory are associated to
certain
positive imaginary roots of
E_{10}
.

We will provide interpretation from our perspective; see after the table
below.

Cyclifying 10 times and the lattice
\mathbb{E}_{10}
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{10}S^{4}
be the 10-fold cyclification of
S^{4}
. Then, by
Section

3.2.2
, we will have a maximal
\mathbb{R}
-split torus
T^{11}=\mathbb{G}_{m}^{11}
in
\Aut M(\mathcal{L}_{c}^{10}S^{4})
, and the dual space
\mathfrak{h}_{10}^{*}
of its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{10}
will acquire a basis
\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{10}\}
, an inner product
(-,-)
of signature
(1,10)
, and a “canonical class”
K_{10}^{*}
. The root lattice
\mathbb{E}_{10}
is generated by the simple roots of (
68
). This time, when the number
k
of cyclifications goes up to 10,

\mathbb{E}_{10}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{1,9}

(73)

becomes the unique even unimodular lattice of signature
(1,9)
.

Blowing up at 10 points

Let
\mathbb{B}_{10}
be the blowup of 10 points on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Different configurations of the blowup points will result in different geometry, one famous example being the Coble surface
[
DZ01
]
[
Do20
]
(see more on it below), but the Picard group and the topological data, including the intersection pairing, will be the same:

\Pic(\mathbb{B}_{10})\cong H^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{10};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{1,10},

and the root lattice
\mathbb{E}_{10}
will be the same as above, (
73
).
A Coble surface
S_{C}
,
a special type-
\mathbb{B}_{10}
surface obtained by blowing up
10

points
x_{1},\dots,x_{10}
in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
with the
property that the 10 blowup points are the ordinary double points on an irreducible curve of degree 6 in
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
Let
W_{10}(2)
denote the congruence subgroup of
W_{10}

that acts trivially on the coset
\mathbb{E}_{10}/2\mathbb{E}_{10}
.
Then every element of
W_{10}(2)

is realized on
S_{C}
; cf. the case for
\mathbb{E}_{6}
in (
66
).
Indeed, the image of
\Aut(S_{C})
in

W(E_{10})=W_{10}
is the subgroup

W_{10}(2):=\{g\in W_{10}\;|\;g(x)-x\in 2\mathbb{E}_{10}\;\text{ for all}\;x\in\mathbb{E}_{10}\}\;.

(74)

This subgroup is normal, and the quotient group is
isomorphic to the finite orthogonal group
\operatorname{O}^{+}(10,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, with the
+
superscript indicating quadratic form
of even type (see
[
CD89
, Thm. 2.9]
).
This makes connection to
the study of orbifolds of
T^{10}
in
[
BGH04
]
, and we offer it as
explaining and strengthening what was suggested there as a possible
description via assigning a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading on the root lattice
\mathbb{E}_{10}
.

We have the following table.

Case

\deg\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}

Inner product on
K_{k}^{\perp}

K_{k}
and
K_{k}^{\perp}

Weyl group
W_{k}

Exceptnl vectors for
\mathcal{L}_{c}^{k}S^{4}

k\geq 10

<0

Lorentzian

K_{k}\not\in K_{k}^{\perp}

infinite

infinitely many

Infinite number of dualizations and imaginary roots.

We highlight the following:

(i)

Dual graviton:
Level decompositions of

E_{10}
and
E_{11}

under the gravity subalgebras
\mathfrak{gl}(k,\mathbb{R})
(for
k=10,11
)
contains a 9-form potential
C_{8,1}

with a 10-form field strength
F_{10}
, which is traditionally associated with
the dual graviton;

(ii)

M8-brane:

U-duality considerations point to the existence
of an M8-brane in 11 dimensions
[
EGKR98
]
which also appears
in Mysterious Duality
[
INV02
]
.
Since this brane should be associated with a field of dimension 10, it is tempting to
ask whether it is related to the above dual graviton or to the field
G_{10}

we have seen to make an appearance in our context (see
section

5.1
).
Recent results
[
BKPPS17
]
indicate that
the statement about the dual graviton might not hold.
However, it could be the analogue of the MK6-brane, which
reduces to D6-brane in type IIA string theory, and in this sense it is in two degrees higher.
All these matters are also related to the parametrized homotopy theory
perspective on the higher rank fields, as indicated towards the end of the Introduction
(see
[
BMSS18
]
for details);

(iii)

Infinite duality relations:

Physical interpretation
of the higher level generators in the decompositions of

E_{10}
and
E_{11}

under their respective gravity subalgebras
\mathfrak{gl}(k,\mathbb{R})

has been pursued for a long time.
The early suggestions stated that such generators correspond to higher excited
string modes or higher spin fields, while more recent
evidence points to possible ‘deformations’ (such as gauged supergravities),
or that such generators should somehow be ‘dual’ to the low order generators/degrees of freedom – see
[
RW07
]
[
dWNS08
]
(and references therein).
Indeed, in the covariant approach of
[
We03
]
, these representations would correspond to ‘dual fields’
generalizing the relation between p-form fields and
(D-p-2)
-form fields.

Hence, as in the
k=9
case (see towards the end of
section

6.1
)
and with the perspective in
section

2.5
,
we have the interpretation of an infinite number of dualizations
associated with the occurrence of
-mK_{10}^{*}

among the imaginary roots.

Relating to
\mathbb{E}_{8}
via quadratic forms.

Let
\mathbb{L}_{2}=\mathbb{E}_{10}/2\mathbb{E}_{10}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{10}
.
Equip this
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-vector space with
the quadratic form

q(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,x\cdot x\mod 2\,.

There are exactly
496=2^{4}(2^{5}-1)

vectors in the set
q^{-1}(1)=\{x\in\mathbb{L}_{2}\;|\;q(x)=1\}
of “mod-2 roots”
[
CD12
, §6.4]
, and all of them
are represented by actual roots in
\mathbb{E}_{10}

[
CD85
, Remark 4.7]
, as in the table below.

Physical/field/universal interpretation of classes.

In this effectively finite perspective, we
provide a form-field interpretation of a collection of roots of
\mathbb{E}_{10}
which uniquely represent the 496 mod-2 roots:

Root

Associated Field

Count

\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}

s_{j}\omega_{i}

\binom{10}{2}=45

\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{k}

s_{k}s_{j}s_{i}G_{4}

\binom{10}{3}=120

2\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{m}-\epsilon_{n}-\epsilon_{r}

s_{r}s_{n}s_{m}s_{k}s_{j}s_{i}G_{7}

3\epsilon_{0}-2\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{m}-\epsilon_{n}-\epsilon_{r}-\epsilon_{s}-\epsilon_{t}

s_{t}s_{s}s_{r}s_{n}s_{m}s_{k}s_{j}(s_{i}C_{3}\wedge s_{i}G_{7})

1

Here all the indices
satisfy
i<j<k<m<n<r<s<t
, except that in the second-to-last line, the index
i
is the least of the three indices different from the seven indices
j<k<m<n<r<s<t
. Here we also assume
s_{l}s_{i}:=-s_{i}s_{l}
whenever
i>l
.
Note that, using the interpretation in
section

2.5
of sums in the Picard group as
corresponding to (wedge) products of fields, we have split
the last two terms as:

3\epsilon_{0}-2\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}-\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{m}-\epsilon_{n}-\epsilon_{r}-\epsilon_{s}-\epsilon_{t}=(\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{i})+(2\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{i})-(\epsilon_{j}+\epsilon_{k}+\epsilon_{m}+\epsilon_{n}+\epsilon_{r}+\epsilon_{s}+\epsilon_{t})

and

\;\;\;4\epsilon_{0}-3\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}-\cdots-\epsilon_{10}=(\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1})+(\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1})+(2\epsilon_{0}-\epsilon_{1})-(\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{4}+\epsilon_{5}+\epsilon_{6}+\epsilon_{7}+\epsilon_{8}+\epsilon_{9}+\epsilon_{10})\;.

The above can be viewed as a reduction from infinite
to finite number of fields.

6.4
Cyclifying
\geq 11
times and blowing up
\geq 11
points

Reduction on
T^{11}
and
E_{11}
-symmetry.

The appearance of an
E_{11}
symmetry in physics has been originally advocated
to be already in 11 dimensions rather than through dimensional reduction
(see
[
We03
]
[
We12
]
).
However, the reduction on
T^{11}
to dimension zero may also lead to possible emergence of an
E_{11}
symmetry (see
[
Ch04
]
[
FK12
]
).

Fields associated to
E_{11}
.

Decomposing the adjoint representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{e}_{11}

under
\mathfrak{gl}(11)
gives the representations in which the charges of the M-branes and other BPS states transform.
Levels 0, 1, 2, and 3, correspond, respectively, to the vielbein, the 3-form or M2-brane charge, the
6-form or M5-brane charge, and an
(8,1)
-tensor or the dual graviton charge,
the latter treated as part of the gravity sector

[
KSW04
]
[
We12
]
.
Considering a tensor hierarchy algebra
introduces new generators starting at level
three, the first of which is a 9-form, but it is still associated with dual
diffeomorphisms

[
BKPPS17
]
.

The composite fields
G_{10}
and
G_{11}
?

We have seen indications for the
existence of fields of higher degree, corresponding to our interpretation of
the anticanonical class
-\mathcal{K}_{k}
in
section

2.5
.
In the perspective corresponding to the Sullivan model, we would have a class,
call it
G_{11}
, that is the wedge product of
G_{4}
with
G_{7}
or the usual Chern-Simons triple wedge

CS_{11}
involving only
C_{3}
and
G_{4}
.
From this
point of view, we stop at
E_{11}

because of the maximal contractions

s_{11}\cdots s_{1}G_{11}
, giving scalar field strengths in zero dimensions.
From the perspective of the Quillen model,
there is a shift down in degree so that we end up with
G_{!0}

as a combination in M-theory, so that we stop at
E_{10}
because
of the maximal contractions

s_{10}\cdots s_{1}G_{10}
,
reflecting
(-1)
-forms as potentials.

Beyond
k=11
?

For
k\geq 11
,

\mathbb{E}_{k}

is the unique odd unimodular lattice of signature
(1,k-1)
.
Without any further conditions, this works for every
k\geq 11
.
This shows that mathematically one can continue with the patterns, through

k\geq 11
, so that the duality between algebraic geometry and algebraic
topology in our Triality in Figure
1

seems to persist. Now extending to the physics node takes us outside
the traditional M-theory range. However, higher degree fields do show up in the
infinite-dimensional setting in this
context, and one would need to view spacetime in an extended sense.
On the side of cyclifications of
S^{4}
and algebraic surfaces, there are some dramatic changes happening at the borderline
k=11
:

(i)

For
k\geq 11
, the Weyl group
W_{k}\subset\operatorname{O}(K_{k}^{\perp})
has infinite index in
\operatorname{O}(K_{k}^{\perp})
; see
[
CD12
]
in the algebraic geometric context.

(ii)

The rank of the Picard group for a rational surface whose automorphism group is large in a certain sense must be 10 or 11.
An arbitrary projective surface with a large automorphism group, which contains a sufficiently large nonabelian part, must have the Picard number at most 11
[
CD12
, Corollary 7.3]
.

It is remarkable that there seems to be a correlation on the limit of
dimension as well, in the sense that this seems to terminate around the dimension of M-theory.
This is perhaps analogous to the situation of
supersymmetry, where one
has a natural transition or upper limit of 11 dimensions,
beyond which one can extend but at the expense of some desirable properties (there:
super-Poincaré symmetry) being relaxed or lost.
